## Chapter 1: The Fire Lord's Birthday

### Notes

#### Work Notes

Latin title from Ovid, *Amores* 3.2: "&#91;you pour&#93; fire into fire". The Chinese title has the same idea.

Content warning: at the start of this fic, Zuko has somewhat disordered eating due to, essentially, IBS.

### Text

Zuko woke up with the sun on his twenty-fifth birthday. He had his breakfast of congee and fried eggs and got through about half. In front of the mirror, he spread the ointment his physician gave him over his burn scars as he did every day. He was perfunctorily dressed in his ceremonial robes by two servants.

He watched himself in the mirror as they strapped on the vambraces, the pauldron. As he grew older, he was disturbed by how much he was coming to resemble his father, especially if he covered the scar. Even his robes used to be Ozai’s, tucked in by the tailors where they didn’t quite fit his body. With the reparations and recession, the accounts office had cracked down hard on wasteful spending; even now the economy was picking up again, frugal habits died hard. Then again, Zuko had outlasted his father’s reign by nearly three years; surely, by now he could call these robes his.

They fit the hairpiece into his topknot. He swept out and climbed onto the waiting palanquin.

The Fire Lord’s birthday was an annual celebration Zuko never quite figured out how to worm out of. Given the discontent in the earlier years of his reign, it had been important to project his public image. His friends used to make the journey over too, but in recent years everyone had become busier and busier. This year, Aang and Katara had sent their gift ahead with their apologies. It all made his birthday feel somewhat impersonal. Then again, he had given so much of himself to his nation already; what more was his birthday?

As he did every year, Zuko went to the square in front of the palace and read out something his speechwriter had put together, something about his achievements in the past year. It was much too self-aggrandising, nothing he could have ever put together himself; then again, he wasn’t a very good speechwriter. There was a whole set of festivities: street food stalls, acrobatic displays, firecrackers. He shook hands with as many people as he could and conjured little sparks for children, shadowed the whole time by his bodyguard-valet, Osha.

And as there were every year, practically during any national event, there were the protests.

By now, Zuko could write the book on dodging assassins. What he had come to discover was that assassination was the extreme end of protest. Today, beyond the phalanx of guards, it seemed two groups had chosen the same day to express their dissatisfaction. On one side, with some bearing the illegal black flame emblem, they shouted for Yu Dao and Cranefish Town to be returned to the Fire Nation. On the other, they were calling for Zuko to share his secret tricks and bring an end to the alteration in firebending. There were certainly more people protesting now than there had been in the last few years.

He beat as quick a retreat as he could.

By some stroke of luck, he had managed to convince the event planners to let him have a private dinner with his mother. As soon as the palanquin stopped outside the palace gates, he leapt out and ran to his private dining hall.

His mother was there already. “Happy birthday, my boy,” she said, reaching up to take his face in her hands, patting him on the cheek. He breathed deeply, feeling warmed by her jasmine and cinnamon scent. He hadn’t seen her in months.

His next greeting was for his stepfather, who clapped him on the back. Kiyi, approaching ten, had decided that she wasn’t into hugs but still waved before turning back to her novel.

Osha caught up, skittering to a halt as she clutched the doorframe to catch her breath. During the war, she had been a military defector, fending off the Fire Nation soldiers sent to recapture her. After her pardon and return to the Fire Nation, it had seemed natural for her to succeed the Kyoshi Warriors as his bodyguard. “Sorry,” said Zuko, not really sorry at all. “I should’ve waited.”

“Never thought you’d outrun me like that,” she wheezed. “Come over, you don’t need the pauldron and vambraces for dinner.”

Mother and Ikem gave him red packets, as they did every year; as he did every year, Zuko protested that he was too old, that he was the ruler of the country and didn’t need their money. “You can give out your own red packets when you get married,” said Mother, and that shut him up.

Ikem, ever chatty, regaled them with his tales about his coworkers at the local Hira’a bureaucracy as they sat around the table. During the recession, he and Mother had found it difficult to keep the theatre running and Ikem had switched careers. Zuko could only nod along.

The kitchens had insisted that Zuko needed to eat lobster-crab on his birthday, and it came out resting on a plate of noodles, red as a rhododendron bush. “For long life,” Mother noted with approval. The server dished it out; meaty chunks of lobster-crab, long saucy strands of noodles in individual bowls. The smell made Zuko’s stomach squeeze.

He picked at the food, chewing up the noodles strand by strand. Mother was too observant to let it slip from her notice. “You’re eating so little.”

“It’s fine, Mama,” said Zuko. “I had a lot to eat at the festival.” *That* was a lie.

“I don’t think so. Look at your wrists, they’re so thin. Don’t you think he looks thin, Ikem?”

Ikem looked like he really didn’t want to supply an opinion on the Fire Lord’s wrists. “I… uh…”

“You haven’t been eating properly,” said Mother.

Zuko glared into his bowl, pushing the chunks of lobster-crab around with his chopsticks before spearing one and chewing it up to make a point. The exhaustion that he had felt at the festival was catching up to him. It was true, he hadn’t been eating properly. It wasn’t like he didn’t want to; he liked lobster-crab. But lately, he had developed stomach pains and every time he ate it hurt. Even with a cup of tea, he felt like he was going to burst open. “I’m just not that hungry.”

“Osha, have you noticed?” Behind him, Osha started, clearly not expecting to be addressed. “Can you make sure he goes to the physician tomorrow?”

The next dish to be served up was a medley of vegetables and mushrooms, and Mother made the servants give him a double helping of them. Then, spiced roast duck. For dessert, a red bean soup. Under her watchful eye, he ate every bite.

Before they left, Mother gripped him by the arm. “Look after yourself, son.”

Zuko looked at the floor.

Ikem clapped him on the shoulder again. “She’s just worried about you, kid,” he said in an undertone. “Don’t worry your mother, alright? Kiyi, say bye to your big brother.”

“Bye, Zuzu,” she said, and Zuko felt the pang of guilt that always came when he thought of Azula.

It wasn’t a charitable thing to feel about his own mother, especially since there was a period of about seven years in his life when he hadn’t even known whether she was dead or alive, but he was almost happy to see her go when she, Ikem, and Kiyi turned to their guest chambers. They would leave for Hira’a at the crack of dawn. Privately, he wondered whether his uncle might have been more forgiving about the situation.

Osha came behind him. “Are you feeling alright, Your Majesty?”

“Feel so disgusting.” Zuko felt his stomach, straining against his belt. “I’ll just be in my room, digesting.”

He gave her the evening off and made his way back to his chambers, flicking on the lamps with a swipe of the wrist. It was the hot-dry season and the nights were much cooler than the daytime. Without the pauldron, it was easier to undress himself; he shucked off robe after robe over a waiting rack. The red packets were left unopened on a tea stand, taels making a heavy *clunk* against the elm wood. The last thing to come off was his Fire Lord’s hairpiece; his hair fell around his face in straight sheets. He felt like he was going to vomit; he felt like the food would never come out. Just as he was about to climb into bed, a bird’s cry caught his attention.

It was coming from the window. There was a dark shape and an insistent tap against the wood. It was a messenger hawk.

Zuko approached slowly so as not to startle the bird. He read the name inscribed on the collar: *Hawky*. The bird ruffled its feathers with an aristocratic air, presenting the small package tied to its talon, then hopping to turn its back to him. Zuko took the package and slid the roll of paper out of the holder strapped onto its back, and the bird took off into the night.

*Happy birthday, man*, the letter opened.

*I’m back in the neighbourhood. Finished up my degree at Ba Sing Se and decided to train again.* *Am now being coached in the literary and martial arts by one Master Piandao. He’s a friend of* *your uncle, I think you would’ve met him back when those White Lotus guys were recapturing Ba* *Sing Se.*

*Hope your birthday was nice. Congrats on passing the Cultural Revival Grant, read about it on the* *Yitpao on the way here. Piandao’s super excited about it, he wants to get a zither club going. Must* *be nice to have that done by today so you could get a break. Toph and your uncle send their best.* *Actually, Toph said you better come over so she can have a bending rematch soon. Said she’d* *squish you flat with a mountain. Her words.*

*Hawky’s bringing you your presents. Tea from your uncle (he’s predictable like that). Toph got* *into pottery lately. You can deduce which one’s mine.*

*You should visit us in Shu Jing sometime. Piandao won’t mind.*

*Toodles,*  
*Sokka*

Zuko ran his fingers over the characters, quietly touched. He hadn’t seen Sokka in ages, let alone heard from him. Generally, the whole group tried to get together each year, right after the New Year. That hadn’t always worked out: after she and Sokka had broken up, Suki skipped one of the get-togethers; another time, Aang had been called on some ultra-urgent Avatar business; Zuko himself had to give one a miss when an assassination attempt put the whole palace on lockdown. Sokka had begged off the last meeting, saying he had to finish his thesis. The last time everyone had been gathered together was probably during Katara and Aang’s wedding.

He opened the packages. Indeed, there was tea from his uncle, a rare first flush harvested from one of the islands off the east coast of the Earth Kingdom. Toph’s present was an exquisite clay cup, glazed a turtleduck egg blue that crept into brown towards the lip. A band of gold stretched around it, likely metalbent into place. Ignoring the full feeling in his belly, he shook a few leaves into the cup, filled it with water from a pitcher, and heated it. His uncle would be scandalised. He always made tea the ordinary way, boiling the water slowly over a fire, but Zuko couldn’t taste the difference and he wasn’t going to send for a teapot at this hour.

Sokka’s present was in a box the size of his palm. He tipped it out onto his bed and a little clay dragon fell out along with a strip of paper. *Fill me with oil and light me :)*, it read. Zuko snuffed out the nearest lamp, tipped oil into the little dragon’s mouth, and touched a spark to it. A tiny flame leapt from its tongue.

Zuko settled back against the headboard, watching the flame as he sipped at the tea. It truly was delectable, with a deep, earthy tone in contrast to the lighter teas Uncle preferred. He missed them all, his Uncle, Toph, Sokka, and everyone else. Far away, the fireworks were starting up, crackling away in the night. Maybe he was imagining it, but his tummy didn’t hurt so much right now.

---

“You’re too heaty,” said the physician.

Zuko suppressed the urge to roll his eyes. Where had he heard that before? He was a firebender. Of course he was heaty.

The physician prattled on. “The heat is causing inflammation and giving you ulcers, which is affecting your digestion. You need to change your diet. What did you eat last night?”

“Lobster-crab. The kitchens insisted. I didn’t want to eat that much but my mother made me.”

The physician tutted. “I’ll be talking to them about it. Cooling foods for you only.”

Zuko tried not to groan. Cooling foods meant no to most spices, and he was Fire Nation bred through and through when it came to the tastebuds.

“What are the heaty and cooling foods? I can never keep track,” Osha piped up. “We’re setting off soon to Yu Dao, so I’m in charge of Fire Lord food for the next few days.”

Zuko wandered out into the corridor as the physician set about compiling his list. Osha came out after what seemed like too long of a while, flapping the open scroll of paper between her hands to dry the ink. “That’s a lot,” she said, grimacing. “He told me to remind you to keep using the ointment for your scars, too.”

“I *do*.”

They went to the airship port at the harbour. The water was thick with ships and the sun glinted a blinding white off the waves that peeked through. Zuko was bringing only a small retinue with him —Osha, Councillor Hara and *her* valet, a backup guard, and a secretary—and budget constraints meant that they had to take the public airship. In fact, much of the public transport system had been overhauled in the last few years, repurposing old warcraft. They boarded first, Fire Lord privileges, and were ushered into the small rooms on the top level. As the airship levitated, the secretary started to read from a brief detailing the latest situation in the former colonies. Zuko only half listened. He eyed the capital as it grew smaller and smaller from the window, red rooftops becoming a distant glitter in the caldera. He imagined Sozin a hundred years ago, making the same journey by sea, to survey his new colonies. He bit his knuckle.

---

“Damn that absolute *bastard*,” said Zuko on their return trip, and let out a huff of flame.

His secretary yelped and snatched the file out of his hand. Lately, everything he did with the Earth Kingdom seemed to end in disaster. Kuei hadn’t even bothered to show up this time, funnelling all his demands through the provincial governor, a stonefaced man who resisted all attempts at compromise.

Zuko flung himself onto the couch, rubbing at his temples. In addition to the growing discomfort in his belly, he had sported a dull headache since they had disembarked on Earth Kingdom territory. The Yu Dao business had been a constant thorn in his side since the armistice. In the third year of his reign, he had managed to negotiate for Yu Dao and its sister city Cranefish Town to enter an agreement of political autonomy and Earth Kingdom. For a long time, he had considered it a political triumph. Five years on, Cranefish Town localists were agitating for independence at the instigation of the charismatic Yee Kwan. The Earth Kingdom seemed more than happy to lose Cranefish Town, but not so much Yu Dao. Reports claimed that they were stacking the local legislature with loyalists, which would violate the agreement.

The airship announcer clicked on. “Currently disembarking for the Jang Hui River. Next stop, Shu Jing.”

“Shu Jing,” repeated Zuko. *You should visit us in Shu Jing sometime*, Sokka had written.

Osha blinked. “What is it, Your Majesty?”

“Change of plans,” said Zuko. “Tell the steward we’ll be disembarking at the next stop.”

Shu Jing was sunny and warm when he and Osha disembarked at the small airship port. The retinue was carrying on back to Caldera to pass the developments on; in any case, Zuko usually had the day blocked out to recover from the time difference in his travels. He strapped on a hat to cover his scar as they made their way through the town.

“Accounts is gonna be mad about this, boss,” said Osha. She had erected a small shadecloth with thin bamboo poles attached to her satchel, which Zuko didn’t have the luxury of using when he was trying to avoid notice.

“Trip’s on me,” he said.

“You know the way?”

“Yeah, it’s just up that hill.” He pointed towards the estate, sitting on the rocky outcrop that overlooked one of the best views on the island. “I learnt to wield the dual dao there.”

The journey wasn’t long and the sun beat heavily through their travelling clothes, burning off the remains of Zuko’s headache. They came to the gates emblazoned with the symbol of the White Lotus. The sight of it lit a flare of nostalgia in him. Zuko rapped the brass knocker.

“Who is it?” said a disgruntled male voice. The gate cracked open.

“Hello, Fat.”

The gate swung open wider, revealing the startled face of Piandao’s manservant. “Your Majesty,” he gulped, and threw himself to the ground.

“Come on, Fat,” said Zuko, twisting his fingers into the straps of his satchel. “You’ve known me since I was a kid, you don’t need to do that.”

Osha helped Fat off the ground as he grumbled and led them into the reception hall, then shuffled off to find the other inhabitants. Osha craned her neck, gawking at the woodwork, the ink paintings and porcelain displays, and peering at the view to the garden. “This is nice, real nice,” she stage-whispered to Zuko.

“Thank you,” came the smooth voice of an older man. Piandao himself strode into the room, hands clutched behind his back. Bands of grey adorned his hair. He knelt on the cushion before Zuko and kowtowed.

“Don’t do that, Sifu,” Zuko hurried to say. “I should be the one bowing to you.”

“It’s rare for my former students to become the Fire Lord. In fact, it has only happened once. Let an old man indulge. And milady, you are…?”

“This is Osha, my valet and bodyguard.” She made the sign of the flame and bowed low. “I hope we’re not encroaching. We brought you some egg tarts from Yu Dao as a gift.”

Osha dug the box of tarts, a local specialty of the city, out from her bag and handed it over.

“To what do I—” began Piandao, and was interrupted by the sound of loud, hurried footsteps. “Ah, there he is.”

“Your Flameyness!” said Sokka, bursting through the doorway. He jogged over to Zuko, lifted him by the armpits half out of the cushion, and squeezed him in a tight hug. Zuko barely had time to move his arms before he was dumped back on the floor and Sokka was heading for the cushion beside Piandao. “Sorry, I was practising my calligraphy. Happy birthday again. Hey, Osha. Let me introduce—”

“The Fire Lord was one of my students, when he was very young,” said Piandao. Fat came bustling back in with a tea set, which he set up between them. “Look at you now, with the eighth anniversary of your coronation approaching.”

“No way,” said Sokka. “We were both trained by Master Piandao? How come you never told me?”

“I wasn’t very good at firebending as a kid. My uncle pulled some favours.”

Piandao looked at him sharply. “*And* I saw great promise in you.”

Sokka took over the tea ceremony, insisting that Zuko’s uncle had taught him well in the last couple of years. Since moving to Ba Sing Se for his studies, he had grown out his wolf’s tail and braided it in the local fashion. He wasn’t wearing blue, instead matching Piandao’s long bruise-red changshan and loose black trousers. The only things that marked him out as a Water Tribesman were his shell necklace, peeking out from under the mandarin collar, and a single blue-beaded earring dangling from his right lobe. He looked cosmopolitan.

“I invited him,” Sokka told Piandao. “Sent him a letter saying he ought to come visit, since I’m living so close now.”

“It’s a three-hour flight by airship from the Capital,” said Zuko, rolling his eyes. He was sipping slowly at his tea, not wanting to bloat himself again. Sokka had prepared it well, almost a perfect copy of his uncle’s technique.

“It’s the same country.”

“I’m always happy to host the Fire Lord,” said Piandao. “Having young people around livens up a place so.”

“Did you pass through here from Yu Dao? Saw something about it in the Yitpao this morning.”

The throb behind Zuko’s left eye threatened to return. “It was. Uh. The usual.”

“Sokka, the Fire Lord may not want to talk business while retiring here. Come, Osha. Fat can show you to your rooms and we can let the two friends reconnect.”

“Of course.” Osha got to her feet. “Ah, Fat, I wanted to talk to you about the Fire Lord’s meals…”

“Come on,” said Sokka when they were alone. “I’ll show you the garden.”

The view was truly as amazing as Zuko remembered: the unobscured, cloudless sky arcing overhead, sunlight picking white sparks on the rushing falls below. The air here was clean, much cleaner than Caldera and Yu Dao, or even the town centre of Shu Jing. Zuko stepped out from the shade and breathed in, feeling his inner flame pulse.

“Good, eh? Piandao’s redecorated.” Sokka led them to a bench under a willow that overlooked a small pond of teeming peacock koi, which had to be a recent addition.

“Pretty,” Zuko remarked. “Thanks for the dragon, by the way. I love it.”

“It’s cool, right? I swear, you can get anything in those Ba Sing Se markets. Your uncle knows all the best places.”

“Yeah.” Zuko groped for a topic. “Sooo… uh, you finished your thesis?”

He knew vaguely that it was something to do with engineering techniques that had been developed by the Southern Water Tribe. But Sokka grimaced.

“Never ask a man about his thesis,” he groaned, slapping a hand over his forehead. “I’d pay money not to have to think about it again.”

Zuko blinked; he hadn’t realised the question would be so offensive. Sokka opened an eye. “Your face!” he said. “It’s a grad student thing. Every student has a love-hate relationship with their thesis. My marks were OK, actually, but I’m sick of thinking about it.”

If it were anything like the Yu Dao situation, Zuko could sympathise. “Why come to Shu Jing then?”

“Have you *seen* this place?” Sokka swept an arm across the vista. “I only spent a few days here with Piandao during the war, and I ought to finish my instruction. He said I could really do with some, uh, *finessing* of my skills. I do some odd jobs around town too, earn my keep. It was about time.”

The answer didn’t satisfy Zuko’s curiosity, which wanted to know: *Why not use that degree you* *spend so long on?* At Aang and Katara’s wedding, Sokka had spent most of the night chattering about wanting to revive Southern Water Tribe technologies that had been lost to the war, to put their cultural contribution on the map.

“I can show you the calligraphy I’ve been working on,” Sokka continued. He stood up and led them back inside. “You know, the last time I was here, I just slathered ink on my face and smooshed it onto the paper. Fat was *so* mad.”

As it turned out, Sokka had been drilling characters when Zuko arrived. The wonky letters sat half-complete on the sheet spread on the desk.

“Oh,” said Zuko, who had walls full of the best antique calligraphy produced in the Fire Nation. He didn’t recall Sokka’s letter looking this childlike.

Sokka sighed, tilting his head critically. “It’s not great, you can say that. I’m super rusty. I’m left-handed too, and Piandao insists that calligraphy has to be done right-handed.”

“It’s not that bad,” said Zuko, and with this context it certainly *wasn’t*.

“I’ll send you some when I get halfway decent,” Sokka said. “How’s *your* calligraphy?”

Zuko shrugged. He’d learnt some basics from Piandao but truth be told, he had terrible fine motor skills that only worsened after his eye was burned. As a ruler now, he had a whole cadre of scribes who could write in perfect clerical script, and no one asked to see his personal reams of notes scrawled in the middle of the night.

Sokka egged him into demonstrating. He knelt at the table and tried to assume the pose as correctly as he could recall, cupping the sleeve under his right hand and keeping his back straight. The brush dropped a large blotch of ink onto the paper as he hovered, and Sokka snorted.

“Shove off,” said Zuko, and wrote his own name.

“It’s neat?”

Zuko sighed. “Yeah, I can’t do any of those fancy flourishes.” He gestured to the walls, where Piandao had hung up his own artfully illegible reproductions of ancient poetry. “Now show me yours.”

Sokka plucked the brush out of his hand, skimming over Zuko’s skin as he did. He wrote his own name in slow, jittering strokes. Zuko watched his face while he did it, frowning focus zeroed in on the moving tail of the brush. Then he hung the brush back onto the stand. “Alright, I’m not embarrassing myself anymore today. Let’s see if Fat has food ready.”

---

The rest of the day with Sokka and Piandao took his mind off the disastrous Yu Dao trip. The next morning, Zuko went to say goodbye to Sokka but he was nowhere to be found. Thinking he might still be asleep, he knocked on Sokka’s door and found him sitting inside at the vanity table.

“This looks kinda dumb, right?” Sokka was holding his long braid up, inspecting his reflection.

Zuko didn’t really think so, but he said, “You’d probably look worse if you shaved the front as well.”

“You’re right. My head has enough bits shaved, anyway, otherwise I might’ve done it.” Sokka rolled his eyes. “To *fit in* and everything, ha ha.”

Zuko was surprised at the bitterness that had crept into his voice, but it was gone as quickly as it had come.

“Anyway, I think I want my old hair back.” Sokka opened a drawer and pulled out his old Water Tribe hunting knife, pressing the blade below where he was holding a fistful of the braid.

“Wait,” said Zuko. “Let me do it for you.”

“Oh it’s no bother, I used to cut my own hair all the time.”

“No… here.” Zuko pried his fingers off the knife and moulded his hand around the handle. He gripped the braid where Sokka was holding it and pulled taut. “Here?”

“Yeah,” said Sokka, and there was a flash, and then the braid was loose in Zuko’s hand. The rest of the hair slipped out and slid over his cheekbones in waves. Sokka whooped.

“Thanks, man,” he said, taking the braid and clapping him on the shoulder. “Hey, safe travels. You should come again soon.”

Zuko thought about all the Council meetings that awaited him, the piles of work that increased whenever he turned his back. “I’ll try.”

“Try? *Try*? Come on, you can take breaks. You’re the Fire Lord, you can do whatever you want.”

Increasingly, it felt like this wasn’t the case. “Thanks for inviting me, I enjoyed coming back.” Then, he blurted, “I missed hanging out. Having friends around.”

Sokka watched him carefully. “Really? Well that sounds to me a good enough reason for you to come back soon.”

## Chapter 2: Lucky Eight

Zuko’s impromptu leave delayed Council by half a day and he spent a whole week paying for it. He had rushed straight back to the Council chamber the second he got off the airship, but even with Councillor Hara’s earlier arrival and debrief the councillors were still displeased that he’d taken personal leave without notice, especially with the Yu Dao situation far from resolution. Zuko had to stress, several times and in his sternest Fire Lord voice, that he was visiting *Sokka, that Sokka,* *the war hero and brother-in-law of THE AVATAR*, before they relented—though the workload did not.

Part of the issue was the encroaching Coronation and Armistice Day; the Council had hoped to use a successful negotiation in Yu Dao to appease the ongoing popular complaints. Now, they were scrambling to find some recent measures to promote. They spent several sessions deliberating whether to bring forward the announcement of the Cultural Revival Grants.

Zuko planted himself face first onto his bed after a particularly finicky meeting. “Am I doing a good job?” he mumbled into his covers. “Should I even have gone to Shu Jing?”

Osha, who had come in after him, tutted and eased him upright to pull the pauldron off. “You’ve achieved so much in these years. Councils are critical by nature but I know they all support you. You know they support you. That’s why you picked them in the first place.”

It was true, he’d spent years curating his Council. It was not a task he wanted to repeat anytime soon.

“Times are rough right now, but you’ve done big stuff. You got us out of the recession. For what it’s worth,” Osha went on, “I think it was great you went to Shu Jing. You enjoyed yourself. You ought to take more regular breaks. Have you eaten?”

“A bit.”

Osha tutted. “What, aren’t the cooling foods working?”

Zuko shrugged.

“I’ll have to book the physician for tomorrow, then. Come on, get yourself in the bath.”

She retreated and he slopped himself into the tub, heating the water as he went, sliding in up to his ears and resting his head on the ledge. The warmth suffused into his wound-up muscles.

A clatter came from beyond the screen, then a soft *thwump* onto the floor. Zuko stiffened, cracking open an eye. *Tap tap*.

In a flash, he was out of the water, pulling on his bathrobe, and flinging the screen aside with flame gathering in his fist. There was a loud screech, a flurry of feathers. *Hawky*.

“Sorry!” he hissed to the startled bird. “Hawky, Hawky, it’s just me.”

The messenger hawk had gone back to the windowsill and ruffled its feathers, flashing a beady eyed *look* at him. It presented its back. Now that the adrenaline was fading, Zuko felt wet and silly in his robe. He plucked the scroll out and watched the bird push off and soar away, then quickly got back into the warm water.

*Hey buddy,*

*Good seeing you last week. Piandao and I have been working on footwork lately; he says it’ll* *strengthen my bad leg. I’ve been exploring the town a little too, and it’s pretty nice, very quaint* *and stuff. Should show you around.*

*Coronation anniversary is coming up right? Congrats in advance. Lucky eight. I can’t believe the* *war was that long ago, look what we’ve all gotten up to since. I asked Piandao if we could go to* *the Capital but he said he’s on the town committee for the local celebrations, so I guess we’ll be* *staying in Shu Jing. You should come over when you’re free so we can celebrate together.*

*Cheerio,*  
*Sokka*

There was a second scrap of paper. It was a simple character, probably one he had been drilling over and over again to make it look this good: 火, fire.

---

The physician tutted over his meridians. “Cooling herbal tea,” he said. “Kombucha, and miso as well to rebalance your gut flora. And acupuncture.”

He stuck a couple of needles into Zuko’s arm then pottered into the adjoining pharmacy, scooping out dried herbs from the jars. Then, he boiled a pot and made Zuko drink it in front of him. He folded up the herbs into a paper packet and passed them to Osha.

It made Zuko run late for the Council meeting. He hurried to the chamber, slowing down whenever he passed someone in the hallways to maintain his dignity. Taking a breath, he swept through the double doors; the councillors bowed.

“This councillor has news to report from Yu Dao,” said Councillor Hara without preamble as Zuko took his place at the head of the table. “A major protest broke out yesterday concerning the Earth Kingdom’s growing control over the legislature.” Her secretary handed her a brief. “It was peaceful, though some of the protesters demanded that the Fire Nation intervene with greater force on the behalf of Yu Dao.”

“If this councillor may, Your Majesty,” volunteered Councillor Po. “A significant intervention from the Fire Nation at this point would make the whole world wary. We have renounced our imperialist past and Yu Dao is no longer under our jurisdiction.”

“Thank you, councillors.” Zuko nodded to each. “I am sure we are all in agreement with Councillor Po. Councillor Hara and I have only last week voiced our concerns to the Earth Kingdom, and there is little else we can do without seeming provocative.”

“The governor made it *quite* clear to us that he had not forgotten the history of past settlements at Yu Dao,” said Councillor Hara.

“Councillor Hara,” said Councillor Ike, “what is the situation with Cranefish Town? Why has the Earth Kingdom diverted its attention from that former colony, even with the agitations of Yee Kwan?”

“Cranefish Town is composed of more citizens who were originally from the Fire Nation. After the agreement, some former Fire Nation colonists from Yu Dao relocated there too. Yu Dao, on the other hand, draws its population largely from the Earth Kingdom locals of the area. Of course, in both cities, there has been significant intermixing. I can only assume that the Earth King feels more of a ethnic *bond* with those in Yu Dao, and believes that this tie legitimises Earth Kingdom ownership over Yu Dao more so than Cranefish Town.”

It was a concise and salient summary, as to be expected of her.

“Your Majesty.” It was Councillor Kang. “This one has noticed growing discontent *within* the Fire Nation about what seems to be happening in Yu Dao. There are the usual malefactors who believe the colonies should never have been given up.”

“I saw myself bearers of the black flame during my birthday celebrations,” said Zuko.

“*However*, Your Majesty, another subset believes the Fire Nation has some responsibility over a safe transferral of power.”

Zuko shifted. “Those of us gathered would sympathise with the latter view. That is why Councillor Hara and I engaged the Earth Kingdom in talks last week. However, it is not up to us to determine what the people of Yu Dao need, since the signing of the agreement.”

Councillor Kang inclined his head. “Of course, Your Majesty. This one is in accordance. With the economy under my portfolio too, it cannot escape my notice that it would not do the Fire Nation well to provoke the Earth Kingdom while we are still paying penance through our reparations.”

A quiet descended over the Council, which Councillor Hara broke. “This councillor will attempt to organise a second meeting with the provincial governor in the next month. His Majesty need not attend, lest the man waste your precious time again.”

“My appreciation, councillor. Let the Council move onto other business. Councillor Po, have you heard from the bureau whether they can fasttrack the announcement of the Grants?”

Councillor Hara lingered after Zuko closed the Council. “Yes, councillor?”

Councillor Hara cleared her throat. She was a disciplined woman in her forties, a former colonial who had entered local politics as a way to dodge military service. Before Zuko had transferred her colony back to Kuei, she had successfully negotiated treaties with the surrounding Earth Kingdom towns, arguing for diplomacy over domination. Zuko remembered how he had practically gotten on his knees to thank the spirits when she agreed to join his budding Council. He had been nineteen. “Your Majesty, I know there’s been a lot of pressure on you recently. There have been… events beyond our control. I wanted to see whether you were alright. I *did* travel with you all the way to Yu Dao.”

“I’m OK, thanks, Hara. The physician says it’s just heatiness.”

She came around the table. For a moment it looked like she was going to touch his arm, then she put her hand behind her back. “All of us care about you and the nation,” she said. “We’re here to share your burdens.”

She made the sign of the flame, bowed, and left. Her footsteps echoed on the timber floors.

---

That evening, after taking his herbal tea in Toph’s cup and a dinner of rice soaked in miso soup, Zuko ignored the files collecting on his desk and dug out his writing implements. After a little hesitation, he brought the little dragon lamp to his desk and let it catch his flame. Balancing his spectacles on his nose, he ground his ink and dipped his brush, deliberating.

*Dear Sokka,*

*Thanks for the letter and invitation. I enjoyed being back at Piandao’s estate. Makes me remember* *training there as a kid. I’m out of practice with the dao. Things are always busiest around the* *coronation, so I’m not sure how soon I can come back. The Council nearly bit my head off for* *taking unannounced leave.*

*Council seems to think they can announce the Grants on Coronation and Armistice Day. I’m just* *dreading how big the protests are going to be. Everyone’s mad about Yu Dao and the firebending* *crisis.*

*Your calligraphy looked decent, but don’t think I didn’t notice you writing one of the easiest* *characters.*

*Send my regards to Piandao and Fat.*

*Best,*

Zuko had two seals, one with his personal name, *Fire Lord Zuko*, in angular seal script; the other was the National Heirloom Seal for stamping official documents, a weighty block of granite two inches on each side, topped with a pyramid of writhing dragons. With a smile tugging at the corner of his mouth, he dipped that one in cinnabar paste and stamped it onto the paper, where it declared with bristling phoenix-eye ligatures:

*The Office of the Son of the Sun, Lord of the Great Fire Nation*

---

*To the Office of the Son of the Sun, Lord of the Great Fire Nation* &#91;this was written in a shaky but passable clerical script&#93;*, His Royal Jerkface,*

*Even Piandao* &#91;Sokka had put vigorous lines beside the characters for his name&#93; *was scratching his* *head over that one. He had to consult his library and match up some of the examples. Phoenix-eye* *large seal script, what a doozy. Of course they had to invent a whole dramatic in-theme script for* *the Fire Lord.*

*It’s a shame you can’t make it but I’m sure the day won’t be as bad as you make it sound. Piandao* *says the Shu Jing celebrations have novelty firebending performances and acrobatics, and* *everyone walks around eating fire flakes and deep-fried samosas. OK, Fat said the last part,* *actually. Look, the protesters will just have to succumb to all the delicious snacks. I know I did* *when Aang, Katara, and I visited that Fire Days Festival during the war.*

*Your showing off actually got me kind of interested in seals. I used a standard-issue university one* *to sign my thesis at Ba Sing Se, but Piandao says there are a zillion different types for a zillion* *different things. He showed me some of his and he has… ten? I think? Some of them he carved* *himself. Wanted to try doing that but he says it’s a lot harder to guide a beginner through than* *forging a sword.*

*You know me, though, I want to make my mark on the world in my signature style. Piandao helped* *me design my own to give to a carver in town. He said it would be too expensive to get one as big* *as yours with “Sokka of the Southern Water Tribe, Boomerang Guy” engraved. I wanted to get it* *right before I sent you my reply. So, I can’t outdo you in the wanky seal department, but I’m sure* *you’ll give it your stamp of approval.*

*Anyway.*

*Happy coronation and armistice in advance,*  
*Sokka, Southern Water Tribe*

The six red characters sat neatly atop each other in seal script. Zuko wasn’t sure whether he was seeing things, but the two side strokes of the character for water didn’t look like the convention for the script font. They looked like little boomerangs.

---

On Coronation and Armistice Day, Zuko once again donned his ceremonial garb. He pinned the flame hairpiece to his topknot and inspected his reflection. Sometime in the last week, a cluster of angry red zits had broken out across the unscarred bit of his forehead and a servant had to dab makeup over them. Up close, the powder made them look worse. Zuko prayed that they wouldn’t be noticeable from afar.

He would be opening the ceremonies at the Square of the Sun’s Peace, which had finished construction earlier in the year. It revived an old town square, relocating some of the old offices and clearing the old monuments of war to facilitate performances and public displays. When he proposed it, he had wanted to make a point about using peacetime to facilitate public dialogue and showcase the contributions of each citizen of the Fire Nation. Right now, as he loitered in the wings of the stage, muttering the lines of his speech under his breath, he just felt sick.

Osha led his mother over as he shuffled through the scroll. “Congratulations, my son. Eight years.”

He nodded stiffly. “Thank you, Mama.”

“You’re feeling better?”

“I saw the physician. He said it was inflammation and he’s put me on cooling medicine.”

She squeezed his upper arm and he smiled, feeling the tightness of the corners of his mouth. “You’ll be great, son. The Square looks brilliant. Very new.”

Ikem and Kiyi were bringing up her rear and as Osha turned to lead them to their seats in the royal box, Zuko detained Ikem with a hesitant hand. “Could I ask you something quickly? Just us two?”

He and Mother exchanged a quick look.

“We’ll go ahead, husband.” She put her arm around Kiyi’s shoulders, and Zuko exchanged a quick smile with Kiyi as they left.

“Is anything the matter, kid?” said Ikem. “She fusses over you, but you know that’s how she shows her love.”

Zuko twisted a strand of hair between his fingers. “You and Mama miss the theatre, right?”

Ikem blinked. “Oh! Well, I suppose we do. We still produce some plays there, you know, when everyone has time.”

“She used to take me and Azula to see *Love Amongst the Dragons* when we were kids.”

“It’s her favourite.”

“At Ember Island, the troupe butchered it all the time. It was amazing to see the two of you onstage at Hira’a performing it. I just,” he took a breath, unable to meet Ikem’s eyes. “I know how much the theatre meant to her, after…” he searched for a word to encompass his father’s cruelty, the court intrigue in which she had been unwillingly embroiled, and found none, “everything.”

“Oh kid, of course. But—”

“I’m announcing the winners of the Cultural Revival Grant today,” Zuko said in a rush. “I don’t know who they’ll be. But I want the Hira’a Theatre to receive funding too.”

Ikem looked surprised. “You don’t—”

“The recession took that away from her. You. Both of you.” Zuko sighed, tucking his hands into his sleeves. “There’s just so little I can do for my mother these days.”

“That’s not true.”

“It *feels* true.”

There was a brush of fabric and Ikem put his hands on the fine, brocaded robes that once belonged to Zuko’s father, squeezing his arms through them. “Zuko, listen.”

“I—”

“Shh, none of that. You need to stop blaming yourself for how your mother and I choose in our life. We had this conversation when we turned down your help during the recession. Your mother made the choice to renounce royal life, and while I love that we can sit in your fancy seats today, your personal patronage is going to look like nepotism. That won’t fly well.”

“Yeah, I thought of that,” said Zuko dully. “Just wanted to make her happy.”

“We did apply for the grant,” Ikem admitted. “If we get it, we get it. If we don’t, then we’ll try again next year. The local bureaucracy isn’t bad either, you know. Big perks with job security.”

“So you want it?”

Ikem chuckled, giving him a final squeeze before drawing back. “Zuko, when a child gets older, it’s harder for a parent to keep showing love in the same way. Look at Kiyi. Five years ago, she was a clingy little kid who liked animal figurines and drawing with charcoal. Now, all she does is read books. Books, books, books. She won’t go anywhere without them. Stays in her room reading instead of coming out to join us. She even uses her firebending to read them after bedtime.” He shook his head, the smile under his greying moustache a little sad. “Your mother and I scold her all the time for it. I know it’s part of her figuring out who she is, and becoming separate people from her parents. Obviously, your mother and I were kids once too.

“Kiyi is ten, Zuko. You’re twenty-five. You’ve already become your own person, and that transformation happened when you were separated from your mother. I know she’s proud of you. You’re the *Fire Lord*. You ended the war and you’re doing everything you can to pay back the wrongs we did to the world. So, save your money kid. Go on more holidays. Osha said you had fun in Shu Jing.” He raised his eyebrows playfully. Outside, a horn blasted and shouts followed. “Crowd’s waiting. Good luck out there, kid.”

As Zuko stepped out, flanked by guards, he started to regret having the conversation with Ikem before his speech. The audience spread before him. There were rice-paper streamers curling through the air, and the smell of firecracker smoke wafted up to him. He could see Ikem making his way to the royal box. There were cheers, and there were boos. His head was whirling through Ikem’s words as he stood in the middle of the platform, and he struggled to recall the speech. “Thank you for eight years, citizens,” he began, and by some miracle the rest of the words strung along like a row of prayer beads. One following the other. Standing before the crowd, voice projecting, acting the role of the Fire Lord; he could revive the echo of that day, eight years ago now, when he had knelt for the Sages to crown him, and the cool grip of Aang’s small hand in his as they surveyed the nations gathered before them. It had felt like the future was theirs to mould in their own image.

The speechwriter had let him add a small section to the end, profoundly edited. “Citizens, we spent the last eight years reckoning with our antagonistic role in the war. Because of us, the other three nations went through unimaginable suffering. Because of us, they lost essential parts of what constituted them. What's more, the war did not only damage the legacy of the other nations.” He paused, allowing the ripple to ebb through the crowd. He caught sight of the royal box, of his mother and Ikem inside and Kiyi too, peeking out from the top of her book. “*Our* culture was perverted too, and the practices of our ancestors forgotten. Our technologies used to destroy homes. Our love for the arts, the things that made us love life, forgotten as we turned our attentions to war. Even the source of our firebending”—the murmur in the crowd was louder now, and Zuko pushed through it—“the source of our firebending became twisted. We destroyed ourselves and turned that destruction to our neighbours. Truly, we have become estranged from who we are as a nation.

“I will change that. We will be reunited with the practices of our ancestors. Not the ones who brought war and shame upon this nation, but the ones who made us who we *are*. Let them stoke our flame anew.”

He shot a spray of sparks into the air and ceded the stage to Councillor Po, who would announce the winners of the Cultural Revival Grant. There were ten, and Zuko held his breath as Councillor Po unfurled the scroll with theatrical gusto. A local clique of poets reconstructing fragmentary predynastic ghazals, an emerging atelier for traditional lotus-flax silk robes, a Sage acolyte’s attempt to restore an old fire temple, a youths’ dance troupe in the eastern archipelago, a zither club from Shu Jing, and so on. The crowd applauded as he moved to the side, welcoming the young dancers who had flown in at the last minute. He never mentioned Hira’a.

### End Notes

#### Chapter End Notes

I put way too much thought into the seals. I imagined Zuko's to look something like this beast. In Chinese: 陽子大烈火國君之官

Ozai's seal in the show calls him 王 (king) even though he's called 君 (lord) in other written documents... I just went with 君.

## Chapter 3: Out of Office

### Notes

#### Chapter Notes

Content warning: This chapter contains some discussion of police brutality.

### Text

Sokka was the one to fling open the lotus gate this time.

“You’re back!” He had a big cheesy grin on his face, reeling Zuko in for a hug. “Where’s Osha?” he asked, right as Zuko said, “Where are Fat and Piandao?”

Sokka leaned back, blinding in the sunlight. The restored wolf tail made him look at once younger and older, a palimpsest of the teenage war hero glinting through the present image. “You first.”

“She’s on leave. Going windsurfing with her wife, probably. Thought I’d come here while she has time off, so I’m not making her travel across the country again. Also,” Zuko turned around to face the two guards who had accompanied him on the way up, dropping a pouch of taels into their hands, “you are dismissed.”

“Your Majesty—!”

“Enjoy the town. Nowhere safer for me than living in a household of master swordsmen. Meet me back here before the flight.”

Laughing, Sokka reached past him to close the door on their flummoxed faces. He slapped an arm over Zuko’s shoulder and hoisted him towards the house. “We’ve got the house to ourselves for a bit. Piandao’s at zither club, Fat’s running errands.” He swung himself around one of the wooden columns in the reception room and down the right hallway to the rooms. “I ought to thank you for the Grant stuff. Piandao has never been happier. Digging through his antique score collection and humming, most days. Say, you don’t get to choose them, right? Any little Fire Lord’s wink-wink nudge-nudge nominations?”

Sourly, Zuko thought about the Hira’a theatre. “Of *course* not. That would be improper.”

“Relax, buddy, I’m not accusing you of nepotism.” Sokka stopped at Zuko’s designated room and gestured for him to go inside. “Good to know Piandao’s club won fair and square.”

Zuko shrugged his bag off onto a finely carved chair. Fat had kept the room as tidy and fresh as the last time he had visited, nearly two months ago. The camphor bed sat on a raised platform, shrouded by a gossamer-thin mosquito-ant net. Piandao had hung a triptych of painted wood panels along one wall, depicting scenes from the *Romance of the Four Nations*; on the other side of the room, there was a vanity and a bathtub behind the antique privacy screen. The breeze came billowing through the light curtains. It was truly as tranquil as the rest of the house.

Sokka was leaning against the door. “I expect you’re hungry,” he said. “Two options. One, I cook. Two, we go into town and buy food.”

Zuko considered. After the walk to Piandao’s, his stomach was feeling calmer than usual. He was up for a meal. “How’s your cooking?”

“I’m a jerky expert, not to brag, but that’s impossible to make by lunch. Give me a fish or a tiger-seal and I’ll work wonders with it. Aaaand with Earth Kingdom ingredients, I can whip up a cup noodle, à la broke university student.”

“A *cup* noodle?”

“Do we even have fish?” Sokka wandered towards the kitchen, Zuko following. “Fat would probably buy them fresh. I’m assuming *you* don’t cook. No offence.”

“What is a cup noodle?”

“Oh, to be a rich boy who has never had to eat cup noodles.” Poking through the pantry, Sokka gave a theatrical sigh. “It’s easy. You buy a massive pack of dried rice noodles from your local vendor. *Has* to be rice noodles, in my opinion. Some people go for wheat but they don’t cook through, I reckon. When you’re hungry, you put the noodles in a cup of hot water and wait for the noodles to soften up. Eat.” He patted his stomach. “Sustained me for five years, that stuff.”

“That sounds worse than the diet my physician has me on now.”

“Don’t knock it ‘til you try it. You can put, like, leftovers in it too. Or skewers from vendors.” He paused, looking a little sentimental. “I guess we’ll go to town.”

There was the problem of Zuko’s scar: hiking up to the estate with a hat was one thing, but interacting up close with the townspeople was another. However, Sokka produced from the storage room a wide-brimmed hat draped with a veil made of the same material as the mosquito-ant nets. “I thought this might happen,” he explained. “Fat uses this to keep off insects when he’s gardening.”

Zuko tried it on. He could see easily enough from inside the veil, and Sokka assured him that his features were obscured from the outside.

The town square was bustling with lunchtime activity when they arrived, and it seemed like Sokka had ingratiated himself with half the townspeople. Calls of *Hi, Sokka* followed them as they squeezed through the street and he was waving back to everyone. It was the most invisible Zuko had been since becoming Fire Lord.

“Do they seriously all like you?” Zuko hissed. Given the divided opinions he regularly encountered from his own citizens about the war, he hadn’t expected the man who helped down a battalion of Fire Nation airships to be so roundly liked.

“Sure. What’s not to love?”

It wasn’t just the older shopkeepers either, who could be relied on to stray into the overly-friendly side of social boundaries. A strapping lad with half a deer-pig carcass slung over his back visibly ran his eyes over Sokka’s form as they walked past, and a curvy young woman came to block their path.

“Who’s your friend, Sokka?”

“Hey, Kriti,” said Sokka easily, directing Zuko to sidestep her. “Just entertaining a former student of Piandao.”

She followed, twirling a strand of hair that framed her pretty face. “Are you coming over again soon? I’m sure there’s something that needs fixing…”

“Sure.” A hand came between Zuko’s shoulder blades, pushing him to walk faster. “I’ll send a hawk.”

They lost Kriti in the crowd. “I was looking at some of her, uh, plumbing. Couple of weeks ago,” said Sokka inanely.

He led Zuko to a restaurant that he, Aang, Katara, and Toph had visited when they came to Shu Jing and requested a balcony seat; he sat Zuko facing the river while he faced the other patrons. He ordered a few dishes and Zuko warily took his hat off when the waiter left. His mind was still lingering on Kriti. Sokka had changed out of his changshan for their jaunt, sporting instead a sleeveless red tunic that showed off the Water Tribe tattoos on his bicep. His boomerang was strapped to his back. A new smattering of stubble followed the curve of his jaw. Under the sunlight, a light sheen of sweat made his brown skin glow.

“You look different with your hair like that,” said Sokka. Zuko started. “You know, this whole”— he demonstrated the vague shape of a bun at his own nape with a twirl of his finger—“deal.”

“Oh.” Zuko touched the chignon, hastily speared into place with a hairpin. It was part of the disguise, since the Fire Lord everyone recognised wore his long hair half down. “You don’t like it?”

“Hey, I never said that. By the way, look—” He produced a little object from the pouch at his waist. “In the flesh,” he said with pride.

It was his seal. It sat heavy in Zuko’s cupped hand; when held up to the sun, the stone was a little translucent. The symbol for water was carved on top and Sokka’s name was tinted liberally with cinnabar. “Those are boomerangs on the water character, right?”

“You got it.”

Zuko kept his head down as the food came out. Sokka ate heartily, keeping up a stream of consciousness commentary on the spices in the dishes. Zuko took most of his meals alone, and it was oddly comforting to have Sokka prattle at him while he ate his tofu and choy sum.

Zuko ended up paying, taking advantage of the pause when Sokka was working out the tael conversions. “You know, I had the ban down pat when we came here the first time,” he complained as they made their way back to the estate. “I don’t think I’ve been back here since you overhauled the currency.”

“It’s ninety bronzes to a silver, two silvers to a gold.” Zuko had spent a long year eking out the finer details of the currency when the debasement got out of hand; the only person more familiar with it would probably be Councillor Kang. “Silver is the standard. We also have a half-silver and third-silver, plus fractions for the bronzes.”

“That’s so confusing.”

“It’s to do with weights.”

Sokka prodded Zuko about the currency as they made their way back to the estate. He seemed truly fascinated by it, as though he’d never thought much about the value of the coin itself, and Zuko fielded question after question. When they reached the gate, breathless from talking on the way uphill, Fat was already back.

“What’ve you two been up to?” he said, eyeing their flushed faces.

“Just lunch.” Sokka shot a grin sideways at Zuko. It was infectious.

---

In the afternoon, Sokka disappeared to the calligraphy room to complete the drills Piandao had set for him. Zuko drifted back into his room to squint over a brief on the Fire Nation’s loan repayments. He had packed a sheaf of missives but the view out the window kept distracting him. Was he really going to sit around with his work in these luxurious surroundings? He scrawled down a couple of notes before wandering out into the garden to watch the peacock-koi weave through the water, then found himself roped into preparing dinner with Fat, who seemed to have quickly set aside his hangups about Zuko’s majesty. With his inexperience in the kitchen, Fat set him on washing kangkung and beating eggs, then shooed him out to set the table.

Piandao returned as the sun kissed the hilly horizon and they gathered around the dinner table.

“The zither club is taking shape,” Piandao told them. “This was a fantastic initiative, Your Majesty. So many old knowledges of the Fire Nation deserve to be revived.”

“You should perform at Caldera sometime,” Zuko said. “We got the dance troupe in during the coronation celebrations.”

“Perhaps. We would need a great deal more rehearsals at this stage.” A teasing sparkle glittered in his eye. “Sokka, did you know our Fire Lord was once a talented tsungi hornist?”

“No!” cried Zuko, spraying vegetable bits. He clapped a hand over his mouth, feeling his face heat.

Sokka gave a little huff of shock and delight, glancing between Zuko and Piandao. “No way. I would never have guessed.”

Zuko swallowed his food, hastily mopping up the table with his napkin. “I was a kid. My mother made me learn it. I haven’t touched it in ages.”

“The horn was bigger than him. But even as a child, he had a real pair of lungs on him…”

Piandao spent all of dinner sharing embarrassing stories from his childhood, Sokka laughing and begging for more. Mortified as he was, Zuko couldn’t help but join in the laughter. For the first time in a long while, his stomach eased up on him. He ate, and everything was delicious.

---

When Zuko woke up the next day, he smeared the ointment over his scars as usual and spent the early hours in the courtyard between the rooms moving through basic firebending forms. He let his muscle memory guide him through the steps, tracking the sunrise with his waxing inner flame. Piandao came out soon after he did, nodding before drifting towards the dining room; Sokka only exited his room when the courtyard was bathed in morning light, wrapping a robe blearily around his shoulders. Time for breakfast, it seemed.

Piandao swung by as they were polishing off their bowls of fresh soy milk. “Finish quickly, my disciples,” he said. “I have something planned for both of you today.”

So Zuko and Sokka ended up in the sparring hall, wooden practice swords in hand as Piandao and Fat surveyed from the side.

“Sokka, see his opening. Zuko, you need to remember to use the left dao to block. Again.”

Since his firebending had improved and he had bodyguards to look after him, Zuko had spent less time with the dao. The movements came back to him with a slight delay, passing through some filter of memory before bursting into his limbs. He hadn’t seen Sokka in action with the sword much, let alone sparred against him, and he found him a formidable match.

“Hold there!” Piandao came up to them, his cloth shoes quiet on the floor as they froze in place, adjusting grips, elbows, and stances. “Drill that again.”

They stepped back into their opening stances. Sokka feinted, lunged. Zuko stepped back, low, and brought his crossed dao to block the sword. He brought the trapped blade down to his right side, and Sokka side-kicked his exposed flank.

“Again, faster.”

Piandao let them go at lunch. They lay on the wooden floor for a moment before heading to the dining room, catching their breath.

“You’re good at the sword,” said Zuko. “I’m so out of practice.”

“You’re kidding. *I’m* out of practice. You think I was swordfighting during all those years I spent at university?”

“You picked it back up quickly, then.”

“Two months with Piandao will do that to you. Why didn’t he teach me the dao?”

Zuko had tried out the sword when he trained here, and he remembered Piandao taking it and slipping the wooden dao into his hands. *A fighter like you should prioritise keeping the two sides* *in balance*, he had said.

“Hey,” said Sokka, lifting up on one elbow, “I’ll make you a deal. You show me some moves on the dao, and I’ll teach you how to throw my boomerang.”

“You’re on.”

---

After lunch, they left the estate to find somewhere flat, grassy, and unpopulated. Sokka had said in no uncertain terms that he didn’t trust Zuko not to take a roof off when he threw it, so they came to a spot upriver on the other side of the estate. Zuko brought two pairs of practice dao; Sokka brought Hawky, who rode on his shoulder and took off when they reached their destination.

“A messenger hawk is such a great pet.” A hand over his eyes to block the sun’s glare, Sokka watched the bird soar out of sight. “He feeds himself and sends my letters. It’s all you can really ask for.”

He hefted the boomerang out of its sleeve and gave a demonstration, flinging the weapon into the air. It whizzed off, glinting in the sun, and arced back into his hand. Zuko was struck by the sudden memory of it slamming into the back of his head. That was the first time they met, he realised, when he had crashed a warship into Sokka and Katara’s village. He wondered if Sokka remembered.

“It’s all about aerodynamics. You need to take the wind into account. It’s pretty still today, so you’re in luck.” He held the boomerang vertically out in front. “Tilt it a little. Now”—he stepped one foot back—“you’re going to step forwards and bring the boomerang back at the same time. Then, you throw it forwards with a flick of the wrist.”

He handed it over to Zuko. Copying Sokka, he threw it—onto the ground.

“You won’t get it on the first go. Come on, give it another shot.”

Like most things in his life, Zuko did not take naturally to boomerang throwing.

“Can’t you train Hawky to fetch this for us?” Zuko groused after jogging out to fetch it after what had to be the tenth go. On this try, the boomerang had looked like it would start on its returning arc, then crashed headlong into the grass.

“He’s a bird. He doesn’t fetch.”

“I feel like I’m doing it all wrong.”

“You got the spin,” said Sokka encouragingly. “Let’s have a look.”

He checked over Zuko’s grip and the angle of the boomerang, rewrapping his fingers around the handle, and adjusted his stance with a touch to the hip. He was standing incredibly close, Zuko realised, and the inches he had on Zuko were quite apparent. *Eyes to the front*, he chided himself. Sokka stepped back.

“OK, try again.”

He threw. The boomerang spun upwards, did a loop, and came flashing back.

“Whoooooo!” Sokka hollered. He grabbed Zuko, who was transfixed, and jumped on the spot. “Whooooooo! You did— Oh wait, shit, shit—”

The returning boomerang glanced off Zuko’s thigh and tumbled into the grass. “Ooh,” said Sokka, wincing. “You good?”

It would probably leave a bruise, but it had already been losing momentum and only the end caught him. It wasn’t enough to wear off the high of getting the boomerang to come back. Zuko laughed, plucking the boomerang from the grass and taking the stance again. “It’s nothing. You should teach me how to catch them, though.”

---

The exhilaration of the afternoon put Zuko in a good mood all through dinner. The practice dao had lain forgotten in the grass; the thrill of throwing and catching the boomerang was just too addictive. Just as Sokka had warned, not every attempt had been successful, but towards the end he was catching more than fetching. After his bath, he went to look for Sokka, tapping on his door and sliding it open.

“Have you seen this?” said Sokka from behind the paper. He was sitting against the sideboard of his bed, and the room was bathed in orange lamplight. “A third of the city’s population protested in Yu Dao yesterday.”

Zuko’s eye twitched. He had been responsible for sponsoring the Yitpao, something about political transparency and a unified national voice, so it would be poor form for him to set the paper on fire.

“*The autonomous city of five hundred thousand saw just under two hundred thousand protesters* *filling the streets in protest of what they claim to be the Earth Kingdom’s manipulation of local* *politics*,” Sokka read. Zuko sank onto a stray cushion with a dawning dread.

“*They demand that the Earth Kingdom obey the terms of the agreement they signed five years ago,* *and that the Fire Nation enforce this obedience should they continue to breach it. Some among the* *crowd told us exclusively that they would seek the path of Cranefish Town if the Earth Kingdom is* *unable to stick to its word.*

“*There have been further reports of violence breaking out between Earth Kingdom guards* *stationed at the city and the civilian protesters.*”

“What?” Just like that, Zuko’s stomach pains were back. He could feel the second the horrid bloating sensation filled his belly. “That fucking *bastard* Kuei,” he said.

Sokka looked up from the paper. “Woah man, I—”

A teenage Zuko might have kicked something over and set it on fire. The Zuko now put his face into the triangle of his hands and growled; a cloud of smoke issued out and Sokka descended into a coughing fit, flapping the paper to disperse it.

“What the hell is he doing? His guards attacked protesters?”

“Yeah, it looks like it got pretty—”

Zuko let out another growl of frustration. “How long has it even been since I was there?”

“What—what is up with Yu Dao anyway?”

“You wanna know what’s up?” Zuko ran his hands through his hair. “I’ve spent my entire reign trying to undo a hundred years of the Fire Nation’s worst crimes. Not a single person is happy with anything I do. That absolute *buffoon* just goes around doing nothing, *or* he goes around making everything actively worse, and no one gives a flaming fuck.” Another plume of smoke came out of his nostrils and he shoved his face between his knees. “Everywhere he goes, if he bothers going, he brings his stupid *bear*. The *bear* bear. You can’t have a serious diplomatic talk with a guy who has a pet bear.”

“Um… speak your truth, my guy,” said Sokka. He was leaning away from Zuko slightly, as though concerned he might explode.

“It’s just—it’s just,” said Zuko, deflating, “you know, five years ago, they signed the agreement. And *I* spent ages on that, just consulting for months.” He sighed, propping his chin on his knees. “I’d only recently found Councillor Hara. We went around the community interviewing local leaders and the citizens. Aang too.”

When it came down to it, Zuko just felt sorry for the people at Yu Dao. When he first visited, he had expected the city to be some perversion of a true Earth Kingdom place. What he had found was a community with a distinct identity, not quite Fire Nation nor Earth Kingdom, and not even a perfect hybrid of the two. It had a different rhythm to anything he had ever seen, stepping to a brusque metropolitan beat in the way people walked, traffic flowed, and even how food was served. And the people there were fiercely proud of how they had shaped that civic identity in Yu Dao’s short history. It was why they had written autonomy into the agreement. “He doesn’t think about how many people are going to suffer—are suffering—because he’s changed his mind on a whim. And everyone expects *me* to do something about it.”

“Maybe it’s cos you actually get stuff done.” Sokka put the paper aside—finally—and approached slowly, as though Zuko might bite his hand off if he made sudden movements. He crouched beside Zuko and patted his elbow, then withdrew his hand.

“That’s the problem with this Yu Dao business,” said Zuko miserably. “I can’t do anything without looking like I want to start a world war again.”

---

Zuko would be heading back to the Capital in the morning, but his stomach pains and the thoughts about Yu Dao pulsing through his head kept him awake. He got up and walked barefoot to the porch overlooking the garden and the rushing waterfall beyond. Overhead, the moon was waxing its way to fullness.

“Zuko?”

It was Piandao, his silk robe thrown over his sleeping clothes, pitcher in hand. He must have been refilling his water.

“Sorry, I just couldn’t sleep.”

Piandao stepped closer. In the moonlight, Zuko could see the whites of his eyes.

“You aren’t well,” Piandao said. It wasn’t a question. “You were better for a bit in the last few days.”

“I’ve been heaty. Apparently. The physician gave me… Anyway, it keeps happening.”

“I caught a glimpse of the headlines today.” Piandao rested his cup on the railing and turned to look at Zuko. “Your physician only has part of the story, I believe. Tell me how you feel.”

Zuko glanced up at him, then turned his gaze back over the garden. “I get these… stomach pains. I feel bloated. Sometimes, it goes away. Not always. I don’t even eat that much now. Feels like someone’s pumped me full of air.”

Piandao said nothing. “I feel… disgusting,” Zuko said, sinking lower.

“When did you last feel like that?”

“I usually feel like that. It went away when I came here.”

“And it came back when you found out about Yu Dao?”

“Yes…” said Zuko. “How did you…?”

Piandao leaned back, the moon illuminating a grim smile on his face. “There’s a specialist around here, a good friend of mine, who can help you with that. I don’t believe she’ll have an appointment before you leave tomorrow, but we can arrange one for when you come back. For now, it might help to eat more fibrous foods. You know? Beans, dried radishes, burdock root, prunes… Well, I can have Fat write to Osha.”

“You know what’s wrong with me?”

Piandao chuckled. “You don’t go this many years without picking up a thing or two. You *feel* through the core of your body. Right where you hold the breath, as a firebender.” He tapped Zuko’s sternum, against the spot where his sister had scarred him with her lightning. “Your body feels emotion here as well. I expect a lot has been collecting here.”

“I feel emotions in my… stomach?”

Piandao continued as though he hadn’t spoken. “As a ruler, you will continue to experience setbacks, many of which will affect you very personally. It is imperative that you develop strategies to not let these things burrow so deep that they impact on *your health*. A healthy Fire Lord benefits the whole world.” He stepped back, collecting his cup, and turned to go. Zuko watched him, feeling a little helpless, a little overwhelmed. “Good night, Your Majesty. An early start awaits you, and you should rest when you can.”

## Chapter 4: Physician's Orders

“Awful, awful, awful,” said Councillor Hara, fresh off an airship from Yu Dao. “The less of that damned provincial governor I see, the better.”

They had passed the rest of the ninth month in agony, and the drizzly start of the tenth was not going much better. Zuko suppressed a wince.

“Your Majesty, if this one may speak,” said the young woman beside Councillor Hara: Hoilam, a Yu Dao activist who had flown over to provide the Council a local perspective. Her words were respectful, but a hard determination laced her voice. “The councillor has explained to me your position. My associates and I have some thoughts on that.”

“The Fire Nation is cognisant of its former role in the war,” said Zuko, trying not to sound as tired as he felt. “The Earth King will interpret any show of force from us as a provocation.”

Hoilam was shaking her head. “It’s not an enviable position. But, Your Majesty, *you* wrote the agreement. You are *responsible*—” Councillor Hara raised her eyebrows, but Zuko gestured for Hoilam to continue. “Yu Dao is my home, and the home of fifty-thousand other people. We’ve carved out our own way of life, but the Earth King wants to absorb us into his hegemony and fill our streets with his agents to make sure we kneel.”

“Madam,” said Zuko, “not all in Yu Dao are favourable towards the Fire Nation either, given our history there.”

“You look at my city and see the legacy of your shameful history. But we have carved something of our own out of it. We wanted to keep things the way they were after the agreement. If the Earth King has his way, we could lose—everything.”

“Hoilam,” said Councillor Hara with a tragic gentleness, “if the Council does not tread carefully, the whole world could lose much more.”

Hoilam did not back down, something Zuko couldn’t help but find himself admiring. “There must be *something* you can do.”

In the end, they compromised on the Fire Nation issuing a stern statement of support for Yu Dao’s political autonomy. Councillor Hara sent a high-level bureaucrat back to the city with Hoilam. Zuko sent them off at the airship port, plagued by a curious sense of shame. He wished he had a better solution but right now he felt like he was trying to catch a peacock-koi fish with his hands: as soon as he thought he grasped it, the solution slipped away.

At the same time, the resurgent problem of the alterations in firebending confronted Zuko when he and Councillor Ike embarked on their tour of schools across the nation to check on the progress of his second education reform. He was gratified to note that the study of history had been much improved, but the implementation of the Dancing Dragon form across the nation had given rise to some consternation. “The students are becoming more capable than their tutors,” one principal from Shuhon Island had told him in a worried undertone as they watched a class of young teens run through their katas, kicking plumes of fire high into the sky.

This testimony matched others across the nation. Without the hatred of war to fuel their firebending, the postwar generation naturally came to draw their power from other sources, inspired by the Dancing Dragon. Conversely, it had proven much harder for seasoned adult firebenders to make the transition. Those who had been less invested in the war effort found the transition easier, but Zuko was confronted to realise just how great a percentage of his people had been direct contributors to war.

“This issue may not be as pressing as Yu Dao,” Councillor Ike told him as they took the airship back to the main island, “but it is having a real impact on your ongoing legitimacy as Fire Lord.”

Zuko scowled out the window. “Tell me something I don’t know, councillor. We’ve thrown so much money at those masters from the former colonies too, are they working out?”

She shrugged. “Some of their methods resonate, some don’t. It’s not the wholesale solution we thought they might be.”

The thinking behind inviting masters from the oldest former colonies was that they had preserved an older form of firebending, before its deeper corruption at the hands of Zuko’s father and grandfather. The problem, though, was that *Sozin* had started that corruption by sending out colonial expeditions in the first place, and a hint of it still marred the technique of the masters from even the oldest former colonies.

“If it works even a bit, I suppose we should keep funding that programme.” Zuko twisted the ends of his hair, feeling the exhaustion from the trip in his bones.

All the while, Piandao’s comments about his condition hovered in the back of his mind. Soon after his return to the palace, Hawky had delivered a letter written jointly by Piandao and Sokka; on one side was a list of recommended foods and on the other was an enthusiastic yet wonky landscape painting that Zuko recognised was the place where they had thrown the boomerang. The memory made him smile. Some of the foods seemed to regulate his digestion. What’s more, he was starting to notice that the pains worsened whenever he received bad news.

Council sat again, again, again. An earthquake sent a crack through a town hall to the north of the main island; the Council released the emergency funds and Zuko travelled there to survey the damage himself. The last rice harvest of the hot-dry season came in, filling the state granaries. During the inflation period in the third year of his reign, Zuko had started to subsidise the cost of rice. It was a steep price, but worth it for keeping some of the discontent at bay. Meanwhile, the academics working through the Archive lodged a request to publicly display some of the artefacts there. A collection of the most shameful parts of the Fire Nation’s role in the war, the Archive housed propaganda posters, doctored schoolbooks, black-flame and phoenix flags: anything that hadn’t been reduced to ashes early on in his reign.

“Thank you, Councillor Po,” Zuko said. Truth be told, he doubted that the Archive would rouse much remorse among his people, at present anyway. “Public re-education remains a priority, and these methods are novel. The Council will need to deliberate on this further.”

“Of course, Your Majesty.”

“And Councillor Kang, I believe you will be engaging in discussions in the North Pole about our ongoing loans from the Water Tribe?”

Councillor Kang shook out his sleeves, accepting a thick scroll from his secretary. “Of course, Your Majesty. Here is the current spending…”

After Zuko wrapped up the meeting, Osha came forward with a slip of paper in her hand. “I managed to get you medical leave,” she said as they exited the chambers.

“Medical leave? I’m not sick.”

“Your digestion?”

“Oh,” said Zuko. “But that’s been going on for months.”

Osha flapped the slip in his face. “Hopefully not any longer. Piandao’s physician managed to slot you in.”

Zuko halted, plucking the slip out of her hand. *Doctor Khoo will see His Majesty the Fire Lord* *Zuko on the nineteenth day of the tenth month, at* shen *hour. Please come to the Shu Jing Practice* *of Fire Medicine fifteen minutes beforehand to complete the patient survey.*

“She’s in high demand,” said Osha. “Don’t waste the opportunity.”

“You seem determined to get me to Shu Jing again.”

Osha crossed her arms, biceps flexing with the motion. “Well, I’m your valet. I’m meant to anticipate your every desire, aren’t I?”

---

So, they were back in Shu Jing. Zuko wouldn’t admit it to Osha, but he liked having the excuse to be back, to hang out in the estate with Sokka. When they arrived at the airship station, Zuko pulled his hood low over his eyes and followed what he could see of Osha’s feet to the clinic. It was a few blocks back from the town centre in an area that looked like it had been redeveloped in recent years, all new pavements and gleaming roof tiles. The reception room was dim and thankfully empty, save for the receptionist.

“Appointment for His Majesty the Fire Lord,” said Osha to her in a hushed tone.

Behind the desk was a framed ink painting of Zuko. The receptionist gasped, and came around to kneel before him. “Sorry, I didn’t recognise you, Your Majesty.”

“You don’t have to do that, I’m just a patient here,” said Zuko.

“Oh! Ten thousand apologies, Your Majesty.”

“It’s… fine. You don’t need to apologise.”

“We need to fill in some forms?” Osha prompted.

She scribbled in his details as he fidgeted with the ends of his hair. Part of him was quivering at the prospect of the stomach pains being healed; another part was thinking, *if the royal physician* *couldn’t heal me, then who can?*

Zuko put his head down as the previous patient came out of the consulting room. “Thank you for coming in today, ma’am,” he heard the physician say. A ruffle of paper as the receptionist handed his forms to the arm reaching from the door, which shut again. His stomach thrummed. The door opened.

“Ah… His Majesty?”

“That’s me,” said Zuko, getting to his feet.

Dr Khoo was a small bespectacled woman, older than Zuko’s mother, steel grey hair plaited down her back.

“Do you want me to come?” Osha whispered.

“It’s fine. I’ll come out to ask you anything I don’t know.”
