Osha squeezed his elbow as he passed, a rare companionable touch from her.

The consulting room was dim, lamplit; the sole window was shrouded by a curtain. Dr Khoo sat at a desk that faced the wall. There were two cushions and a mattress raised on a low platform. The room smelled of incense and bitter medicinal herbs. “Take a seat, Your Majesty,” she said, bowing with the sign of the flame. Zuko bowed back and folded himself onto one of the cushions.

Dr Khoo was shuffling through his forms. “Family history of high blood pressure… Injuries: significant burns to the face and chest… Conditions: poor vision in left eye, corrected by spectacles for reading; hard of hearing in the left ear… Ongoing medications: ointment for scars and cooling tea containing chrysanthemum, monksfruit, and mesona. And you are seeing me for persistent digestive issues, correct?”

Zuko nodded. “Master Piandao recommended you to me. I’ve been having problems since… it must have been half a year ago. My physician said it was due to heatiness, but…”

“How do these digestive problems present?”

“I feel bloated and full all the time, even when I drink a cup of water. It’s… painful.”

Dr Khoo pulled out a sheet of paper, starting to jot down notes. “What kind of pain?”

“I’m not sure… just like it’s going to burst?”

“Sharp pain? Dull? On a scale from one to ten?”

“Dull most of the time. Maybe a three. It’s not the worst pain I’ve ever had. Actually, though”—he pointed to a soft spot beside his left hipbone—“sometimes, I get a sharp pain here.”

“Do you ever feel a burning pain in the middle of your chest, especially after a meal?”

“No. Yes? Just once or twice, I think, and it’s not too bad.”

“And the bloating, you feel that all the time?”

“Yes.” Then Zuko thought. “Actually, not when I wake up. And sometimes I don’t get it at all.” He hesitated. “When I was last in Shu Jing, I was fine most of the time. Even ate a lot. Then I heard about that protest in Yu Dao… That’s when Master Piandao came and recommended you.”

“I see. How is your diet? How are your bowel movements?”

Zuko spluttered. “My…?”

Dr Khoo blinked patiently behind her spectacles.

“So it’s not heatiness?”

“Your Majesty, the physician’s clinic is a safe space, and your information is kept confidential. Please answer if you can.”

“I, uh, go to the. Toilet. Basically every day now.”

“You say *now*. Was there some change?”

“Master Piandao recommended some foods, he said they were more fibrous.”

“Yes, fibre can regulate digestion.”

“Yeah,” said Zuko. “It’s gotten better since, though it’s not. Um. Perfect.”

“Alright.” Dr Khoo hung up her brush. “If you could lie on the bed and lift your shirt, let me observe the belly.”

Zuko did as she asked, undoing the ties of his shirts. Dr Khoo washed her hands and prodded his stomach with a firm, warm touch. Swallowing, Zuko stared up at the ceiling.

“Does it hurt when I touch?” she asked, prodding the soft spot he had pointed out before.

“Um, no.”

“I can see you are a little bloated, but visually it doesn’t look as severe as how you describe it to feel. I can also feel the chi clustered around here,” she said, touching his lower belly.

“You can feel it?”

“Of course. It’s yang energy, heat. I can also feel the source of your bending. The sun inside you.”

“You’re a firebender?”

Dr Khoo laughed. “The clue is in the name of my practice.”

*Shu Jing Practice of Fire Medicine*. Of course. It hadn’t even occurred to him.

“So I *am* heaty.”

“That’s one way of putting it,” said Dr Khoo, gesturing for him to put his clothes back on. “Our bodies must balance yin and yang constitutions to function harmoniously. We associate a primarily cold, yin constitution with paleness, lethargy, weak immunity, stiffness. On the other hand, a primarily hot, yang constitution is inflamed, feverish, and prone to ulcers.”

“Piandao said it was something to do with my feelings,” said Zuko slowly, moving back onto the cushion. “He said I hold my feelings in my stomach.”

“Why might he think that?”

“It was right after I heard about Yu Dao, and I was in pain again. It was almost instant,” he said, remembering that sudden onset.

Dr Khoo hummed. “And how do you feel about Yu Dao?”

“Like I never want to think about it again.”

“I expect running a nation is not easy.”

“Osha got me medical leave to come here,” said Zuko. “I’ve probably taken too much leave already. One of my friends moved here in the seventh month and I’ve visited a few times.”

“How much is too much leave?”

“This is my third time in Shu Jing since then. I don’t stay more than two days. I—enjoy spending time here, though.”

Dr Khoo raised her eyebrows. “How much leave do you get?”

“Well, Council is closed over the New Year and during public holidays, so I’ve taken those days off. Although as head of state, I *am* involved in a number of the holidays.”

“You don’t have a day off each week?”

Zuko bit his lip. “Yeah… but I’m usually still working. Reading missives, reviewing proposals… I mean. There’s a whole country to run…”

“Do you have leisure activities? Hobbies? Physical activities?”

Zuko racked his brains. “Firebending?”

“Your sleep quality?”

“Not always the best. I find it hard to fall asleep sometimes. I used to get nightmares a lot. Fewer now.”

“How is your virility?”

“My… what?”

“Your sex life.”

“Oh.” Zuko blushed. “I, uh, don’t have one.”

“And that is voluntary? Do you experience sexual frustration?”

“Sorry,” said Zuko, “but what does this have to do with my digestion?”

Dr Khoo gave him a reassuring smile. “This question may seem a little invasive, and you don’t have to answer it if you aren’t comfortable. However, I am trying to determine something about your psychological state, Your Majesty, and what your body might be trying to tell us. Your responses may give us some clues.”

“I *am* frustrated,” Zuko said in a rush. “Not just… sexually, either. It feels like ages since anyone’s touched me. Properly, I mean, like I…” *Like I was precious*, he wanted to say, but it seemed too silly to say aloud, to a physician he barely knew. Nonsensically, he thought of Sokka’s fingers on his hip, adjusting his stance when he had thrown the boomerang. “What’s wrong with me?”

Dr Khoo steepled her fingers. “The initial diagnosis from your physician of excess heat is not inaccurate,” she said. “Even for a firebender who draws heat from within, you are running hotter than you should be. I can feel this heat in your meridians. The problems with digestion and associated pain are likely due to ulcers and inflammation caused by the heat. I can easily treat this part for you. However, your physician did not investigate the cause of this heat. That is why the treatments so far, targeting only the heatiness, have not been as successful. Without targeting the *cause*, these symptoms will only return. There are several potential causes. Lack of sleep is one, hot and dry weather another. If you have not been releasing your jing, there may be a buildup of yang energy. I strongly suspect, however, that the symptoms you are experiencing are ultimately the product of stress.”

Zuko was speechless for a moment. “...stress.” He felt almost angry.

“Yes,” said Dr Khoo. “It’s actually quite common.”

“The pain is real,” he said. “I’m not just making it up.”

“I’m not saying it’s all in your head, Your Majesty. Your stress is manifesting in your body as digestive issues. Think about it. You felt relaxed in Shu Jing on holiday, but the symptoms came back when you heard stressful news.”

He knew she had to be correct. Hadn’t he noticed those changes in his gut? All those years ago as well, didn’t he become violently sick after saving Appa; didn’t his uncle point out that the illness was born from his struggle over which path to take, following his father or helping the Avatar? “No… no, I…”

“What Piandao said is true, we feel emotions in our gut. There is a connection there. When you’re nervous, do you not feel cicada-crickets in your tummy?” said Dr Khoo in her even voice. “Stress is a natural response to danger. Stress gives our bodies the alertness to overcome danger. In the long term, however, this alertness affects our bodily functions. Our ability to think and act rationally, sleep, digest, so on and so forth. You need to give your body time to rest and recover.”

“But, but… How do I even *stop* being stressed? I can’t just tell myself to stop. It just happens.”

“Well, part of it is stress management, learning strategies to deal constructively with stressful events in your life. The other part is very simple: give yourself a *break*. Honestly, I was a little surprised at the brevity of your list of conditions,” she said, picking up his patient form again. “You had very traumatising experiences during the war. Did you ever address them?”

“I can’t,” said Zuko. “I’m the Fire Lord. I don’t have time to just… Everyone depends on me. I can’t just…” Fire Lords past had regents, but Zuko didn’t have the luxury. His mother left the court, Uncle’s royal status was stripped after his trial, and Azula… He sank into his hands. “It’s all on me.”

“It’s *not*.” He looked up and saw the glinting sternness in her eyes. “Fire Lord Zuko, I wouldn’t presume to speak for all the citizens of the Fire Nation, but I believe most of us would prefer if our ruler did not work himself into an early grave. It’s not promising that your family has a history of high blood pressure. You have councillors. You have a bureaucracy. There is no reason why *you* must shoulder all the work of restoring this country after the war.”

“Only I—” His uncle’s words rang in his ear: *An idealist with a pure heart, and unquestionable* *honour*.

“You are not the only one who has a vision for what the Fire Nation can become,” she said, more gently now. “Either way, it’s not up to you, or me, or the Council, or the people, at this point. Your body is telling you it’s time to slow down. Don’t wait until it’s too late to listen.”

---

Zuko left the practice with a doctor’s note and his head abuzz. Before he left, Dr Khoo had dispersed some of his stagnant chi with her firebending, leaving an odd sensation. He and Osha made their way to Piandao’s estate, where they would be spending the night.

“How was the doctor?” said Piandao, pouring out their tea. Sokka was out in town, apparently, and Zuko’s mind strayed to Kriti before batting the thought aside.

“I think she freaked boss out,” said Osha. She had been reading the doctor’s note on the way up. “I’ve been telling him for years he ought to take a break, so I’ve been vindicated. She wants to see him again in a month.”

“I’m here now, aren’t I?” Zuko groused. “I’m taking a break.” He took a too-hot sip of his tea.

“Say, Master Piandao,” said Osha. “You couldn’t teach me a few”—she made mimed a few swordfighting forms—“moves, could you?”

He gave her a critical eye, finger tapping against the rim of his teacup. “Perhaps I will… *if* you spar with the Fire Lord.”

When Sokka found them in the training hall, Osha was thwacking the flat of her practice sword on the backs of Zuko’s thighs. “Sorry,” he said, cocking a hip into the doorframe. “Am I interrupting?”

“Please do,” said Zuko, collapsing onto the ground with no small amount of dramatic flair. “They’re ganging up on me.”

“Come on now, Your Majesty.” Osha ran a lap, then poked Zuko with the wooden point of the sword. He swatted her away. “You don’t want a bodyguard who’s worse at fighting than you, do you?” She turned to bow with the sign of the flame to Piandao. “Thank you for your expert feedback, Master.”

“Aw, don’t tell me I missed the Osha v Zuko showdown!” Sokka came inside to crouch on the edge of the training floor, drumming his hands on the timber. “Come *ooon*. Go again.”

Osha reached out a hand, which Zuko took as Sokka egged them on. They took their stances: Zuko low and guarded, Osha high and unfurling. Zuko moved first, dao clashing with the sword, spinning away, parrying with both blades. He knocked the sword aside, but Osha spun it in her hand. The sword dislodged and the momentum sent Zuko’s dao slashing wide. He caught his footing but Osha was coming down; he ducked, hit the floor, and rolled to the side, springing to his feet. He aimed low, for her legs. She jumped, swapping the stance midair to land hard and low. Another parry; the sword, trapped between his dao, pulled from her grasp and he flung it high. Osha sprang up; she kicked his knee down, plucked the hilt from the air. Zuko tumbled to the ground and she followed, knee to his chest, wooden blade against the throat.

“And that’s why I hired you,” said Zuko dryly.

“Bravo! Bravo!” Sokka called from the sidelines. “Osha, that was a masterclass.”

She helped Zuko up. They made the sign of the flame and bowed before hanging up the practice weapons. “You put up a good fight, Your Majesty. We should train together more back home.”

“Any chance of Osha v Master Piandao?” said Sokka hopefully.

“Don’t push it, kid,” said Piandao. He wandered off with Osha, nattering on about the arcanities of different blade forms.

“I take it that it’ll be a raincheck on you showing me how to use the dual dao,” said Sokka, coming to lean on the wall beside Zuko as he gulped down a cup of water.

“No thanks. You were in town earlier?”

“Pitching in on one of the construction sites, sponsored by His Majesty.” Sokka must have seen something in his face, because he then asked, “Hey, what’s up? How was the doc?”

Zuko shrugged. He hung up the practice blades and turned to leave the hall. “She said it was stress.”

“Huh.”

“Apparently, it can manifest as a physical illness.”

Sokka was quiet for a while. “Man, that sucks,” he said eventually.

---

The next day, Sokka wheedled Piandao into letting Zuko demonstrate the dual dao for him.

“I still want that footwork drilled by the time I return,” Piandao said at the gate, as Sokka and Zuko saw him off to his zither club. He had asked Osha to tag along, even though she insisted that she had no musical talents. Both of them donned broad brimmed waxed straw hats to keep the rain off. “You aren’t going to neglect your primary weapon.”

“No worries, Sifu,” Sokka chirped. “Zuko’s enough of a stick-in-the-mud to keep me on track.”

“*Hey*.”

Sokka took the steps back up to the house two at a time. “Show me your moves, hotshot.”

In the training hall, Zuko tossed Sokka a pair of practice dao, swinging his pair around to get back into the rhythm. “Let’s start off simple.” He took a stance and demonstrated a basic chopping motion. Sokka copied him. “You want to keep the blades straight. Only move the wrists. Good.”

Zuko demonstrated another move, more complex this time. He swung the dao first on his left, then his right, letting the blades chase each other in loops around his body. “Don’t think of the weapons as separate. They’re two halves of a whole. Move them as one.”

An Earth Kingdom boy, a field of sunflowers at night. Sokka copied his move and the blades tangled. “Slower. Watch. Remember to keep those elbows in.”

Sokka grunted, concentrating hard on the arc of his blades. He found the rhythm quickly, dao swishing on either side.

“Then you can add the footwork.” Zuko let his feet trace triangles on the floor; side step, forward step, side step, back step.

Sokka stumbled.

“It’s fine, it’s fine,” he said, brushing Zuko off as he got back to his feet, picking up his dao. There was a hard look on his face. “My footwork still needs a lot of practice. Piandao says I need to strengthen the muscles there. It’s not been the same since I broke it.”

“You broke your foot?”

“My leg.” Sokka took his stance again, twirling more slowly and running through the footwork. “Don’t you remember? I was on crutches and everything.”

Zuko didn’t, or maybe he did. “Can I see?”

Sokka took both dao in one hand, lifting his left pant leg to reveal a small scar on the shin, a white bump mostly hidden by hair. Zuko crouched to see. “The bone came out there. It was so gruesome. I guess you were with Katara when it happened, so you probably don’t remember that part.” Unthinkingly, Zuko reached out a hand to brush over the raised bump. “It still hurts?” His own burn scars didn’t hurt; at most, the skin just felt too tight.

He looked up and met Sokka’s eyes. There was a beat, Sokka’s face impassive. “When it rains and stuff, yeah.” He let the fabric fall back over his leg, and the moment broke.

---

At night, Zuko lay awake, cocooned in the mosquito-ant net. The fresh, woody scent of camphor incense drifted through the air. The conversation with Dr Khoo looped through his mind. He had to fly back to Caldera tomorrow. Heat, inflammation. Wanting to please the Council, the people, the whole world, his mother. Untouched and alone. Azula, however she was doing now. If what Dr Khoo had said were true, would he be mired again in this labyrinth of his own making once he returned to the palace? Was all that truly making his body sick?

He slipped out of his room. The waning moon touched the estate with silver; Zuko conjured a flame in his palm and wandered through the halls. Sokka was now occupying the room Zuko had slept in as a child. Piandao’s was further down; Zuko had never been inside, but glimpses from the outside had revealed a larger, more spacious room with partitions shuttering the sleeping area from view. The reception room, the dining room… His shadow threw monstrous shifting shapes on the walls. He made his way to the garden and came to the bench beside the peacock-koi pond, depositing the flame into a lantern hanging over the fish. The rain had eased up and while some clouds lingered, they did not obscure the sky overhead, awash with stars, their glittering reflection playing over feathered fins.

A soft crunch of footsteps came behind him. “You look like you could use some company,” said Sokka.

“Did I wake you?”

“Nah, just giving Hawky a checkup. Heard you pass by and came out to be nosy.” He came around and nudged Zuko. “Hey, thanks for showing me the dao today.”

“That’s alright. Had to repay you for the boomerang.”

They were quiet for a bit. “Moon,” said Sokka, pointing.

Zuko looked. “Yeah.”

They spent a while watching the fish dawdle their way around the pond. Sokka was quieter than usual, which Zuko took to be a testament to his sleepiness. He wanted to tell Sokka that he could go back to sleep if he were tired, that he didn’t have to stay with Zuko—but a guiltier part of him liked that he wasn’t outside alone.

Sokka broke the silence. “My dad.” His voice came out croaky and he coughed. “My dad taught me how to read the stars to navigate, during the long winter night. You move further north, though, and you get a whole different set of them. I only found that out when we left home that first time.” He clutched his robe tighter around himself, staring into the sky. “In Ba Sing Se, you can't see all the stars from the South Pole. But here, in the Fire Nation, you can see both the northern and southern ones.” He gestured. “Quturjuuk, the collarbones. Qimmiit there, the polar dogs.”

Zuko was still watching the peacock-koi, the glimmer of their scales in the firelight. “The cause was myself,” he said, barely above a murmur, the kind of sound that could slip away and hide itself in the night. “*I’m* the reason why I was having digestion problems. Me being *stressed*.” He scoffed. “As always, I am the author of my own unhappiness.”

Sokka hummed. “Years ago, when it was just us travelling together, we came across this fortune teller. I didn’t believe any of that mumbo-jumbo. But then she said that my life would be *full of* *struggle and anguish, most of it self-inflicted*.”

It suited Zuko’s life extraordinarily well, he felt. “Sometimes, I feel like I’m the only person in the Fire Nation who has a vision of what it takes for this country to get better.”

Sokka turned to face him, but Zuko looked resolutely forwards. “That can’t be true.”

“Eight years on, people are still flying the black flame.”

“It was an extremist ideology that gripped your nation for a hundred years, Zuko. It’s not gonna go away that quickly.”

“I’m sorry.” Zuko gave a humourless laugh, burying his face in his hands. He felt the swish of his hair falling to shroud his face. “You came here to learn from a master and catch up with an old friend, and I’m here having a crisis all over it.”

Sokka fell quiet again, leaning back on the bench.

“It’s always ups and downs,” said Zuko, feeling seized to fill the silence. “We’ll win over a bunch of the citizens and they’ll reject the past, and it all goes wrong and they’re against me again.”

“You mean the firebending crisis?”

It had been cropping up in the Yitpao, so it was no surprise Sokka knew about it. “The Council prefers to say *alteration*, rather than *crisis*,” said Zuko automatically. Then the guise of the politician fell away. “It’s what happened to me when I joined you guys, on a larger scale. Except I found some dragons and no one else has.”

“When you and Aang went off to the Sun Warriors?”

“Right. My firebending had been fuelled by the rage and hatred of war, and without that fuel I had to find a more sustainable source. Here, feel.”

He held out a hand and Sokka gripped his fingers. “Yeah, you’re warm.”

Zuko withdrew it. “Aang’s like this too. We have the sun inside us, but I never experienced this growing up in the Fire Nation, before we met the dragons. Not every firebender feels warm. As my people renounce the mindset of war, they lose their original fuel, except this time there’s no convenient dragon around to give them a new purpose.”

“Sooo,” said Sokka slowly, “forgive me if I’m being obtuse about this, but can’t you just, y’know, ask those dragons to come and breathe some fire all over your country, job done, happy happy flamey days?”

“The Sun Warriors swore us to secrecy,” said Zuko. “Anyway, they refused to come as well. Aang and I asked them a few years ago.”

“You told *us*.”

“Well, yeah. But you’re not a nation of rabid ex-imperialists who’ve lost control over their element.” Zuko sighed. “A bunch of people cracked it. Kids who grew up after the war, people who weren’t heavily involved in the war effort… But also, my father and grandfather practically mobilised the whole nation. If they weren’t fighting, they were making uniforms and equipment, or organising logistics, or colonising, or paying taxes. So, too many people have been implicated.”

Sokka was looking much more awake, contemplative. “My thesis was about this. For the Southern Water Tribe, though.”

“Your thesis… on engineering?”

“You feel like the Fire Nation lost something fundamental about itself to the war, right?” Zuko nodded. “For a bender like Katara, it’s obvious. She was the only one left, and the Southern waterbending techniques were snuffed out before they could be passed onto her. In the South Pole, we used to have… Everything was made of ice and snow, and without maintenance they melted back into the landscape. We used to have ports, we could communicate quickly with other Southern villages. We had aquaculture: dams, sluices, fish farms and traps. We had observatories to watch the stars. I hadn’t even known half of it until I started researching.”

“You lost knowledge,” said Zuko, understanding. “We tried getting old masters from Cranefish Town and Yu Dao to teach us the techniques again.”

“How does that work?”

“Well, it’s not just a hybrid culture they have. They’ve preserved a lot of old traditions. Since they’re distanced from the Fire Nation itself, it’s like they haven’t followed all of our progress.”

“I assume they haven’t been the fix you wanted, though?”

Zuko shook his head.

“What does Aang think?”

“Aang?” Zuko scoffed. “He thinks *I’m* the one who’s meant to bring it back.”

“Are you?”

“*No*.” He had tried demonstrating his new source to some Fire Nation masters, especially while Aang was around to complete his training, but the unspoken display the dragons had conjured expressed more than Zuko ever could with the Dancing Dragon form, let alone *words*.

“Honestly, I think it’s a problem for the Avatar.”

“I just don’t want to bother him with petty domestic stuff again.”

Sokka gave him a look. “Domestic? Zuko, you can’t keep thinking like that. He’s the elements and balance guy, it’s right up his alley. Isn’t all this a big spiritual imbalance? Look, I’ll write to him. Hawky will know where to find them.”

*Them*. He must mean Aang and Katara.

“How are they? Have you seen them lately?”

“*Have* I seen them lately?” Sokka parroted. “With a flying bison and all, you’d think they’d be able to come around more often. It’s not like I was there with the two of them every step of the way, when we were travelling together.”

“Oh.”

Sokka looked sideways at him, a little remorse coming into his eyes. “Sorry. You don’t need to hear this. It’s not that I don’t love Aang. I really do. He’s a great kid. Every brother wants his baby sister to marry the best person in the world, and she’s married the *Avatar*.”

Zuko wasn’t sure what to say. He had never put much thought into Azula or Kiyi’s hypothetical future marriages. “I’m sure they’re just busy. Saving the world and stuff.”

“Yeah, and the world always comes before your big brother. It’s just—” He was staring into the sky, not quite addressing Zuko. “Do you ever think they got married kinda young? Is it just me? And I don’t mean *legally*. I know *technically* Aang’s like one-twenty-one but he basically *is just* twenty-one, so twenty when…” Sokka took a deep breath. “*I* remember being that age and what a complete pisshead I was. I mean, I still kinda am one.” He snorted. “That’s probably why I’m the one doomed to a lifetime of self-inflicted anguish.”

Zuko looked at him, looked at him properly. He had always seen Sokka as a happy-go-lucky kind of guy, his cynicism coming off more ironic than genuine. Even at his most serious, at the Boiling Rock prison or while they had prepared to confront Zuko’s father, he had never sounded truly bitter. It occurred to him that, before Sokka had moved to Shu Jing, so many of their previous interactions had been mediated through other members of the group. This had to be Sokka at his most unfiltered. The thought stunned him a little. Did Sokka find *Zuko* unfiltered here too?

“A guy just wants to see his sister sometimes,” said Sokka, voice back to normal.

“I miss Azula too.” He wasn’t sure what he was doing, but perhaps Sokka’s confession warranted a responding show of vulnerability. Who else was listening, except for the moon and the fish? “I know you have no reason to care for her, but last time I saw her I really thought she was doing so much better.” The institution released her soon after too, saying that she had passed all the checks with flying colours. The next thing he knew, she had enrolled herself into the Sagehood. There, not even the Fire Lord could touch her if an acolyte decided she did not want to see him, no matter how many times he came to their gates. “I thought I could be for her what my uncle was for me,” he admitted shamefully. “I just don’t know why she decided to cut me off.”

“Do you think she’ll ever reach out?”

“I think if she really wanted to, she could ignore me forever.” Zuko tipped his head back, looking into the dark branches of the willow tree. “When I found my mother again, I thought everything would go back to normal, and then I wouldn’t need Azula anymore. I had a new father in Ikem, a new sister in Kiyi. Isn’t that awful?”

Sokka didn’t respond.

“Anyway, then I just started being this big annoyance to her, or something. Every time I see her, she’s always on my case for something. Ikem says it’s how she shows she cares. During the recession, she lost the work she loved…”

“I’m sure she doesn’t blame you for that.”

“That’s what Ikem says too,” Zuko said, glum. “I think I remind her of my father. I look more and more like him every year. Scar aside, obviously.”

“I’ve only seen your dad once and he looked like a massive loser,” said Sokka. “You know, Aang once made a portrait of him out of noodles.”

It was so out of left field that Zuko barked out a laugh. “What?”

“Yeah, he enrolled in a Fire Nation school and one of the activities was apparently making a noodle portrait of Ozai. Hey, does that mean kids make noodle portraits of *you*?”

Zuko shoved him.

Sokka was knocked over the arm of the bench, laughing. “Ah, fuck it,” he said. He lifted an arm over the back of the bench. “Get in here, buddy.”

Gratefully, Zuko scooted under his arm, into his side. Sokka gave his shoulders a bracing, manly squeeze. “No offence, but I think if I saw your sister again I’d shit myself. *Blue* fire? Like, how freaky can you get?”

“Hey, *your* sister was plenty scary to me too. If *we* can be friends now…”

In the morning, he woke up to Sokka yelping when they realised they had fallen asleep on top of each other on the bench, feeding mosquito-ants all night. Fat and Osha took turns scolding them as they rubbed strong-smelling herbal ointment over the welts, but Zuko was only half listening. He was looking at Sokka, where the glint of mischief in his eyes had chased away the last traces of his solemnity from last night as he bickered with Fat, and he was thinking about the rippling shapes that the peacock-koi had painted in the moonlit water, and the arc of stars from the northern and southern skies overhead, and the sturdy pillow that had been Sokka’s shoulder, and he thought, *if* *he were by my side like that, for all time, then—then nothing could stand in my way*.

## Chapter 5: Wet Season

### Notes

#### Chapter Notes

This chapter contains sexual content.

### Text

Now that Zuko’s brain had… *noticed* Sokka, it seemed determined to turn completely stupid. It was like his face was lingering in empty hallways, in between the bushes of the palace courtyards, in bowls of soup. At night, Zuko pressed his fingers into the fading welts the mosquito-ants left on his skin and thought about the matching set on Sokka. He would sit in Council while Councillor Ike bickered with Councillor Hara about Yee Kwan in Cranefish Town, and his mind would wander off to think about the heaviness of Sokka’s arm around him and he would register, and shake himself back to the present.

It was maddening. Zuko told himself, rationally, he was reacting to their closeness that evening, that he just hadn’t been held for too long. Sokka was an objectively good looking guy, and everyone and their mother in Shu Jing knew it too if Kriti and the deer-pig guy were anything to go by. It wasn’t as though Zuko had never developed some passing crush on a brash courtier’s kid or flitting bureaucrat. Like always, he could snuff it out before it grew too big.

The drizzles gave way to the rains of the hot-wet season, and the palace was cloaked in humidity. The Council seemed appeased that the Avatar had been contacted to address the alteration in firebending. In Cranefish Town, riots broke out between those with Earth Kingdom and Fire Nation ancestry: shops looted, homes ransacked, individuals beaten by gangs in the street. To the Zuko’s surprise, Kuei’s response was diametrically opposite to his response towards Yu Dao: the city was swiftly expelled from the Earth Kingdom.

Meanwhile, Osha was enforcing Dr Khoo’s orders. On his designated rest day, she appeared at his doorway as he settled at his desk.

“None of that today,” she tutted. “Your physician wants you to take a break.”

“It’s raining everywhere,” said Zuko. “We can’t even go out.”

“Well… you could try beating me in a fight?”

Zuko scowled up at her, then broke. “Alright. Lead the way.”

They spent the morning sparring with empty hands, then Osha on the sword with Zuko on the dual dao, then Zuko’s fire against whatever Osha could get her hands on. In the former two, Osha beat him easily, but he was gratified to see that his fire had her sweating.

“We really should train together more,” she said, frowning as he helped her up from the floor. “I’m gonna need that practice against fire if you’re fighting like that.”

It made him think back to the first time they had sparred together, when he hired her to replace Suki. Osha had fought dirty guerilla tactics that caught Zuko by surprise, but her form was impeccable, a testament to her years of rigorous martial arts training. Back then, his swordwork had been a lot better, too.

“How’s the tummy? You up for lunch?

He touched his belly. “Feeling fine at the moment. We can let the kitchens know.”

“Nah, don’t do that. Come have lunch with me and Toh-ki.”

“Are you sure? I don’t wanna intrude…”

“Nah, don’t be silly. She hasn’t seen you in a while.”

Zuko shrugged a vest on and followed Osha to her apartments. She and her wife lived in the palace, in the quarters reserved for those who worked there. The quarters were bustling with workers on lunch break, and they parted respectfully as he and Osha walked through.

Toh-ki was inside, unpacking a stack of steel lunch containers with stoic hands. She was a stocky firebender, hair bound into a tight topknot, an ever-present film of ash clinging to her sleeves: she worked in the palace smithy. “You’re back,” she said to Osha, then bowed to Zuko. “Afternoon, Your Majesty.”

“Hello, Toh-ki. I hope I’m not butting in,” he said.

“Osha mentioned you might swing by. I’ve bought enough lunch for all of us.”

They tucked in. Osha really must have planned this, since Zuko was allowed to eat most of the dishes: boiled lettuce, steamed fish, lotus roots. Osha and Toh-ki shared the spiciest dish, an otter-goose rendang, between themselves; they permitted Zuko to eat a single chunk when Osha noticed him eyeing it between bites.

“So the appetite’s coming back?” she said dryly, dropping the piece into his bowl.

“I’ll take the stomachache if it smells this good,” he said. As expected, it was delicious: sweet, spicy, and melt-in-mouth. He hadn’t realised how much he missed eating spicy food.

Osha exchanged a look with Toh-ki, shaking her head a little. Toh-ki flashed her a corresponding smile, a mere twitch of the mouth on her otherwise impassive face. The gesture made Zuko’s heart feel suddenly bereft, like a chasm had yawned open in the middle of his chest.

“I bought us dessert too,” said Toh-ki, taking out a banana-lotus leaf package of cassava cakes. Zuko welcomed the distraction, grabbing a slice to wash down with cinnamon tea.

Toh-ki made Zuko wash the dishes. He carried them out to the communal sink in a basin, trying to ignore the other residents as they gawked at the singularly uncommon sight of the Fire Lord struggling with the lye soap as his bodyguard stood by and laughed. It made him think of his days in the teashop, the comforting mundanity of it all.

“You can have the afternoon off,” he told Osha when he finished.

“Can I trust you to *not work* for the rest of the day?”

“Of course!”

“Well, what’re you going to do?”

He hadn’t thought that far yet. “I’ll read a play.”

“You’ll… read a play,” she said, eyes narrowing.

She insisted on accompanying him to the library. He had read most of the classics as a child and was thinking of rereading one, but the librarian recommended one that had come in recently from the Northern Water Tribe, so he picked that up instead.

“You’re gonna tell me the plot tomorrow. In *great detail*,” said Osha, throwing him another look over her shoulder after she dropped him at his chambers.

In the courtyard, the rain was still coming down in sheets, washing the view in grey. The pattering sound was soothing, soporific. Zuko stretched out on his couch, Uncle’s tea brewing in Toph’s cup, and read. Against his expectations, he found himself enraptured in the story: a reindeer spirit and a wind spirit who came together in the mortal realm only during springtimes, dancing around each other for thousands of years.

Before he realised it, he was blinking awake in muggy heat. The rain outside had stopped and his skin was sticking to the fabric of his clothes and the couch. He could feel his stomach pulse. The book had tumbled to the floor. He rubbed his eyes, feeling the spectacles slip further down his sweaty nose, chasing flashes of his dreams: the two spirits flitting through the melting snow… a slain reindeer, its blood a shock against the white ground… the reindeer spirit’s white furs painted red, robes of henna red… the wind spirit now a shock of blue, and when you looked into his eyes…

Zuko could blame the heat; he could blame Dr Khoo for talking about his *frustration*. He pressed a palm to his crotch, breathing through the thick air. He wasn’t fully hard yet, but he would be soon. He shrugged the vest off one shoulder, dragging his hand slowly over the lengthening hardness in his trousers. He panted noiselessly into the air. He thought of the two spirits intertwining in their dance of aeons, because he didn’t want anything else to come unbidden into his mind. The ties of his pants and undergarments were loosening in his hands and he was putting his hand inside like a thief, gasping when he took himself in hand and started to stroke. *Red on the thawing ground*, and he was biting into the heel of his other hand, trying to muffle the sounds, *a touch to the hip, a* *bump under the finger*, it was too dry, even with the sheen of sweat over his body, it was too dry, but he was afraid that if he got up to find ointment the spell would be broken, *the ocean, blue, the* *blue of a beaded earring, the blue of—*

He lay there panting, looking at the hand-carved wooden ceiling of his room, feeling a little revulsion towards himself. Then he got into the bath and washed the cum and sweat off his skin.

---

The monsoon continued to linger low over the archipelago. Zuko travelled to a refinery shrouded in umbrellas and shook hands with the workers. The poets who won one of the Grants performed a reading of one of the ghazals, something about magma. A lengthy letter flew in from his uncle. Sokka didn’t send anything, not that Zuko was expecting. In Cranefish Town, Yee Kwan took power, to nobody’s surprise. He declared the city an independent state and renamed it the Cranefish Republic. Yu Dao was faring worse. The formation of the Cranefish Republic rekindled public outrage as the residents realised their sister city had been granted the liberation so many of them had sought to no avail.

The day for Zuko’s follow-up appointment with Dr Khoo was approaching too. In some ways, he felt like he was doing better, with Osha’s strict enforcement of rest days. Now that he was looking for it, he could feel when the stress came back into his body as the foreign developments continued to unfold. There was another thing to be stressed about too.

Three mornings before his departure, he stuck his head out of the doorway to his chambers. “Oshaaaa?” he called. To one of the guards at the door, he said, “Can you find Osha?”

“What is it?” she said, rushing into his rooms moments later.

He was sitting in front of his mirror, scar freshly smeared with ointment, examining the right side of his face. “How do I get rid of all these pimples?”

He could see her eyes in the reflection boring holes into the mirror. “That’s what you called me here for? Since when did you care about your skin?”

Maybe he just wanted to look presentable and Fire Lordy. It had nothing to do with Shu Jing or any individuals who might reside there. The zits had popped up on his cheek and chin, making him look like an unkempt teenager.

Osha sighed, rolling her eyes. “The humidity makes you more oily. Keep your face clean and stop *touching*.” She smacked the hand that was creeping up to his face. “I’ll find you a cream.”

Zuko applied the cream religiously for the next few days, and then the typhoon hit.

A clatter of noise outside his bedroom woke him. He sprang upright. People in the courtyard, too loud to be sneaking around. A distant howling of wind. It was not morning yet. He pulled a robe on and opened his door, flame in hand.

“Your Majesty, sorry to wake you, but we have to secure your chambers,” said the intruder—a maintenance worker, and behind him was a team of workers pulling down the blinds around the courtyard.

“Secure? Is something…”

“Typhoon coming. Looks like a big one. You can hear the wind, right? Just go back to sleep. You’ll be safe.”

Zuko got back into bed. The shutters came down, the workers left, and the wind bristled against the bamboo. He thought about the trip in the morning, and he felt the pain curl into his stomach.

---

In the morning, Zuko tried very hard not to sulk, and failed.

“There’s just no way they can fly in this weather, boss,” said Osha. “I know you’re disappointed —”

*Disappointed*? No, Zuko wasn’t that—

“—you’ve been making some progress and who knows when Dr Khoo can fit you in again?”

Right. Dr Khoo, the whole point of the trip. Zuko flopped back into his bed, still in the travelling clothes he had put on with some ill-considered optimism. Outside, the rain lashed the shutters.

“Do you need to tell her that I’m not coming? And, um, Master Piandao?”

“We can’t get a hawk out in this weather. They’ll figure it out. It’s a big storm.”

“I’ll just have two days of leave stuck here,” he complained into his bedcovers. The councillors had alternative activities planned already, but in this weather who knew whether they could get anything done.

“Maybe you can read another play?” said Osha on her way out.

Zuko flopped onto his other side, trying to chase away the irrational dismay rising in his throat. He saw the little clay dragon lamp, still sitting beside his bed. Maybe it was a good thing he couldn’t go this time; maybe he could get over his momentary stupidity and be normal again. In front of… well, no one in particular.

---

Sometime in the afternoon, a knock sounded at Zuko’s door.

“Your Majesty?” Zuko looked up to see Osha, a smile playing on her lips. “Someone’s here for you.”

He could still hear the wind battering against the shutters. *Could it be…? But how?* He got up and followed her to the reception hall, heart thudding against his chest.

“There’s my favourite great-grandson!” cried a blithe tenor as he stepped into the room, and Zuko found himself caught in the Avatar’s unnecessarily long limbs.

“Do *not* call me that,” said Zuko without heat. He turned to give Aang a proper hug. “Aang. How did you get here?”

It really was him, breezy yellow robes a vibrant splash against the dreary day. Appa had managed to fit into the reception hall too, looking surprisingly dry. The palace staff were eyeing him with trepidation. “Well, when it’s windy and rainy and you can bend both air and water…” said Aang, shrugging. It looked as though the palace staff had given him a cup of mangosteenana juice, and he took an enthusiastic slurp. Momo peered out from behind his shoulder to eye a tray of kueh dadar, swiping one before flying off to perch on a ceiling beam. “Also, I stopped a neighbourhood from flooding down at the harbour, so you’re welcome.”

“It’s good to see you again,” said Zuko. He wasn’t disappointed at all, he wasn’t. In fact, Aang’s sunniness might be the antidote to his drudgery. “Where’s Katara?”

“Oh, she’s attending a Water Tribe sibling reunion at Piandao’s,” said Aang.

“You were at Piandao’s? Are you taking me there?”

“No, why? Didn’t you want me to deal with the firebending crisis?”

“The Council prefers *alteration* to *cri*—”

“Come on, no time like the present!”

Aang drained his juice with a loud slurp, smacking his lips. Zuko hesitated. He was meant to be in Shu Jing, wasn’t he? But Aang was here now, and there was a typhoon, and he had nothing else to do on leave… “What did you have in mind?” he said.

“We go look for the, uh”—Aang cast his eyes over the assembled servants and Osha, lowering his voice to a whisper—“*Sky Fireball Fighters*, again. And their, uh, *fire noodles*.”

Zuko looked at him, nonplussed.

“Well?” said Aang. “Are you coming?” He airbended himself onto Appa, taking the reins.

Zuko was still in his travelling clothes. What did he have to lose?

---

It was almost nostalgic being back in Appa’s saddle. Aang created an impervious sphere of air around them and they jostled through the downpour before bursting high up above the cloud cover. Momo clung to Zuko’s clothes, chittering. There was a rumbling darkness as Aang strained, then light: the endless blue of the stratosphere and below, the terrible beauty of the swirling typhoon, its eye hovering somewhere over the sea to the west of the main island.

“Alright! Clear skies ahead,” Aang said, releasing the sphere. The sun had long passed its zenith, settling to their left as Appa veered northwards.

“It’s funny you were in Shu Jing,” said Zuko. “I was meant to go today.”

“Oh yeah, Sokka mentioned. But I knew you wouldn’t make it.” Aang waggled his fingers at the typhoon below. He had developed the ability to tell the weather, something to do with atmospheric pressure. “So, you’ve got me instead. He says you’ve been around a bit?”

Zuko rubbed the back of his head. “I guess.”

“I would’ve brought Katara, but the Sun Warriors wouldn’t let her in. Anyway, after everything that happened in Ba Sing Se, I guess he’d want to have some. Y’know. *Sibling time*.”

“What about Ba Sing Se?”

“Sokka didn’t mention? Ah, damn. Sorry. Forget I said anything,” said Aang. “Uh… I was in Cranefish earlier?”

What happened in Ba Sing Se? What did Sokka not mention? Distantly, Zuko remembered the flash of a knife, a braided queue coming loose into his hand. Sokka’s voice echoing in his memory, *…suffering and anguish… self-inflicted…*

“Tricky state of affairs in your former colonies,” said Aang, interrupting his thoughts, sounding eager to change the topic. “It’s like all the old agreements we signed have fallen apart.”

“Perhaps too much good faith and not enough wisdom were put into those,” Zuko said dully. He put his thoughts of Sokka aside, something he felt he was becoming quite adept at these days. “You think the Sun Warriors will really help?”

Aang shrugged. “Maybe we missed something. I got a good feeling.”

Zuko watched as they left the last of the clouds behind and the undulating ocean appeared below, an unfurling steely blue.

“I don’t envy the Yu Dao stuff,” said Aang eventually. “I’ve been working on a few projects, myself. Collecting some of the more intact artefacts from the Air Temples... I suppose I should tell you now, actually.” He looked over his shoulder at Zuko, a little sly, a little embarrassed. “Katara and I are trying for a kid.”

“Oh. Wow.” Sokka’s voice again: *Do you ever think they got married a bit young?* Four years Aang’s senior at twenty-five, Zuko—an heirless head of state—was nowhere near childrearing himself. “Um. Congrats?”

“I know!” trilled Aang. “I can’t wait to have my own little airbenders.”

He continued nattering on, talking about how they hoped to settle down a little, maybe even somewhere near the former colonies. If Aang and Katara had an airbender, Zuko realised, that child would be the first one born in over a hundred years. He settled back in the saddle and tried to picture a world with more than one airbender: the airspace teeming with sky bison, children swooping on gliders? What could it look like?

---

They came across a cluster of unpopulated coral islands ringed in turquoise. They had to be close to the Sun Warriors but the sunset was already setting the sky aflame; Appa landed on an atoll and they set up camp along a beach. Aang, who was not a talented sandbender, mixed seawater with the sand to bend them a passable shelter. It was strange to be camping again. They had travelled north enough for the night to be chilly, so he and Aang lit a fire and shared the food Osha had packed when she relented to letting Zuko travel without her.

They set off again after first light, Appa rumbling blearily as they skimmed over the water. Hazy shapes hovered at the horizon, hinting that they were nearing land.

“Do you see that?” said Aang, pointing.

Somewhere to the east, a plume of white smoke was twisting high into the air. Zuko squinted, making out the dark shape of an island.

“A volcano? A fire? Is this island populated?”

“Aang, that’s obviously not a volcano.”

“I can’t tell, can I? Should we take a look?”

“The Sun Warriors, though—”

“We’re not far! Won’t take long!”

It was Aang’s Avatarish propensity to sense anomalies and try to fix them; far be it from Zuko to try to redirect him. Appa swooped over. It was a forested island and the smoke was billowing from its northeast. They landed on a sandy strip and waded through the plush dunes to the tree cover.

“This is a rainforest,” said Aang. “It shouldn’t even be burning. Look.” He bended a large sphere of water out of thin air, then let the water disperse into a mist. “It’s too damp here for anything to catch on fire.”

A rainforest, this far north? “You want to go in?” said Zuko, peering into the dark forest. “What if we get lost?”

“Nah, we won’t. I can get us out with earthbending.”

As it turned out, they had to use earthbending to get *in* as well. The undergrowth was too thick to walk through; Aang raised stepping stones of earth in the direction of the smoke, which they could barely see from under the canopy. The forest was loud with the sound of animals. A cool damp clung to their skin as they hurried through, brushing aside the vines and fronds and branches in their way. They slowed their pace when the chittering and twittering died away, trying to sense the fire.

“There!” cried Aang, pointing to their left. Sure enough, there was a telltale crackle and an orange glow came into view. They felt the press of heat as they neared. But the flames weren’t moving at all, not licking onto any other stray branches—just blazing in place.

“Let’s try to bring it down,” said Zuko, frowning. Together, they stretched out their hands, finding the connection to the flame and trying to reduce it.

“It’s not working,” said Aang. “I can’t manipulate it.”

Zuko went first, leaping from mound to mound of lifted soil. The glow became a glare and the flames rose before him, a wall of roaring red that consumed the trees in front of him. It was hot, like the sun that flared from his core.

“Zuko! Don’t get too close.”

“Aang,” he breathed. “It’s not burning *up*.”

Zuko stretched his fingers out, so close to the flame. The image of the leaves within wavered in the flickering heat but he could see enough, enough to know— “They’re green! The leaves are still green.” A fire that could not be controlled by a firebender, a fire that did not destroy?

Aang came up behind him, panting. He cast a nervous look at the flames before them, the red reflected in his wide eyes. “What? A spiritual flame?”

The roar of the fire was so loud they almost missed the chirrup beside them. They whirled around. There, coiled around a branch, was a scaly rust-red body, the head dipping down towards them, flat tongue flickering in the air.

It was a little dragon.

## Chapter 6: Druk

In a sinuous movement, the dragon dipped its head level to Zuko and Aang. The air in Zuko’s lungs felt frozen. He had seen Ran and Shaw, not to mention countless paintings and sculptures of dragons, but he had never seen one like this: small and wingless, the size of a koala-sheep, bony nubs in the place of antlers. Its fierce yellow eye observed them, shimmering in the firelight.

“A juvenile,” Zuko murmured. “Ran and Shaw are no longer the last of the dragons.”

The dragon’s tongue flickered again, tasting the air. Tasting him. Zuko let his mouth fall open and felt the fire leaping up in his throat.

“Careful,” called Aang. His voice was drowning under the roar of the fire.

“Who are you?” said Zuko. The dragon cocked its head. Instinct over reason; Zuko put out his hand again, into the fire.

“*Zuko*!”

He sucked in a searing breath through his teeth. It was so hot, hot enough for his flesh to fry and crisp away, hot like the fire pressed against his eye so many years ago, a transcendental heat—but he did not burn. He flexed his fingers in the fire. The dragon was looking at him, yellow eyes unblinking. Zuko took a deep breath and reached for his inner flame, the sun inside himself; he felt it radiate outwards, travelling into his fist, which burst open. *There*, the connection he had sought, flaring to life. Flames of colour spurted where his energy met the fire, just like the technicolour dragonfire Ran and Shaw had breathed on them a lifetime ago. He had never bended colourful flame before. He felt it deep in his core, more powerful than the pull of the sun at dawn, like a third eye bursting open to gaze upon the world anew. It was exhilarating.

Then the dragon turned to the wall of flame as well and sort of—*sucked* the flames back into its mouth. Like a cloak, the fire fell from the trees and tunnelled into its mouth. When the last of the flames slid from Zuko’s hand and swirled into its mouth, it gave a fiery colourful burp. The vision revived the flash of inspiration Zuko had felt seeing that fire for the first time, the vortex of swirling heat and colour and life.

“You bended *colourful* fire.” Aang skipped up to him, grabbing his hand and turning it over. Completely unblemished. “How did that happen?”

Zuko brushed him off. He made the sign of the flame, bowing low to the dragon. “I am Fire Lord Zuko and this is Avatar Aang. It is an honour to meet you.”

The dragon fixed him with its full gaze. Its head swayed forwards; Zuko could see that its rusty colour came from the uneven tone of its scales, mostly brown with patches of red. The tongue flickered out again, almost touching his nose. Aang was still half a step behind him, watching with wide eyes.

The last time Zuko had come face to face with a dragon, its whole head had been the size of his body and he had been terrified out of his mind. But this little one, whose flame he had felt that connection with… Unbidden, he raised a hand, presenting the backs of his fingers for the dragon to sniff.

“*Oh*,” said Aang, and the dragon was surging forwards, rubbing the side of its jaw along Zuko’s hand. A giggle spilled out of Zuko’s mouth. The dragon rubbed again; its scales were dry and smooth, river pebbles worn by running water.

“Hello,” said Zuko. “Hello, you.” In wonder, he scratched the dragon’s beardless chin and its yellow eyes fluttered half-closed, tongue flickering.

“It *likes* you,” said Aang. “I wonder how it got here.”

The dragon’s coiled body slid along the branch as it slithered forwards, then it was holding on with its hind legs as it wrapped itself across Zuko’s shoulders. He gasped at the warm weight distributing itself over him as it rumbled, stroking down the length of its body.

“Can I touch?” Aang held out a nervous hand for the dragon to sniff. It snapped a little and Aang recoiled.

“Hey,” Zuko said, a gentle admonishment. “Be nice to him.”

Aang reached again. This time it assented. His hand joined Zuko, scratching behind the long ear. “Wow, I never knew dragons could be so cuddly.”

The dragon swivelled its head back around to look at Zuko. He felt himself smiling, blowing a puff of flame at it; it blinked and shook its head, then with a snap of the mouth let out a small flame.

“I almost forgot how beautiful dragonfire was,” he whispered.

“What should we do?” said Aang. “We need to keep going to the Sun Warriors. They’re not far. They’d probably know what to do about the dragon.”

Zuko sighed, rubbing his hands along the dragon’s tail. “Can it come with us?”

“That’s probably up to the dragon,” said Aang. “We can come back for it.”

But when Zuko tried lifting it off, the dragon clung on, body coiling tighter and claws digging into his skin. Even when they made their way back along Aang’s path, it didn’t let go.

“I think it wants to come,” said Zuko.

“We could probably ask the Sun Warriors about it.”

They retraced their path through the forest. The dragon clung on the whole time, apparently happy to hitch a ride on Zuko. They emerged from the dark canopy blinking onto the sun-soaked beach where Aang’s pets were lolling. The sight of the dragon made Momo screech, leaping high into the air in a spray of sand and alighting warily on top of Aang’s head.

“We’ve got more passengers!” Aang told them. “Play nice!”

They climbed the air; Momo decided it was safer to stay perched on Aang and sent wide-eyed looks of betrayal back to Zuko. The dragon rumbled beside Zuko’s ear, tongue flickering with curiosity.

Zuko tickled it under its chin. “When you grow your wings, you can fly like this too.”

---

The chief greeted them when they arrived at the Sun Warriors’ city, headdress askew, huffing his way down the avenue.

“Druk!” he cried.

Aang and Zuko exchanged looks. “Um… hi?” said Aang.

The chief reached them and lunged straight for the dragon, picking it up by the armpits. The tail, however, was twisted around Zuko’s shoulder and it wouldn’t let go. “You brought him back!” said the chief. “Come on, Druk, you’re home now. Let go of the Fire Lord.”

“Oh I don’t mind,” said Zuko, while Aang said, “The dragon’s yours?”

“Yes, he’s the progeny of Ran and Shaw.” The chief abandoned his struggle against the juvenile dragon and stepped back, casting a stern look at him. A couple of his attendants were catching up to flank him. “He escaped about a week ago.”

“Ran and Shaw had a baby?”

“I believe you were acquainted with Druk’s egg,” said the chief.

Aang snorted. “No way. The glowing orb you touched, Zuko…”

“And you kept scolding me. Oh… *Druk*…” Zuko scratched the dragon’s little head, delighting as Druk butted his head into the hand.

“You got us covered in slime, I think I had a right.” Aang crossed his arms, grinning over at them in spite of himself.

The chief invited them to lunch with the clan, feeding them flatbread and juicy roast weasel-hog. Druk’s weight on his shoulders seemed to ease the discomfort in Zuko’s stomach and the dragon happily snapped up the bites of meat Zuko fed him.

“Druk seems fond of you,” said the chief.

“Oh.” Zuko ducked his head a little. “He’s pretty cute. It’s pretty amazing to be this close to a dragon. One of the *last* dragons.”

“So, are the Avatar and the Fire Lord here to return the dragon, or do you have business with the Sun Warriors again?”

Aang looked expectantly at Zuko. Business? Oh. “We wanted to ask more about the… alterations that have been happening in firebending ability across the Fire Nation,” said Zuko. “We’ve tried a number of techniques to promote the formation of a sustainable source of firebending, but the results… They haven’t been consistent.” He met Aang’s eye. “Not like when the masters showed us their flame.”

The chief nodded sagely, running a finger through his goatee. It was streaked with grey, Zuko noticed; had it been like that when he and Aang came here that first time, all those years ago? “Nothing is a more reliable teacher than the vortex of dragonfire that the masters produce. But of course, it is not always necessary. Your ancestors”—he looked meaningfully at Zuko—“before the war were able to reproduce and transmit the original form of firebending successfully without undergoing the ritual both of you performed, though it was a longer and more complex process.”

“What was that?”

“Simply put, many firebenders studied *with* the dragons and taught others what they learnt. Not every firebender would have seen a dragon, but they would have learnt the techniques that their predecessors learnt directly from the dragons.”

That made sense. Toph had learnt her earthbending directly from the badgermoles, though not every earthbender had this opportunity. Then, she had passed on those very techniques to Aang. “You’re saying we have to stay and properly learn these techniques from the masters, from scratch, to effectively teach them?”

“That is one solution,” said the chief, “but I believe another has presented itself.” He raised his eyebrows at Druk, still curled around Zuko’s shoulders.

“Druk?”

“He seems to have chosen you, Your Majesty.” The chief smiled, a little bittersweet. “Before your ancestors slew dragons, they reared them. Tell me, Fire Lord, how did you form the connection with him?”

“Connection?” said Aang.

“When we found him, he had created this forest fire, in the middle of a rainforest,” said Zuko. “We tried to bring it down but it was impossible to bend. It was almost like instinct… I just put my hand into it. I knew… I knew it wouldn’t burn me. It didn’t burn the trees. And I reached inside for my inner flame and made the connection. And… coloured flames came out of my hand. Just like the masters had showed us.”

“You have proven yourself to the dragon,” said the chief gravely. “Connection to a dragon’s flame and the ability to bend it is an intimate thing.”

The revelation lit a warmth in Zuko’s chest. “Me? You want to be with me?” Zuko looked between Aang and the chief, and down at Druk. “What about… what about Ran and Shaw?”

“He should say goodbye, I suppose.”

“No, I mean… Shouldn’t he be here, at home? With the other dragons?” But hope was blooming in Zuko’s chest and the petals were suffusing in his lungs; he brushed his fingers against the precious, smooth scales and he hoped, and he hoped.

“If it is Druk’s choice,” said the chief, “then it may be time that the dragons returned to the Fire Nation.”

So when Zuko climbed back into Appa’s saddle, it was with his new animal companion in tow. Druk had bid Ran and Shaw a long goodbye, blowing colourful sparks and puffs of smoke. On his knees, prostrate on the platform between the dragons’ caves, Zuko had sworn to protect and cherish his dragon. Now, they were watching the stone city grow smaller as Appa soared high into the air, the mountains spearing up on either side of them, and then the mountains too were shrinking in their wake and then the path forwards was carpeted in water again.

---

The sun was on the brink of the horizon when they caught the first glimpses of the Fire Nation archipelago.

“Are we going to make it before nightfall?” said Zuko. The typhoon had long dissipated but the clouds were still hanging low, muggy and oppressive.

“I hope so,” said Aang through gritted teeth. “Appa’s getting tired but there really isn’t a better place to land before we get you home.”

The night was catching up as they raced over the sea, indigo chasing pink, the moon rising high to the east. Appa crested over the mountains just as the last glimmer of sun slipped beneath the ocean, then they saw a patch of twinkling firelight in the dark.

“City lights,” said Aang; Zuko could hear the grin in his voice. Appa began his descent. Beneath them, the lights sketched the grid of the city.

“There,” said Zuko, pointing with a flame on the end of his finger. Appa circled the air above the main palace courtyard, then landed. Zuko unpopped the pressure in his ears, climbing down with Druk clinging to his shoulders; in front, Aang collapsed theatrically from his seat and airbended himself a soft landing before slumping to the pavement.

“Your Majesty! The Avatar!” came the shout of a passing servant. Zuko could hear more feet pattering into the courtyard, before they stopped abruptly as Druk released a kaleidoscope of fire into the night with a snap of his jaw.

“A *dragon*?” someone breathed, then all around people were falling to their knees, shouts filling the air.

“A dragon!”

“Is this real? A real dragon?”

“The dragons have returned!”

“The Fire Lord and the Avatar have brought the dragons back to the Fire Nation!”

Aang got to his feet slowly, looking at the gathered crowd in wonder. “This doesn’t happen every day, right?”

“No,” said Zuko, but he was grinning. He stood tall, gripping the body of the dragon draped across his shoulders. “No, it doesn’t.”

---

Aang left the next morning, heading back to Shu Jing to pick Katara up before going up to the Western Air Temple. Zuko had half a mind to follow him there, approved leave be damned, but the typhoon had left a mammoth cleanup job for him and the residents of the nation’s capital.

Oh, and it turned out his new pet dragon was in the interest of every single one of his councillors.

“I believe Druk must be a part of the solution to the alterations in firebending,” said Zuko to his councillors after a few days spent stomping around in muck. “Our ancestors learnt from the dragons. The return of the dragons is a serendipitous sign from the spirits, and we cannot squander the second chance they have given us.”

Now that the dragons’ return from extinction was public knowledge, the Council passed in record time the legislation to outlaw their slaughter. Zuko found himself a few days later accompanying Councillor Ike to the dojo of one Master Kurin. Druk had a new leash, though he wasn’t quite adept at walking on it and preferred to ride on Zuko’s shoulders. Around new faces, the dragon proved suspicious, preferring to hide behind Zuko hissing before accepting there was no threat; at the dojo it was no different. They spent a long afternoon with Master Kurin as Zuko coaxed Druk into breathing flames, while she studied his movements and translated them as best as she could into human movements.

“Good boy!” said Zuko when they finished. He rubbed Druk’s neck as he twisted happily, feeding him a jerky treat that had been stashed in his loose training robe. “Aren’t you such a clever boy?” “What did you think, Master?” Councillor Ike came up to them, arms folded behind her back and giving Druk wide berth. “Is it a viable plan?”

Master Kurin took a long gulp of water, wiping the gathered sweat at her brow. “I can feel his raw power,” she said. “I have never seen anything like it. Even now, there is much I can learn from him. Those coloured flames… Imagine what he will be like when he grows his horn, his beard, his wings.” She made the sign of the flame and bowed. “Your Majesty, thank you for sharing Druk’s bending with me.”

“It was a pleasure, master.”

She gave him a quick nod. “I will attempt the new techniques with a pupil and report back. The masters across the archipelago will be very interested.”

---

“Look sharp,” Osha muttered into his ear that evening. “Dr Khoo said she had a cancellation. You can go to Shu Jing the day after tomorrow.”

Zuko’s pulse leapt traitorously at the prospect of going back. “Will the Council allow it? Druk being here is kicking a lot of things into gear.”

“I think it’s important you have that follow-up appointment. Don’t you want to go?”

Didn’t he? He did want, but he didn’t *want* to want. It was a tricky affair.

---

The airship company had no policy against dragons on board, and in combination with Zuko’s most imperious Fire Lord voice the air hosts relented to him bringing Druk on board. Osha had found a rattan basket that belonged to Toh-ki’s brother’s neighbour’s late ferret-retriever; they sprayed it with flame retardant and put the juvenile dragon inside.

“He’s not gonna stay this small forever,” Osha grumbled as they took their seats. “You can’t just keep bullying the staff into letting you bring a dragon on board.”

“When he’s bigger he can fly *me* around,” said Zuko. “Won’t you, boy? Won’t you?”

Osha turned her back to him, grumbling something that sounded like *get him a pet* and *loopy*. Zuko ignored her. He had better things to do, like teaching Druk how to do a paw trick.

---

“My goodness,” said Dr Khoo. “The dragon.”

“I hope you don’t mind.” Zuko shuffled inside, tucking Druk’s basket between himself and the wall. “He’s a baby, I didn’t want to leave him alone at the palace.”

“A baby.” Dr Khoo eyed the coiled length of Druk stacked inside.

“Would you mind if I took him out? He likes sitting on me, he won’t knock everything over.”

“I… suppose.” Dr Khoo leaned closer, adjusting her spectacles to peer at Druk as he scrambled up Zuko’s side. “Well, well, well. I never thought, in my lifetime!”

“Isn’t he sweet?”

“Sweet, hmm. It appears the dragon’s appearance in your life has been a positive force, Your Majesty.”

“Yeah.” Druk made his way onto his customary perch on Zuko’s shoulders, tail swishing. He fixed his big eyes on the physician. Zuko reached up to scratch behind his ears. “We had this… connection, when I saw him on the island.”

“Do you feel his presence has improved your stress levels?”

“I guess so. I was really worried about the firebending business, but Druk being here means we have a real chance of learning from a dragon again.” Druk flickered his tongue out, swiping Zuko’s cheek. “It’s amazing having a real dragon as a pet too. Just like Avatar Roku… I never imagined this would happen.”

“Certainly, Your Majesty. Have you cared for an animal before?”

Zuko shook his head.

“Some people find animals quite therapeutic,” said Dr Khoo. “They provide a very different kind of companionship to the one found with other people. An animal is totally reliant on you, and the bond you have with it is one of absolute trust.” Zuko thought of Aang with Appa and Momo, the unique kind of friendship he seemed to have with those animals. “You have been taking your breaks too?”

“Well, Osha’s been making me.”

Dr Khoo looked at him over her spectacles. “I find with some of my patients who present similarly to you, Your Majesty, they take a little time to get used to taking proper rest. It takes a bit of practice. How about the digestion?”

“On and off again. More off than it has been on, lately.”

“Promising, but we can do better. Can you get onto the bed again?”

Druk ended up sitting on the mattress beside Zuko’s cheek, looking warily at Dr Khoo as she approached. “It’s alright, Druk. Shh,” Zuko whispered, scratching his chest.

“My, he is clingy isn’t he?” Dr Khoo put warm, gloved hands over his abdomen, following the threads of energy inside his body. He felt the chi move inside him beneath her fingers.

“Is there anything you can do about the… my face?” he asked when she was done.

“Ah yes.” She moved up, dispersing the heat gathering at the spots on his cheek and chin. “Very common for acne to spring up during times of stress and humidity. They should be much reduced in an hour or so. You can get up now.”

As Zuko got back to his feet, she pulled a jar of pills from the shelf. “I procured these from the Earth Kingdom. There is a new ingredient that neutralises stomach acid. This may alleviate some of the symptoms while you work on managing your stress, and after a month or so you may try eating as you please. Can I ask you something, Your Majesty?”

“Of course.”

“Do you want to get better?”

Zuko reeled back. “What? Of course I do.”

“I can give you all the treatments you need, I can tell you what to do, but with matters of the heart I need your active participation in changing the way you think. The way you approach your life.” She looked at him over her spectacles again, which Zuko was coming to find sharp and disconcerting. “It is common for us to become stuck in our ways, to simply accept the state in which we find ourselves—even unconsciously.”

Hadn’t he lived with the pains for months before his mother made him see a physician? Zuko looked at the floor and Druk, perhaps sensing his shame, let out a reassuring puff of smoke.

“I want you to consider this, Your Majesty. Not just whether you get better, but where you might want the end of your treatment to take you.” Then she was looking away, flurrying through papers on her desk, scribbling out another note. “I will see you again… Well, I suppose it will be in the new year. You can confer with the receptionist.”

“Wait,” said Zuko. “Can I ask you something? About yourself?”

“Ask away, Your Majesty. I will answer if I can.”

“Your bending… Did it ever alter?”

She smiled faintly as she handed him the slips of paper. “I think you know the answer to that. The medicine we practise in the Fire Nation was derived from firebending. *Firebenders* discovered the concepts of hot and cold energy. My bending was never used for war.”

“Firebending to heal.”

“Exactly. To make living better.” She nodded towards the door. “Good health to you, Your Majesty.”

---

It was raining when they left the practice, Druk stowed back into his basket and pills tucked away in Osha’s satchel. Fat droplets spilled over the eaves, gathering into the drains on the street, kicking up the scent of the rain on warm earth.

“Damn monsoon,” Osha said. “I have an umbrella somewhere. Do we want to brave it?”

“Hey!” cried Sokka’s voice out of nowhere and there was a splash, a whirl of colour. A motorised tuk-tuk pulled up in front of them and a hand pulled the rain cover aside to reveal Sokka’s grinning face. “Piandao told me to find you guys. Hop on.”

Zuko met his eye and looked away hastily. *Be normal*, he told himself. He busied himself with bundling Druk and his belongings beside Sokka. Osha took the seat beside the driver and they trundled off down the narrow street through the rain.

“Uh, hi,” said Zuko. He resolutely batted away any thoughts about any Northern Water Tribe plays. “You came to find us.”

“This the little fella?” said Sokka, gesturing at Druk’s basket. In his other hand was a half-eaten meat bun, which he took a bite out of.

“Yeah. I’ll show you when we get there.”

The tuk-tuk scaled the hill to Piandao’s estate; Zuko kept his hood low over his face as Sokka paid. Fat, waiting with a large umbrella, ushered them inside.

“This infernal weather,” said Piandao mildly over his teacup as they tumbled indoors. “This is the dragon?”

“This is Druk,” said Zuko. He hoisted Druk from the basket, tail and hind legs dangling, and let him scamper to his usual spot on his shoulders.

“Truly a wonder,” said Piandao. “The Avatar told us all about how you found him.”

Zuko caught Sokka’s eye again; he caught the faint wonder in his expression. When did it become so hard to look him in the face? Like staring into the sun. He grabbed a teacup and swallowed against the sensation building in his chest.

---

Before bed, Zuko tucked Druk into his basket as he was starting to do each night, scratching behind his ears and rubbing his back through his blanket to settle him down. Outside, the rain continued; Zuko could hear it spilling into the courtyard in films of liquid.

“Who’s my little lizard? Who’s baba’s best boy?” he cooed. Druk burrowed into his blanket and blew contented rings of smoke through his nostrils. He was cutest when he was sleepy, warm and affectionate. “Are you gonna sleep well tonight? Or are you gonna wake me up—”

“Am I interrupting?”

“Sokka!” Zuko straightened.

Sokka edged inside and plonked himself down on the edge of Zuko’s bed. “Never thought I’d see you this mushy, Your Flameyness.” He pointed an accusatory finger at Druk. “You broke him, little guy. I want grumpy awkward Zuko back.”

“Hey,” protested Zuko, sounding a little weak even to his own ears.

“Shrimp cracker?” Sokka held out a little paper cone.

Zuko probably shouldn’t be eating them but he took one anyway, savouring the umami crisp on his tongue.

“Can I pet him?” said Sokka. “He’s not gonna bite off my fingers, right?”

“Just be gentle. Let him sniff you out.”

Zuko moved aside to let Sokka lean down, holding a cracker to his snout as a peace offering. “Hey buddy,” he murmured. “You been keeping our Fire Lord company?” Druk snapped up the treat and let him rub his neck.

“He likes getting petted under the chin.”

Sokka’s long fingers moved there and Druk’s eyes fluttered shut, a little rumble sounding from his belly.

“Cute.” He adjusted the blanket around Druk as he burrowed in. “Is he a reptile? Does he get cold when the sun isn’t out?”

“I… never really thought about that.” Zuko reached out to touch Druk around the middle, filching a cracker with his other hand. “He feels warm. I assume he’s like me and Aang; he has the sun inside himself.”

“Any funky dragon tricks he can do?”

“Yeah, actually. When we found him, he made a fire that didn’t burn anything, and when I connected to it I could bend colourful fire. Just like the dragons. He can suck a fire back into himself too. I’ve been trying it…” Zuko let a small stream of fire out of his mouth and inhaled; some of it hit the back of his throat, some of it dissipated. He coughed. “More practice needed, I guess.”

“Sick.”

“How’s…” He remembered what Sokka had said last time, about the rain. “Uh, how’s your leg?”

“Hmm? Just dealing with it.” Sokka gave him a tight smile. “The footwork has helped strengthen the muscles there.”

“Right.”

“You know what really sucks about this place, though?”

“What?”

“You guys use *hard* pillows,” said Sokka. He flicked the porcelain block at the head of Zuko’s bed. “I asked Fat if they have any soft ones and he gave me a seat cushion. And *that* was hard too.”

“I have soft pillows. They’re just too hot to use most of the time.”

“See! You subscribe to their propaganda too.” Sokka pointed a quivering finger at him. “Porcelain, more like porce-*layin’* on a block of ice, if you ask me.”

Zuko pursed his lips. “Get it?” said Sokka. “Like porcelain, but—”

“*Stoooop*,” said Zuko, pushing his knee.

They went back to petting Druk and Zuko watched Sokka out of the corner of his eye; it was easier to trace his features when he wasn’t looking directly back. “You didn’t send me a letter.”

“What’s stopping you from sending *me* one?” Sokka retorted. “Hawky’s been on a trip to the South Pole. I suspect he’s taking the long way back.”

“You wrote to your father?”

Sokka’s hand stilled on Druk. “It was about time.”

“How’s the South going?”

“Fine, I expect. Building things bigger and better than before. Should probably visit.”

“And you saw Katara again.”

“Yup. All grown up.”

They were both staring at Druk, Zuko realised, hands stilled over the slumbering dragon. “Aang said they were gonna have babies.”

“Haven’t you heard, Zuko?” said Sokka tartly. “That’s usually why people get married.”

“Technically, I don’t have to,” said Zuko, then regretted it. Sokka quirked his eyebrows. “I was thinking of having a child by surrogacy. My heir is meant to be of my blood.”

“Sorry, hang on. How did we get from that to surrogacy?”

“Um.” Zuko lapsed into an embarrassed silence. He didn’t want to admit to Sokka that he had been tragically single since Mai dumped him, that he couldn’t quite work out where Zuko started and the Fire Lord ended when it came to his sad attempts at dating after that. Surrogacy was the uneasy compromise he had made with the Council.

“Riiiiiiight. Whatever floats your boat,” said Sokka. “I’m sure there’s a zillion loyal citizens that’d let you wife them up and give them little fire babies, is what I’m saying.”

Zuko shrugged, feeling his face heat up. “I just… I can’t shake the feeling people don’t want to date me for—me.” What he didn’t say as well was, *I’m afraid if someone touched me like they* *meant it, I would burst. I would tell them everything about myself, especially the ugliest parts. And* *then…*

He could feel Sokka’s eyes on the side of his face, heavy with the unspoken. *Ba Sing Se*, he thought suddenly. “I know what you mean,” Sokka said eventually. Then, stuffing a cracker into his mouth with some forced jollity, “You reckon they’d let a dragon become your heir? I mean, Druk is pretty irresistible…”

## Chapter 7: Tail End

Before Zuko knew it, the twelfth month and its flurry of accompanying activity were dawning over the palace. The Councillors were wrapping up their portfolios for the end of the year in anticipation of the holidays to come over the New Year. Dr Khoo’s words echoed in his mind whenever he took his pills. He told himself he wanted to get better; he *would* get better, even if he didn’t always believe it.
