So he let the Council discuss the appointment of a Grand Chancellor, someone who might take over Zuko’s duties when he was on leave while he lacked a regent.

Druk made it easier too. The dragon was becoming less shy, taking to exploring Zuko’s private courtyard by himself, catching large bugs to eat and scaring the turtleducks. He shed once, leaving translucent sheets of scale around the palace, and some of his brown scales turned red. He was better on the leash too; Zuko took him on walks around the palace grounds, sending startled servants and workers scattering before the excitable juvenile dragon. Druk was even becoming adept at walking to Master Kurin’s dojo for their weekly sessions, his talons clattering on the cobblestones past the throng of prying journalists sent by the Yitpao.

The Council welcomed Master Kurin and two of her disciples to give their testimony about learning from the dragon.

“Your Majesty, this one would be honoured to demonstrate the new capabilities of myself and my disciples.” Master Kurin offered a quick, martial bow and directed her students to take their stances: one an older man with a wispy greying beard, the other a man who looked to be in his forties.

“Before his retirement, Parvin was a captain in the Fire Nation navy,” said the Master, circling the old man. “A talented firebender, of course. Yet he sought me out five years ago, his flame barely a puff. A similar story with Tiong-lee.” Now she walked behind the younger man, hands clasping behind her back. “A metalworker from the north of the city, who smelted our artillery during the war. But when he came to me, he could barely melt candle wax, let alone metal.

“For years I have stoked their flame to give some semblance of the power they once had. But with only a few weeks of the dragon’s instruction… Parvin, please.”

The older man took a low stance then, with a sinuous ripple that looked like Druk twisting, he punched and gave a reedy kiai—a fireball suffused from his fist, puffing across the hall before it dissipated. The councillors murmured among themselves. Druk, watching the fire, snapped a happy little flame in response.

“Impressive progress,” said Master Kurin. “Parvin is an older man but he has produced an incredible amount of fire for his age, especially given the state of his bending just weeks prior. Now his companion will demonstrate.”

Tiong-lee broke into a low-stanced, sinuous kata, undulating the way Druk’s spine did when Zuko took him out for walks. He made a lash of fire, making Master Kurin jump back, and breathed a thin jet from his mouth. “I couldn’t even breathe fire before!” he blurted.

Zuko shook his head in wonder, casting Druk a fond look. “You made this happen,” he whispered.

Councillor Ike rushed out to congratulate the group. “You have done promising work here, Master.”

Even Councillor Kang seemed more optimistic than usual. “Druk has proved to be an auspicious portent, Your Majesty. Might I remind everyone that the coming year will be the Year of the Dragon? It seems the spirits have shown their mercy at a serendipitous time.”

“Your Majesty, you can see now the New Year celebrations *must* include Druk,” said Councillor Po eagerly. “I have discussed it with the bureau and we think it is feasible; in fact, it must be done. You have brought hope back to our nation.”

Zuko stroked Druk, contemplating as the councillors spoke excitedly over each other. *Hope*: it wasn’t something he had thought about for a long time. In fact, he wasn’t always sure that the Fire Nation deserved it, after everything they had done. Had there ever been hope for the Air Nomads? A sour part of him had thought perhaps the alteration was their penance. Realistically, he knew that Druk and the promise he held for the firebenders would increase his personal popularity, make his people more receptive to moving forward in step with him. He met his dragon’s blinking yellow eyes.

“Druk and I would be honoured to lead the celebrations,” he said. “Council dismissed. I know all of the councillors are busy.”

As he left, a creaky voice called after him.

Zuko turned back. “What is it?”

It was Parvin, carefully avoiding his gaze in deference. His stooped back made him shorter than Zuko but in his heyday he must have been a powerful man. “This one wants to share a few thoughts,” he said tremulously.

“Speak.”

“I served under your grandfather,” said Parvin. “I am ashamed now to say that I believed everything he told us. Maybe if I were more enlightened… I have blood on my hands, Your Majesty. I was angry when you first came to power. A young man like yourself, who had barely tasted war! Coming in and telling all of us we were wrong.”

In the periphery of his good eye, Zuko could see Osha’s hand slide towards her hip.

“My daughter was chosen to participate in the reburial at the Southern Air Temple. When she came back, she told us…” His eyes clouded over. That had been one of the early initiatives Zuko had implemented to break the extremist ideology, by tangibly demonstrating the horrors the Fire Nation had wrought on the Air Nomads. Zuko could remember the days he spent there, crouched with Aang on the ground in the cold alpine air, collecting fragments of human bone with his hands, putting them in unmarked ossuaries. People Aang had known, people dead for a hundred years. “Unparalleled terrors. I never even knew about the genocide. That was the day I lost my bending. And when I lost it, I grew angry again. I felt like I was being punished for discovering the truth. It took many years to understand that it was a symptom of our destructive old ways. But when I felt the power of the dragon channel through my old chi paths…”

“Parvin…” Zuko said, chest unexpectedly full. “I… Thank you. I’m—I’m sorry it took so long.”

“I learnt many important lessons,” said Parvin. “Including how to light a match!”

As the guards escorted the old man to the exit, Osha said, “Now, isn’t that lovely?”

“It’s nice to feel like I’m doing something right,” said Zuko.

---

Druk got a dragon minder, one of the more enterprising komodo-rhino stableboys. Rasim beckoned Zuko to the back of the stables one afternoon, showing him a cavernous, boarded up space that hinted at a lost grandeur.

“I think this might have been the room used by Fire Lord Sozin’s dragon, Your Majesty,” he said. “Perhaps even royal dragons before his. Look at its age, its size. I can’t think of what else might have lived here.”

Druk flickered his tongue, eyes roving over the peeling paintwork. He had chosen to perch on Zuko today though he was gradually becoming accustomed to Rasim, thanks to the endless supply of jerky treats he kept in his sleeves. One day, Druk would grow to the size of a hut; he wasn’t going to fit in Zuko’s bed forever. It was a problem Zuko didn’t mind putting off until later. “I don’t suppose my great-grandfather left you any helpful dragonkeeping manuals either,” he said.

“Unfortunately not. I’ve been reading a lot of old draconic romances, though?”

How much could an old fairytale help with the day to day upkeep of a dragon? Zuko supposed he would have to find out. “Keep it up, Rasim.”

On the other side of the Mo Ce Sea, the newly-formed Cranefish Republic staged a referendum, a novel kind of vote among the people about who might continue in the presidency.

“It seems the options were between Yee Kwan and an opponent named Siong.” Councillor Hara looked up from her missive. “Unsurprisingly, Yee Kwan won.”

“Councillor, who is this Siong?” said Councillor Po.

“A figure little outside of Cranefish Town had known of,” said Councillor Hara. “His contribution to their revolution was, ah, *modest* and I cannot imagine anyone took him to be a serious challenger against Yee Kwan.”

“A vote among the people,” said Zuko. He found the idea grotesquely fascinating, like watching fire eat through a house. “Imagine if we did that for the armistice. The war would never end.”

“And news from Yu Dao?” said Councillor Ike, a little reluctantly. After months of bad news, the rest of the Council shared her trepidation.

Councillor Hara sighed. “The difficulty is that there are no leaders in the movement. It is a groundswell. Some are more outspoken than others but it’s hard to know who to consult with. The guards have intervened brutally at several points. A violent clash happened a few days ago at one of their subway train stations, underworld mobs against civilians. My envoy’s contacts are adamant the underworld had connections with the Earth Kingdom guards.”

“Perhaps after the new year, something we could discuss with the Water Tribe whether they could act as neutral peacekeepers,” said Zuko tightly. The sensation in his stomach threatened to return but he pushed it down. “I can’t sit by while people get hurt.”

That evening, as he took his dinner, Hawky flew in through his courtyard. The bird shook his sopping feathers at the window. When he caught a glimpse of a curious Druk, he leapt screeching for a rail outside in the courtyard. Zuko went outside to retrieve the letter; he tried to pet Hawky but the bird edged away and swooped off again into the pouring sky.

Thankfully, the paper had stayed dry in its tube. He unrolled it, heart beating, and let Druk snuggle up beside him.

*To His Bossiness the Son of the Sun, who demands to be written to,*

*As you can see, Hawky decided to come back from the South Pole. Think he stayed there with my* *dad a bit. Never thought he’d like the cold, but he’s gotten noticeably fatter. Like someone fed him* *tiger-seal bits. Sounds like they’re doing well down there. I should visit in the new year.*

*Can’t believe it’s been practically half a year since I got to Shu Jing. It’s been really nice getting to* *know this town properly, since we passed through so quickly during the war. I know you haven’t* *had a good look around because of the disguise stuff and all, but hopefully I can show you more* *when you’re here again.*

*Piandao’s zither club is going well. They’re performing in town tomorrow. He let me plan the* *event so you know it’s gonna be fantastic. I’m bringing the decorations, cushions, snacks! Lots of* *snacks.*

*Druk’s famous. Hardly a day goes by that I don’t see something about him in the Yitpao. “Fire* *Lord’s Dragon Brings Back Bending!” “‘The Dragon’s Flame is Breathtaking’: Witnesses Weigh* *In!” “Steal His Look: Druk’s Leash!” I really hope he does not start a trend of people wearing* *leashes. That would be awful.*

*Piandao says my calligraphy is “passable” enough to show you. Find attached.*

*New Year coming up too. Keen to see the gang get back together?*

*Catch ya later,*  
*Sokka of the Southern Water Tribe*

&#91;Again, using that beautiful seal he had designed himself. And the calligraphy rolled up beside the letter; in curling characters he had written 無所事事: *lazing about*.&#93;

---

*To Sokka of the Southern Water Tribe,*

*Truthfully, I’m just trying to survive the rest of the year. Council is wrapping up. If Yu Dao* *devolves in the next few days I will explode. Councillor Kang will announce our annual budget* *tomorrow, and Druk’s appearance had prompted a number of last-minute changes already.*

*Druk is being put to work training firebenders. He has a dragon-minder now, Rasim, who takes* *him to his sessions. Thankfully, they get along pretty well now. Apparently, the Fire Lord needs to* *run a country or something and can’t dragonsit at all hours.*

*Piandao is lucky to have you organising his event. We should have hired you to organise the* *celebrations that will happen in the Capital. The bureau planned everything months ago but now* *they need to fit Druk in… Day in, day out, my secretary is fielding requests about the most* *pointless things, down to the shape of confetti. At least everything was dragon-themed already,* *otherwise who knows what further last-minute changes they would need to make.*

*Looking forward to seeing everyone and my uncle again. Maybe you can join us on the same* *airship when we make the trip over to the Earth Kingdom.*

*Prematurely wishing you a happy New Year,*  
*The Office of the Son of the Sun, Lord of the Great Fire Nation*

---

Zuko closed Council in his customary manner on the second-last day of the year, with a fruit hamper and generous red packets for each of his councillors and their families. The scent of nectar filled the chamber as each councillor presented the challenges and triumphs of the previous year. He spent a long time wishing each well before they left Council for the year, grasping their hands and exchanging New Year wishes.

One of the servants detained him as he left. “Your Majesty, Sokka of the Southern Water Tribe is waiting for you in your chambers.”

“Sokka?” said Zuko and Osha in unison. They exchanged a glance.

“Did you know about this, Osha?”

“No? Did you?”

They made their way to Zuko’s chambers, Druk hot on their heels. Sokka was reclining on the couch in the reception room, dressed in the loose indigo-dyed Water Tribe blues that Zuko hadn’t seen in years. He popped a cube of dragonfruit into his mouth. “I see you redecorated,” he said, gesturing at the calligraphy Zuko had asked his feng shui specialist to hang up.

Druk leapt onto the couch, burrowing under his arm. So much arm, in that sleeveless shirt. Had his biceps gotten bigger since Zuko last saw them? Inanely, he edged across the room to stand in front of the calligraphy, even though Sokka had already seen it. “You came here?”

“Yeah, well, I figured you’re the one always making the trip over. I should probably return the favour.” Sokka speared another piece of dragonfruit with his toothpick. Zuko averted his eyes from the gleam of juice on his lips. “Actually, Piandao’s zither club is part of the New Year gala. I tagged along, but I think it’d be more fun to stay with my buddy in his massive house than slumming it with some old people?”

“We’ll arrange a room for you,” said Osha drolly, then backed out of Zuko’s chambers with raised eyebrows.

Zuko took a seat and stabbed his own chunk of dragonfruit. It was his palace and his food, after all. “You’re wearing Water Tribe clothes again.”

“Yeah. Even did the braids.” He twisted his head to show them off, beaded, swinging beside his temple. “Helps with getting recognised by the palace staff.” Druk blew an impatient puff of smoke at his face; Sokka yelped and coughed. “Alright, alright, I’m giving you attention now. Happy? I hear your baba’s putting you to work?”

“I should warn you,” Zuko said as Sokka scratched a squirming Druk’s belly, “it’s a bit boring hanging out with me over New Year in the palace. I have family dinner tomorrow and then I have some duties to welcome in the year.”

“Well, that’s too bad, cos I was going to ask you to go shopping with me.”

---

“Shopping?” said Osha, when she came back from preparing Sokka’s room.

Sokka nodded, wolf tail bobbing eagerly. “Piandao says the New Year night markets here are the best in the Fire Nation!”

“It’s raining. Why do you want to go out?”

“It’s *sprinkling*!”

“Did you… need anything?” she said, glancing sideways at Zuko. Arms folded, changed out of his heavy Council robes, he was trying to blend in with the furniture. “We can get some servants to go and buy whatever you need.”

“I asked him that already,” Zuko said. “He says he just wants to go for *fun*. To *browse*.”

“His Majesty does not browse,” said Osha.

“That’s what he said!” Sokka said. “But it’s fun! I can’t believe he’s never gone to the night markets!”

“What if someone tries to assassinate him?”

“With what, a firecracker?” Sokka rounded on him. “When was your last assassination attempt?”

“...two years ago,” said Zuko. “That doesn’t mean anything though. I got more unpopular this year.”

“And now everyone loves you because of your dragon shit,” said Sokka. “Plus, all the stall owners will love that the Fire Lord is browsing their wares. Come on! Pleeeeeeease?”

“It’s not Shu Jing, Sokka. Some people here haven’t gotten over your thing with the airships. And we wouldn’t be able to go in disguise.”

“What, the airship slice?”

Osha pursed her lips, pressing her fingers to her eye sockets. “If we’re going, you’re bringing your sword,” she told Sokka. “And we will need a security detail.”

Half an hour later, the palanquin dropped them off at the Square of the Sun’s Peace. A sea of dark heads bobbed inside; the crash of cymbals and drums thrummed through the crowd. Sokka leapt onto the pavement. Zuko watched as the lamplight played on his smile, flakes of rain fluttering across the vision he made. He turned back to Zuko, frozen at the doorway, and something in his expression changed.

“I can go alone if you don’t wanna come,” he said, close so the assembling guards wouldn’t hear.

“No. Let’s see what the fuss is all about.” Zuko hoisted Druk higher up on his shoulder and double checked his collar and leash. “Let’s go.”

The crowd parted before them and the detail. Zuko could hear the gasps, *The Fire Lord? Look, the* *dragon!* And, *Who’s that dashing Water Tribe man with him? Oh by the spirits, that’s* obviously *Sokka! I never thought he’d…*

Zuko had never dealt well with being gawked at by his people; he ducked his head and nodded at the greetings thrown his way. Sokka took it all in stride. He wove through the crowd with a toss of a grin, a flurry of winks. It reminded Zuko of the way he had moved through the town centre of Shu Jing, disarming. It was an easy magnetism that had little trouble turning reservation to affection in a way that Zuko could never hope to mimic.

Sokka led them at random to the closest store, selling satay skewers, and ordered one for each of their company from the delighted shopkeeper.

“Happy New Year, Your Majesty and my good sir! Many happy returns!” He handed them their skewers with trembling hands. “Any plans for the holiday?”

“Welcoming in the year with an old friend,” Sokka said. “Oi! Zuko!”

Zuko dropped his taels unapologetically into the hands of the owner’s daughter, raising a challenging eyebrow at him. Sokka narrowed his eyes. “I’m getting the next one.”

They made their way through the packed avenues. Zuko nibbled at the skewer. His digestion had truly been improving, he was sure. The press of the crowd sent nervous energy gathering at his stomach but he pushed aside the sensation, soaking in the riot of red: not the bruised burgundy of the Fire Nation but the fresh crimson of good fortune. The rain was light enough that they could forgo their bulky umbrellas. Some stalls sold trinkets and red envelopes, others sold fruit and statuettes of the zodiac animals. Zuko refused Sokka’s offer to buy everyone a round of firewine; alcohol made him go stupidly red, so he was forbidden to drink it in public. The next stall sold fai chun for the Year of the Dragon, red papers of all shapes and sizes declaring, *great fortune in the* *Year of the Dragon*, *dragon’s jing and tiger’s might*, *become the dragon in the New Year*. Zuko pointed them out to Druk, who blinked at the twisting brush illustrations of his kind.

“Your Majesty,” said the shopkeeper, letting his forehead brush the tops of his tables, “these greetings match your mighty dragon perfectly. I will do a deal for you. Two for ten bronzes.”

“No way,” said Sokka, as Zuko reached for his purse. “Four bronzes.”

“What are you doing?” Zuko hissed.

“You have to haggle, you rich dumbass,” Sokka hissed back. “I’m getting you a deal.”

“I can afford it! I can’t be seen haggling with my subjects.”

“Yeah, and you can’t be seen getting ripped off by them either.” Sokka turned back to the owner, arms akimbo. “Four bronzes.”

“Your Majesty, these are handcrafted by a calligraphy master of *forty years’ experience*, using a rare artisanal lotus-flax paper. They are worth at least eight.”

“A master?” Sokka scoffed. “I know my calligraphy. Look at this tail. That wobble?” He pointed at one of the characters, which looked perfectly even to Zuko. “Five.”

“Seven.”

“Five and a half.”

“Five and three-quarters.”

“Five and *a half*.”

An electric glare passed between the shopkeeper and Sokka, then the shopkeeper relented. Sokka slapped down his taels triumphantly and made Zuko pick out two, which he foisted onto one of the guards.

“That was so embarrassing,” Zuko said in undertone as they left. He shot a grimacing smile at the shopkeeper over his shoulder and clutched Druk’s coils tighter. The man wasn’t looking back, counting out his coins to stow beneath his display tables. “Are you really going to do that for every shop? What if he hates me now?”

“You’ve seriously never shopped in your life, huh? He’s not gonna lead the revolution because you paid less than his ridiculous asking price.”

Zuko twisted around to get Osha to back him up, but she pulled a face and held up her hands. “Don’t look at me, I’m not here, this was your idea.”

Sokka flitted from store to store to chat up the shopkeepers and other patrons. He bought some ridiculous headgear in the shape of a dragon’s snarling face and crouched down to let Druk inspect it. As he bolted off after a whiff of food—followed inevitably by a guard—Zuko found himself drifting towards a stall bearing long branches of puffed pink buds. All those years ago, when he and Uncle had been on the run, how many of these flowers had they passed? Uncle had always stopped to admire them; Zuko had rushed them past.

“Fire Lord Zuko!” came the voice of a girl from knee-level. She was peering at him from between the branches, much younger than Kiyi; she made the sign of the flame and performed a solemn bow. Zuko crouched down to her eye level and bowed back.

“You look different from your portraits, Mister Fire Lord. Just a teeeeeny bit.”

With the exception of his youngest sister, Zuko did not talk to children often. “Oh… In a bad way?”

She ignored him. “Is that really your dragon? My teacher told us about him.”

“This is Druk.” At Zuko’s nudge, the dragon dropped to the ground and peered up at her.

“Can I pet him?”

“Let him sniff you first. He’s a wild animal at heart, so he might react badly if he feels threatened. Druk, be good.”

She held out a bold little hand. Druk’s tongue flickered and he sniffed, hovering close. “Is that yes?”

“Yes. Touch his neck slowly.”

Small fingers ran across the scales. “He feels smooth. I thought he would be *slimy*.”

“Well no, dragons aren’t slimy. They have scales.”

Druk gave a happy little burp of flame and the girl giggled. “Wow! It's a rainbow! So pretty!” Then she gave an answering puff of fire, narrowly missing one of the pink buds.

“Kuntum!” A woman came hurrying over, hoisting the girl back by the armpits. “Ten thousand apologies, Your Majesty. This lowly one hopes my daughter wasn’t bothering you.”

“Mama, the dragon makes rainbow fire!”

“Of course not, madam,” said Zuko. “I think she was making friends with Druk. She looks like a talented firebender.”

Kuntum’s mother relaxed her grip, relief washing over her features even as she kept her eyes averted. “Ten thousand apologies. Please, Your Majesty, let your servant gift you some of our humble flowers.”

“I’ve seen these before in the Earth Kingdom,” said Zuko. “These pink blossoms. They don’t grow here in the Fire Nation, do they?”

“His Majesty is correct. They are cherry-plum blossoms, and they grow at the end of winter. The Fire Nation does not have the right climate.” With delicate fingers, she cupped the flowers clinging to the nearest branch. Kuntum went back to playing with Druk. “I have these imported from Yu Dao, where it is cold enough.”

There it was again, that indelible city. “You have connections there?”

“I grew up there.” A tight smile crossed her mouth. “My parents sent me to the Fire Nation for my schooling. I wanted to return home permanently but after my marriage to a citizen here and the armistice, they urged me to stay.”

Zuko had heard of people like her, Yu Dao locals whose families had sent them as footholds in another nation. The politically unstable position of the city, even during the colonial period when it stood as a bulwark of the Fire Nation surrounded by Earth Kingdom territory, clouded it with a constant sense of insecurity. “I’m sorry. The situation there is at the top of my priorities but it is delicate. The risk of…” The woman’s mouth twisted. “I want you to have a home to go back to,” he said, sounding feeble to himself. But it was a true sentiment, born from a thirteen-year-old tasked with the impossible, who had feared that home might never be in reach again.

“How can we trust Ba Sing Se?” said the woman, her voice dropping. “Crawling with the Dai Li, not to mention how they abandoned the whole Earth Kingdom during the war…” She shook her head. “Yu Dao will always be there, as a place. The buildings, the neighbourhoods… But what makes it the Yu Dao I know?”

Zuko wanted to ask her, *Do you think the agreement was a mistake?* Out here, with so many listening ears tuning in, he never could. He didn’t know what to say. He pitied her. He grabbed an armful of the cherry-plum blossom branches and insisted on paying for them. Finally, he coaxed Druk back to his side, letting Kuntum say her goodbyes.

He brushed Osha off when she tried to reach for the blossoms. In the sea of red, a patch of blue pushed through; Sokka was back. He had laden his own and his guard’s arms with food packets and was chewing on a rolled up roti for good measure.

“Zukooooo,” he crowed. Passers-by shot him scandalised looks at his casual use of the Fire Lord’s name, but he ignored them to bump Zuko’s shoulder with his. “Where were you? You hungry?”

“Here,” said Zuko. “These are for you.” Before he could beat himself up over the impulse, he held out the blossoms.

“Oh. Cute.” Sokka juggled the packets around in his arms, dumped them onto Zuko’s second guard, and grabbed the branches. “I bet you paid full price for them.”

“More, actually. Do you still want them?”

Sokka heaved a dramatic sigh. He held the blossoms to his chest. “I guess I’ll have to give them a good home.”

---

“Mama, welcome. Happy New Year. Wishing you a dragon’s jing and tiger’s might.”

“Oh my,” said Mother. She was looking at Druk coiled around Zuko’s shoulders; the dragon was watching her back, mirroring her apprehension. “Is he safe to approach?”

“Just approach slowly.” She and Ikem crept closer, just shy of touching the dragon; even Kiyi unburied her nose from her book. “Good boy.”

“How wonderful,” said Mother. “A dragon! Just like my grandfather, husband. I only ever heard stories…”

“Druk is your grandson now,” said Zuko.

Mother put a hand to her heart. “Oh son, don’t joke like that.”

Zuko hadn’t really been joking. He heard Sokka come up behind him. “Hi auntie, uncle. Happy New Year.”

“And you have a visitor? Hello again, Sokka.”

“Long time no see.” He was grinning, bowing, then staying a little stooped to accommodate for her height. “Zuko let me crash his New Year party. I hope I’m not disturbing the family dinner.”

“No, of course not. It is so exciting for a mother to have a guest join us. Come, come.” She took his hands and led him towards the dining hall. “You’ve grown so tall, Sokka. And so handsome…” Zuko heard her say, and resisted the urge to hide his face in Druk.

“I’m—” said Ikem. He gestured after Mother and followed.

“Nice catch, Zuzu,” said Kiyi, leaving Zuko to trail, flabbergasted, after them.

Sokka and Mother were already sitting down. She was nodding along while he gesticulated about something. The sight of them together made something in Zuko’s chest settle, the domestic ease of it. The food came out, piled atop large platters. Osha set his pills and a cup of warm water by his side and he used the welcome distraction of Sokka to slip them into his mouth without his mother noticing.

“Fish!” said Sokka with delight.

“This is the yee sang, Sokka,” said Mother, gesturing to the centrepiece. “Have you had this?”

“I don’t believe so, auntie. Tell me about it.”

“We have to toss the ingredients high up into the air with our chopsticks. The higher the toss, the greater your prosperity.”

Sokka stuck his chopsticks into the dish with gusto, laughing and shouting along with them as the ingredients scattered high into the air.

“How come you’re back in the Fire Nation, Sokka?” said Ikem, shovelling the mixed yee sang into his bowl. “Last we heard… you were in the South Pole?”

“Ah, that was a while back,” Sokka laughed easily. “I went to university in Ba Sing Se, but since mid-year I’ve been studying with Master Piandao.”

“Master Piandao?” said Mother, looking towards Zuko. “Didn’t you…”

“Yes, Mama, I trained with him before.”

“I remember,” she said. “He had that lovely house. But it was so painful to let you go too. You were there for a year.”

Zuko smiled thinly into his chunk of fish. When he had gone to learn the dual dao, he didn’t know he was going to lose his mother just a few years later, and for a significant portion of his childhood. “That is painful,” said Sokka. “But it’s wonderful you meet so regularly now.”

“And you boys reunited when you went to this master?” Ikem wanted to know.

“Zuko’s been visiting, keeping me company.”

“How very sweet,” said Mother. “Sokka, I want to know: how do you celebrate the New Year in the Water Tribe?”

“Oh! I can only speak to the South Pole,” he said. “It’s not as big a celebration for us. Up north, they celebrate Quviasukvik now. But down south, New Year is during summer. We celebrate Quviasukvik when the sun comes up again after winter. It’s not too different in the ways that matter, I guess. Big feast, celebration for the spirits.”

“Sokka-ge,” said Kiyi. Everyone’s heads swivelled towards her in surprise. “Have you read the *Romance of the Four Nations*?”

“No, Kiyi, I’m afraid I haven’t. I’m more of a poetry guy, myself. I’ve read *Journey to the West*, though?”

“Yes, I love that one. The Hogmonkey King is my favourite.”

“Isn’t he great? I’d love to make stuff out of my own hair. Hey, you know, haha, would you call him a… a *hairbender*?”

Kiyi giggled. Mother’s eyebrows went all the way up. Ikem caught Zuko’s eye across the table, mouthing, *How does he do that?*

Zuko felt his inner fire glow, warm and bright. He stuffed a slice of raw fish into his mouth and its sweetness unfurled over his tongue.

---

“Sokka? Sokka, you have to get up.”

It was *yin* hour. Flame clutched in one hand, Zuko rapped on the door of Sokka’s guest room. He heard a muffled groan inside and took that to mean he was awake. Pushing the door open, he let himself and Druk inside the dark room. By the firelight, he could see the cherry-plum blossoms arranged in a vase by the window, the moving lump on the bed behind the mosquito-ant net. An arm shot out from behind it and Zuko, startled, let his light snuff out.

“It’s dark.” Sokka’s voice was rough with sleep.

“We have to be at the temple at dawn,” said Zuko.

“*At* dawn? You mean you made me get up *before* dawn?”

“I told you we had to get up early.”

“Early? This isn’t early, it’s ungodly.” He heard Sokka fumbling out of bed behind him. “Can you hit the lamps, at least?”

Zuko sent out a flurry of sparks and they caught on the lamps, making the room glow. “Hey, you look nice,” said Sokka. Instinctively, Zuko looked back to reply and regretted it—Sokka was dressed in nothing but a loincloth, and Zuko felt his face burn.

“Thanks,” he said, fighting to keep his voice steady. He brushed a hand down his crimson New Year robes, fished out of storage each year. A cloud of steam came from his mouth and he tried to bat it away. “I’m gonna. We’ll wait for you.” He sidled out of the room, Druk in tow.

Sokka came into the dining hall where they were having breakfast a few minutes later, yawning and scrubbing a hand through his hair. “Morning, all. Happy New Year.”

Zuko pressed a cup of tea into his hand, warmed with firebending. Sokka took a hearty sip. “That’s the stuff. Thanks, bud.”

Zuko caught Mother looking at him over her slice of pan-fried year cake, the way one might look at a particularly adorable baby dragon. He took a long sip from his own cup to avoid her gaze.

---

“You know,” said Sokka once they were in the palanquin, “I seem to recall one very angry Fire Lord saying how much he hated it when the Earth King brought his little pet around with him everywhere.”

Zuko’s hands stilled in the process of polishing Druk’s scales. “*Excuse* me? Little pet? Druk has an important sociopolitical function in the Fire Nation.”

“You’re excused.”

In spite of himself, he felt his mouth twitching. “Take that back, you—”

“Your Majesty, can you not move around while I’m trying to pin your cloak on?” grumbled Osha.

The palanquin lurched to a halt and lowered. “That was a short ride,” said Sokka.

“The temple is next to the palace.” Zuko smoothed his hair, tucking a stray strand behind his ear. “Do I look alright?”

“Yeah, yeah, just.” Sokka licked his thumb and before Zuko knew it, Sokka was reaching for his face. Zuko felt his breath stutter. He felt Sokka swipe at his hairline for what seemed like an agonisingly long time. “All good now.”

“All good,” Zuko echoed weakly. He exited the palanquin with as much dignity as he could muster.

The walkway to the temple was lined with flaring bowls of fire that bristled against the persistent patter of rain. A shrouded acolyte bearing an umbrella led him and Druk down to the temple. On either side, worshippers fell to their knees as he passed through. At his rear, he could hear Sokka and Osha follow; his mother’s palanquin also stopped to let its passengers out.

“High Sage,” he said, making the sign of the flame and bowing low to the figure waiting for him at the entrance of the temple.

“Fire Lord Zuko,” rumbled the High Sage. “The fire spirits have blessed you, and blessed this nation, for the New Year.” He eyed Druk beside him. “Come, the hearth awaits you.”

He and the High Sage went inside. Out of the corner of his eye, he saw Sokka make to follow but Osha put out a discreet hand to detain him. They passed through the arcing atrium, out into the courtyard with its intricate, tessellating tiles, shadowed in the predawn gloom. In the centre stood the national hearth, an ancient stone structure older than the founder of Zuko’s dynasty, older even than the Fire Nation if the legends could be believed. Through its open arches, he saw the flames within simmering away.

“The first sacrifice, of air,” said the High Sage. He held out a tray; Zuko took the fan and snapped it open, fanning the embers.

“The second sacrifice, of water.” Zuko took the pitcher and poured the libation of water into the embers, sending them sputtering.

“The third sacrifice, of earth.” He scattered the lump of rich volcanic soil into the hearth.

“The final sacrifice, of fire.”

“Come, Druk,” Zuko commanded. Now, even the High Sage hung back. He looked into Druk’s yellow eyes. It was the Year of the Dragon, he had brought the dragon back to the Fire Nation. He was invincible.

A jet of dragonfire poured from Druk’s mouth into the embers. They flared to life. It was like they were back in the rainforest. Zuko reached his hand into the fire and felt that pure, searing heat, the refiner’s fire; he reached for his inner flame again and let it collide with Druk’s fire. The fire exploded from his hand, a tunnel that roared through the open roof and high into the sky, swirling with colour, just as dawn touched fingers of sunlight into the clouds. The cheers went up from the gathered crowd outside. It was the New Year. Zuko could feel his blood sing with the rising of the sun.

## Chapter 8: Year of the Dragon

Zuko led the New Year procession. The komodo-rhinos pulled his open-top carriage across the city as the sunrise illuminated the city, filtered through rain. Druk fit himself around Zuko’s pauldron, tasting the air as it burst with sulphurous gunpowder. Firecrackers blasting apart, cymbals crashing, drums thumping through the ground like a heartbeat. Fat raindrops landing on his cheek, confetti dusting the air. The dragon dance troupe undulated after them, chasing the pearl of wisdom spinning on its stick.

“That’s you, when you’re bigger,” Zuko whispered into Druk’s long ear, and the dragon let out an blast of swirling dragonfire that was answered by cheers on all sides.

In the carriage, his mother was leaning out the side, waving like the Fire Lady she had once been, letting the wind play through her greying hair. Ikem, squeezing her hand, gazing at her profile in rapture. Kiyi open-mouthed in spite of herself, book tucked away to keep it dry, eyes following the sinuous twist of the tremendous dragon puppet. Osha, beady-eyed as ever, looking beyond the riot and glitz for lurking danger. And Sokka. Arms spread along the backs of the seats, looking at the festivities with awe. He caught Zuko’s eye and winked.

They arrived at the Square of the Sun’s Peace, climbing into the royal box. Sokka and Kiyi fell upon a wheel of snacks while Mother and Ikem sat enraptured by the performances onstage. There was a ribbon dance, an erhu player, the ghazal reconstruction group. Piandao’s zither group came out and Sokka hollered, bringing his fingers to his mouth to give an ear-splitting whistle. It was the first time Zuko had seen them perform and the music was like running water to his ear, at times steady, at times a cascade of sound rushing like the rapids below Piandao’s estate. He stood to applaud when they finished and bowed in his direction.

The performances finished, as they always did, with an abridged rendition of *Love Amongst the* *Dragons*. In previous years, Zuko watched his mother as she sighed, mopped her eyes, intertwined her fingers with Ikem’s. This year, he let his eyes linger on Sokka as he experienced the play for the first time, leaning forwards in his seat to follow the romance between the Dragon Emperor and Dragon Empress as his fingers lifted fire flake after fire flake to his mouth. What did he think of it? The fated lovers, persevering through the trials of mortality? Did he believe in the red string, did he ever think—

The roar of applause took him by surprise. That was his cue to go onstage. He let Mother straighten his clothes, then Osha led him and Druk up. After a year of general unpopularity, the wave of cheers were a welcome surprise. He took the stage. The streamers rained down, coiling through the damp air, and Zuko deposited Druk onto a bespoke dragon stand beside him. At the sight of the dragon, the crowd went ballistic.

“The Fire Nation welcomes the Year of the Dragon,” Zuko said when the cheers softened enough for him to speak. “The spirits have blessed us with the return of a dragon. Druk.”

Cheers again. A firecracker went off somewhere. “For years, we have reckoned with our disgraceful past.” The cheers muted a little, but he continued. “As many of you came to see the truth, you lost something. The source of your firebending. Many of you saw this as a discredit. But to me, that signals one thing.” He paused, waiting for quiet. “It signals a truly reformed heart.

“I understand that during the Year of the Rabbit, the Fire Nation saw more and more alterations in firebending. I know it is a painful process. The Council searched for solutions far and wide, but it took the true, original source to come back to us. A dragon.

“For so many years, we thought they were extinct. We thought we had hunted and killed them all. Well, not today.” Druk gave an answering roar; coloured fire blast from his mouth, over the heads of the gathered people. “Druk has come to us. And he is prepared to teach us again the true meaning of firebending. It will not be an easy process. It will take time and patience to relearn. We must be prepared to listen, to learn. We cannot harbour the same callousness our fathers did towards the dragons. Let this be a year of more growth and self-betterment for us in the Fire Nation.”

The applause was overwhelming. He let it wash over him, shouting back into the wave of sound: “Happy New Year!”

---

Mother hugged him goodbye at the airship port in the afternoon. He held her slight body. It felt like a long time since he had embraced her and though he was significantly taller than her now, it never failed to make him feel like a little kid.

“When you gave that speech today, you looked so regal,” she said, sweeping her hands over his pauldron. “When I gave birth to you, I never imagined you would be Fire Lord. Your uncle was the Crown Prince. But the mantle has never looked better on you.”

Zuko blinked against the sudden prick of tears in his eyes.

“There’s just one *tiny* thing. Tiny, *tiny*.”

Something else again, now? “Wh—what is it, Mama?”

“Well.” She stepped back, twisting her hands together. “I know you must have only gotten together recently, but as a couple I wanted to remind you that one day you’ll need to give Kiyi a red packet. Just as your stepfather and I gave you both red packets.”

“What?”

“I know it’s early days for you both. Of course you’re only *expected* to do this when you’re married. But I just want to remind you it is the *polite* thing to do before you cause offence to someone.”

“M-marri— Mama, what on earth are you talking about?”

“What do you mean?” She looked at him with wide eyes, giving a shake of her head as though it would jog his memory. “You know, Sokka! You and Sokka!”

Zuko was speechless, genuinely speechless. He looked up to make sure everyone else was still out of earshot. Sokka caught his eye, flashing a grin over Kiyi’s head. Zuko looked away quickly, feeling the heat under his collar. “Mama. Sokka and I aren’t dating.”

“...you’re not?”

“No! Why would you think that?”

“Oh! Oh no!” Mother sounded like she was actually upset. “You had him over for family dinner, Zuko! I thought you were trying to tell us, you know, he was *family* now. And you were so sweet to each other. And he called you *bud*!”

“*Bud*,” Zuko repeated slowly. “Short for *buddy*. Not *flower bud*.”

“Oh! Look at your silly mother.” She cracked open her fan and hid behind it. “Can you blame me though? Any mother would want her son to date someone so handsome and charming and intelligent. Even *Kiyi* likes him.”

Zuko smiled tightly, looking off at the waiting airship. He was well acquainted with Sokka’s handsomeness and charm and intelligence. “Well, I would need to bring something to the table too.”

Mother closed her fan. “Nonsense, son. You are the Fire Lord.”

“So was my father, and you didn’t want to marry him.”

“True,” Mother conceded. “But—” She took a deep breath, trying to catch his eye. “You are nothing like him, son. You are… a better person. You always strive to be.”

Zuko’s heart seized. “Mama…”

She reached out to squeeze his arms. “We’ve been fundraising for a new theatre project. You should come back down to see it sometime.”

Idly, he wondered whether Ikem had told her about their last conversation. “OK. I will.”

“And bring Sokka.”

He snorted in spite of himself. “Maybe *you’re* the one who should be dating him.”

She batted him with the fan and he chuckled. Sokka came to stand beside him, waving, as they watched them board.

“In those years of my exile,” Zuko murmured, mostly to himself, mostly to hear the words out loud, “I thought she would be the only person in the world who could understand what I was going through.”

“And she doesn’t?” said Sokka.

“I think I grew out of what I needed from her. Is that stupid? I sound so ungrateful…”

“I think the same about my dad sometimes, even though I love him to bits.” The airship rose into the sky and Sokka gave him a rueful little smile. “Let’s get back. Don’t wanna miss the fireworks, right?”

---

The next day, they set off for Ba Sing Se.

With Druk and two guards in tow—as usual, Osha had the holiday off—they took the airship to the edge of the archipelago, then to the terminus on the western coast of the Earth Kingdom. In the years since the armistice, the kingdom had built a direct highway from the coast to Full Moon Bay. According to Sokka, it was a true marvel of earth engineering; from the coast the road tunnelled through a mountain range and emerged into a forest, straight as the path of a cannon save for two deviations around hills. He produced a map as they whizzed through the tunnel in an earthen subway, showing Zuko the web of highways that radiated out of Ba Sing Se. Sozin had built roads to connect his new colonies, but these were on a whole other level. Some adapted old roads, some were still under construction, others were yet to be built.

“They’re called the royal roads. Kuei wanted to unify the Earth Kingdom by making it easier to travel through it. I know people who were hired to work on them,” Sokka said. “What do you notice about the tunnel?”

“I… didn’t realise there was anything to notice.”

“Well, have we turned any corners? Any twists?”

Was this a trick question? “No…?”

“Exactly. It’s perfectly straight.” Zuko looked blankly at him. “Come on! A perfectly straight road! How many have you seen before? No changes in elevation either. It’s amazing. Curves and corners slow you down, so this basically means we can go super fast.”

Zuko looked at the map, the line dissecting the forest. Distantly, he thought about the trees that had to be logged for the project, the people and animals displaced. “I see.”

Super fast on land was still slower than air travel. When the subway deposited them on the other side of the mountains, the time difference between the Earth Kingdom and Caldera meant it was already late in the evening. They dined and slept at an inn at the highway town and the next day, they boarded a coal-fired tank train—Fire Nation design, Earth Kingdom build, no doubt part of the intellectual reparations they had paid. They arrived at the Full Moon Bay port as the sun was about to set and were ushered through a private customs queue, then on board the ferry.

“You’re going to meet your great-uncle soon,” Zuko told Druk, curled in his basket. He was amusing himself with a little turtleduck-shaped toy, and Zuko rubbed the bridge of his snout fondly. “Hey Sokka, do you think they should’ve built a bridge over the bay?”

“Hmm?” Sokka jolted from where he had been staring out the window. In the cooler climes of the northeastern Earth Kingdom, he had shrugged a thicker coat over his shoulders. The shadowed bulk of Ba Sing Se’s walls lay beyond the stretch of water on the horizon. “Say what?”

*Everything that happened in Ba Sing Se*, Aang’s voice echoed in his mind. “Are you alright?”

“Me? Yeah, I’m fine. I’m cool.”

---

Ba Sing Se was as vast and crowded as ever. They breached the walls of Ba Sing Se proper the next morning on the monorail, which took them across the vast agricultural plains that encircled the city and into the roiling urban sprawl of the Outer Ring. The sight of it never failed to take Zuko back to those days of being Lee the teashop boy, a nobody among the neighbourhoods of nobodies. It was the afternoon by the time they finally speared into the section of the Middle Ring where Uncle lived. They disembarked from the monorail and walked the rest of the way to his apartment, through the streets emblazoned with New Year red, and over melting streams filled with peacock-koi. During the holiday, which the locals called the Spring Festival, Ba Sing Se was emptier than usual as the residents made their pilgrimages back to their families; Zuko pulled back his hood to breathe in the crisp, cool air.

“Is the university around here?” he asked.

“No,” said Sokka shortly.

“You were finishing your thesis there this time last year.”

“Right.” A flinty look came into his eye, and Zuko almost regretted pushing the topic. “We’re here.” It was Uncle’s modest apartment block. They ascended the lift and made their way through the corridors. Uncle’s was emblazoned with fai chun, the upside-down character for fortune and under it, a little ink drawing of a teapot. Zuko knocked.

“Nephew! Sokka! Happy New Year.”

Zuko held his uncle tight, curling into him. “Happy New Year, Uncle. I missed you.” He blinked rapidly. “You have a new great-nephew.”

“Well, come inside,” said Uncle. “Let’s meet him.” He turned to Sokka. “Wonderful to see you back.”

“Good to see you, Uncle Iroh.” He accepted Uncle’s warm hug.

“I’ll get the kettle going. Make yourselves at home.”

Uncle put Zuko and Sokka in his spare room, while the guards rolled out mats in the living room. Druk stretched his legs in the apartment, poking his snout into every corner to Uncle’s delight. Sokka went to bed while Zuko caught up with his uncle, spinning tales late into the night, but after three cups of bedtime magnolia tea he pushed Zuko into his room. “An old man needs his rest. And you have been travelling for three days. We have the whole week. Go to bed.”

Zuko lay on his mat, listening for a long time to the rise and fall of Sokka’s breath on the other side of the room. Distantly, he thought about how he was in the same city as Kuei, and maybe he could go march up to the palace and let him have a piece of his mind about Yu Dao and Cranefish Town. Or maybe that would cause an international incident. He fell asleep to that thought.

---

When Zuko and Sokka stepped through the doors of the Jasmine Dragon, they crashed into Katara, promptly getting splashed in tepid tea.

“Sok*kaaaaaa*!” she cried.

Drippingly, Sokka said, “At least you stayed dry!” But he looped her in nonetheless for a crushing hug, ignoring the upended cups at their feet—by some miracle, *not* smashed—and then Katara’s arm was coming up to fish Zuko into the damp cuddle pile.

“Here, allow me,” said Aang, and they found themselves dried with a blast of air—curiously warm, as though it had been heated by firebending—that left Zuko staggering.

“Happy New Year,” said Uncle blithely, strolling inside with Druk sitting atop his shoulders. “So nice to have everyone together again.”

Zuko wiped at his face one last time, gathered the fallen cups, and looked around. It was a full house, perhaps fuller than usual too—the whole group was there, and it looked like Suki had brought Ty Lee as well. Zuko wandered around to greet everyone properly. Aang and Katara hugged him warmly, Toph kicked him when he complimented her lingbatou. Suki asked after Osha. He wasn’t quite sure what to say to Ty Lee, whom he hadn’t seen in a few years, and asked whether she had heard from Mai—she hadn’t, besides that Mai was teaching at a Fire Nation school, which Zuko already knew.

They spent the day feasting, celebrating, catching up. Sokka challenged Uncle to a game of pai sho and lost—though not too badly, to everyone’s surprise. Aang and Ty Lee swapped dance moves. Suki and Toph ended up in some kind of wrestling match on the floor. Katara came up to coo over Druk and before Zuko knew it, she had coaxed him into spilling all the sordid details about his digestion problems in the past year. “Do I sense Sparky *wallowing*?” came Toph’s voice, extra loud, and the configuration switched around again. Katara went to shut her up. Uncle and Aang helped themselves to more food. Suki and Ty Lee wound up on a table together, feeding each other pork-lamb pockets. As far as public displays of affection went, they were decent enough, but the sight of them so casually together set off a dull ache in Zuko’s chest.

“Did you know they got together?” said Sokka. Plate piled high, he eased himself onto the bench next to Zuko.

“Yeah. Suki mentioned at Aang and Katara’s wedding.”

Sokka crunched at a candied berry. “Damn. I just never realised how out of touch I was with everyone.”

“Is it weird seeing your ex with someone else?” Zuko said in an undertone.

Sokka shrugged. “Eh. It was so long ago. Suki and I are cool. Would *you* feel weird seeing Mai with someone else?”

Zuko thought about it, and realised he probably would. It wasn’t like he felt anything for her anymore but knowing himself, it would probably stir up something bitter and weird.

“It’s not like I haven’t been with other people since,” Sokka continued, moving on to graze on a fried mantou.

*People*, plural. Druk was prowling on the other side of the room, so Zuko fiddled grouchily with the dragon’s turtleduck toy in his sleeve. “I’m gonna talk to Aang,” he said.

“Hey, hotman,” said Aang as he approached, a sunny smile plastered on his face. “Baby dragon looks good.”

“He is. What the Sun Warriors recommended seems to be working. We’ll be organising more dragon observations this year.”

“Love that.”

“Listen,” Zuko said, thinking about Rasim, “do you know any dragon-rearing tips?”

Aang looked down at him, candied lotus root halfway to his mouth. “Huh?”

“Your past incarnation had a dragon. You can talk to him right? Can you ask my great-grandfather?”

Aang blinked. “You want me to use my Avatar powers to ask Avatar Roku how to raise a baby dragon.”

“Who else am I meant to ask? I just want a bit of a growing timeline, or to know what to do if he gets sick.”

“Well,” Aang said, giving in, “I can put in a good word for you. I’m sure Roku would love to hear about a baby dragon. Actually, that reminds me.” He clambered onto a chair, tapping his chopsticks against a teacup. “Hey! Everyone! Katara and I have news.”

Under the delicate lines of her facial tattoos, Katara went pink. “We’re gonna have a baby!” she squeaked, then clapped her hands over her mouth.

“A toast is in order,” said Uncle, raising his teacup in the storm of activity that ensued. Zuko raised his as well. He caught sight of Sokka’s expression across the room: the grim set to his smile, quickly hidden behind porcelain.

---

They passed an indolent week in Ba Sing Se. They convened at Uncle’s to wrap a veritable mountain of dumplings, then stuffed their faces. Toph took them to the sprawling city gardens where Katara loaded them onto a for-hire boat and floated them across the lake. When Uncle opened his teashop back up, Zuko spent a couple of days helping out—boiling tea in the back, of course, because the political elite of the Earth Kingdom didn’t need to see the head of state of another country waiting their tables. After closing time, they took the monorail back to the apartment loaded with leftover snacks, which they distributed between Sokka and the guards. Toph held her birthday party, filling her earth and metalworks studio with friends, disciples, and her many, many acquaintances. Zuko left Druk at home with his uncle; he and Sokka made way over on the monorail on a cold evening, trailed by one of the guards. Toph had used her Beifong money to purchase a swanky townhouse in a hip Middle Ring neighbourhood and they passed streets full of bars, art studios, and glimmering red lanterns before arriving at her chartreuse-painted door, where the fai chun were looking worse for wear. A bored doorman let them in and they felt the music hit them like a wall.

“Never change, Toph,” Sokka grunted, pulling off his coat and shoes and piling them into the cloakroom.

There were unfamiliar faces everywhere, lit by scattered stained-glass lamps. On a countertop, someone was pouring tiny cups of alcohol; Sokka grabbed two and handed one to Zuko. “Surely you’re allowed to have wine here,” he said and downed his. “Hoo!”

Zuko held his breath and drank it, wincing as it hit his throat. It was a baijiu so strong he could probably set it on fire just by breathing on it. He grabbed a roast duck pancake to soak up the wine before Sokka dragged him off to the next room. Zuko gratefully spotted the gleam of Aang’s head.

“Good party,” Aang said loudly over the music. “I was showing some of Toph’s students an airbending trick.”

“I don’t know how I’m going to sleep with this noise,” Katara complained, hunched over Momo. “We should’ve stayed with Uncle Iroh. Or booked a hotel.”

“Sweetie, Toph’s house is literally the only place I can park Appa in this city.”

“Have you seen Toph?” Zuko said. “I need to give her my present.”

“Yeah, she’s fighting people in the training grounds,” said Aang, pointing.

The “training grounds” were really a pit dug into one of Toph’s courtyards. Most of the partygoers were milling around there to spectate. It looked like Toph was in the middle of decimating some burly dude by twisting the earth into a whirl of sand beneath his feet.

“Hey, you guys got here.” It was Suki, arm slung around Ty Lee. She was sipping some kind of cocktail out of a tall earthenware cup that Zuko recognised as a Toph production.

“Getting around Ba Sing Se’s a bitch, you know the deal,” said Sokka, leaning in to hug both of them. “Are we allowed to talk to the birthday girl?”

“Sparky,” Toph boomed suddenly from the centre of the pit. Zuko tried futilely to hide behind Suki. “Get over here.”

Sokka and Suki pushed him forwards. He could hear when the people around recognised him, the gasps, whispers of *that’s the Fire Lord*. “Uh, happy birthday, Toph,” he said. “I got you a present.” He fished it out of his sleeve and put it in her outstretched hand.

“Stone knuckles. Diorite. Eastern Fire Nation make. Style seems to be from the time of Avatar Kyoshi, late period. Nice.” She stuck her fingers through it, forming a fist. “You still owe me a rematch.”

An ooh rippled through the crowd. After their last spar, he had been expecting it. “You’re on.” He took off the sash at his waist and his outer robe, shaking out the sleeves of his undershirt. Sokka took them off him, then pressed another cup of baijiu into his hand with a smirk and clap to his back. Zuko downed it and exhaled—fire.

Toph rolled out of the way, then a slam of the palm sent the ground beneath Zuko sliding. He followed the momentum, punching a ball of fire in her direction. She held her ground and raised her arms: a wall of earth rose up to block it. Then the wall careened towards him. Zuko ran up, kicked at the moving earth, and leapt over, raining down bolts of fire. The soil crashed somewhere behind him, spraying clods against his back. The crowd was going wild, some cheering for Toph, some—inexplicably—for him. Toph turned the earth at his feet into sand and he went down messily. The stone knuckles he had gifted her flew from her fingers towards his solar plexus; he blocked it with a swipe of fire and it shot back into her hand. Zuko freed a foot from the soupy sand to kick a lash of flame in her direction. The crowd ducked. She tunnelled into the ground. He panted, looking around for where she might resurface. Right underneath him, apparently. He regained his footing before a pillar of soil hit him on the backside; Zuko sent a wave of fire in her direction, then leapt to avoid another pillar. Toph was laughing. The pit undulated before him. He leapt high again, out of reach of the ground, lashing out with a flaming roundhouse kick—and when he landed, a mound of soil landed on top of him.

“Ouch,” he said mildly as Toph’s feet appeared before his face.

“I promised I’d squish you with a mountain. Sokka passed on the message, right?”

“I did,” Sokka supplied from the sidelines.

“Good fight. Can I go eat your canapés now?”

“Live your life.” She took a stance and shoved the soil off him. A skinny guy ran up to pass her a drink, probably her latest boy toy. “Alright, who’s next?”

Sokka helped him up. Zuko’s heart fluttered against the ground as he gripped his hand, and he saw Toph’s head turn minutely towards him. He beat a quick retreat.

Zuko had never been much of a party guy and as he rejoined the hubbub indoors, he felt conscious that he might already be getting too old for them. Sokka had loped off somewhere, so he sat next to Katara on the couch.

“Are you OK?” he shouted to her over the music.

Katara made a face. “No one really tells you how much your body *changes* when you’re pregnant,” she shouted back. “I can feel everything. Maybe cos I’m a healer. And it feels *weird*.”

Zuko nodded, though he didn’t quite understand. “Aang said you guys were going to find somewhere to live.”

“After this one pops out. I want to go back home to have them, have Dad and Gran-Gran close. Any word from your sister?”

Zuko sighed. Katara always made a point of asking after Azula, which was thoughtful on her behalf; whether or not he appreciated being asked depended on his mood that day. “Still nothing.”

She patted his arm in sympathy. “In time, in time.” A particularly loud blast came from the horn and she winced. “Tui’s gills. Toph’s parties are too *thumpy*.”

Zuko flagged down a tray of snacks; they picked at it until Katara went to bed early, not wanting to stay up late while pregnant. Suki and Ty Lee found him again, juggling a tray of cocktails, and he sipped at his slowly. If his face was turning red, then it was too dark for anyone to tell.

“Seat’s not taken?” Zuko looked up. It was Sokka, back again. His expression was blank, void; then the dim light caught the wince that flashed across his face as he sat down.

“What’s wrong?”

Sokka leaned back. “Nothing. Just the leg. It’s cold.”

“Oh.” Zuko bit his lip. He shouldn’t, but… He held up a hand. “Would this help?”

An eyebrow went up before Sokka understood. “Yeah. Let’s give it a shot.”

It wasn’t really a titillating position at all, or it wasn’t meant to be: Sokka with his pant leg hiked up, Zuko’s hand warming his shin, rubbing circles into the skin with his thumb. “Heaps better,” said Sokka with a sigh. “If you ever get sick of being Fire Lord you can be my personal heater.”

That was something Zuko was *not* going to think too hard about. “So you’re gonna be an uncle.”

“Yeah.” Sokka let out a gust of air, then gave him a tight smile. “Exciting.”

Something about that expression made Zuko’s insides twist. Since that night, under the stars, it was like he couldn’t look at Sokka without seeing too much. He leaned in, hand gripping the shin, not wanting to shout. “You don’t have to pretend. Not to me.”

“Pretend what? She’s my sister. I’m happy for her.”

“You’re not—”

“Hey,” said Toph from behind them. They jumped, Zuko’s drink splashed over his sleeve. “Can I borrow Sparky?”

Zuko had been expecting her since she had detected his heartbeat. He steeled himself and followed. He shot Sokka a parting glance, but he was looking away already. They dodged Toph’s many well-wishers, some canapé servers, a writhing pile of half-naked bodies. “Don’t stain the upholstery,” Toph said as she breezed past. Her room was on the other end of the townhouse, up a flight of stone stairs. There were no lamps so Zuko kept a fire going in his hand as he perched onto a seat.

“How’s teaching been?”

“You like Sokka, huh?”

Head on. Zuko pursed his lips. “Is this an interrogation?”

“An induction. Join the club.”

Zuko slumped back into his chair, clutching his flame to himself. “I know *everyone* likes him. It’s cliché, I get it.”

“True,” said Toph. “When he was here it was like, inductees every other week.”

The thought sent a wave of sourness through Zuko. “You think I should get over him.” A few months ago, all he wanted was to be rid of these feelings; now, after a whole week spent with Sokka they were a guilty pleasure he nursed in his heart. He couldn’t tell whether Sokka returned his feelings and he didn’t know whether it might be a good thing, if he did.

“I think you should know what you want.” Toph leaned close, pale eyes fixed somewhere over his left shoulder. “Just how repressed are you, Sparky?”

He recoiled a little. “Don’t make fun of me.”

Toph reclined on her bed, foot hiked up, tapping a finger against the wooden frame. After a long time, she said, “Did he ever tell you about why he left Ba Sing Se?”

“When he went to study with Piandao?” The unexpected question shook Zuko’s attention away from the ball of hurt collecting in his chest. “Does this… have anything to do with the thing that happened here?” Toph inclined her head, raised an eyebrow. “I honestly don’t know much more…”

“You should ask him. Let him tell you.” Toph was still for a moment, quivering on a precipice. “Sokka’s my bro. During the war… So many times after I moved here…” She shook her head. A crack in the façade. “I like you, Zuko. Tell you what, I think you could even make him happy, unlike the other twits. So sort your shit out and figure out if that’s what you wanna do.”

---

Toph left him in her room. He heard a clunk of stone and a whoop, and then that faded into the general hubbub of the party. He sat there for a long time, staring into his little flame until his vision doubled. He made his way to Suki and Ty Lee’s room, washing the dirt off his face, climbing into the prepared bedroll. He lay there for a long time, eyes closed, feeling the gurgle in his belly.

“Are you still awake?” he heard Sokka whisper some time later when he crept inside. Zuko pretended to be asleep. Sokka sighed. Zuko heard him shucking off his layers, wriggling into the roll next to him.

The next morning, Zuko felt the pull of dawn force him awake. Sokka was sprawled across his mat, face turned away. The curtains around Suki and Ty Lee’s bed were drawn. Exhaustion still weighed on his limbs; he felt like he had spent all night snatching at scraps of sleep. He tried to fall back asleep but it eluded him, so he fished a robe out of the closet and wandered outside.

The house was strewn with debris and people, some asleep, others being shepherded out by Toph’s doorman. He found the courtyard where Appa slept, and atop him was a tall figure folded into a lotus position. He climbed up, settling beside Aang on the bison’s fluffy back. Breath in, breath out. His inner flame pulsed in time but his mind swirled.

“Morning,” said Aang after some time.

Zuko opened his eyes, blinking to adjust to the light. “Hey. Katara got her sleep last night?”

“Probably slept better once they stopped throwing rocks around.” Aang stretched. “Ah right, the Roku thing. I’ll send a letter when I get around to asking.”

Zuko watched him, mind still puttering through what Toph had said last night. “How did you know you wanted to be with Katara?”

“I dunno. Just couldn’t imagine living without her.”

Zuko tried to picture living without Sokka. He could imagine it; he’d done it already, for quite a few years. But maybe those years weren’t quite as nice as when he was around, the half-year when he had been in reach at Shu Jing.

“You like someone?” said Aang. In his eye was a slightly pitying look, and Zuko knew he was thinking about how single he had been since Mai. He felt a spark of annoyance. He shouldn’t have asked Aang, happily married and on his way to fatherhood at age twenty-one, catching up on life at double speed after a hundred years frozen in ice, singularly convinced that love was the best thing that had happened to him and that it ought to be experienced by everyone else in the world in the exact same way.

“I was just asking.”

“I want you to be happy.”

“A relationship isn’t going to fix me,” Zuko said waspishly.

“No,” said Aang thoughtfully, “but it might be a fun little thing in your life. You’re allowed, you know.” He gave Zuko’s knee a little squeeze—*patronising*, Zuko thought—and floated down to the ground, humming as he went.

## Chapter 9: The Knife Twists

### Notes

#### Chapter Notes

This chapter contains sexual content.

CW: one character frames their experience of sexuality in terms that some readers may find distressing. Please feel free to request summaries/skips.

### Text

Toph kicked out the last of the stragglers when she woke up, a little before noon. Her cook prepared them a typical Gaoling meal of steamed dumplings and rice paper rolls, which the hungover members of the group fell upon with great gusto. Across the table, Katara picked queasily at her food. Aang rubbed her back. Zuko thought about setting his dumpling on fire.

Before they left, Toph pulled him down to hiss into his ear. “Listen up. Take Sokka along the northeastern monorail line, get off at Tangshan. Then walk up Wenxue Road. *Trust me*.”

“I don’t know if I do,” said Zuko, but half an hour later they had disembarked at Tangshan Station and he was pretending to be interested in a homewares boutique along the main road.

“Where are you taking me?” Sokka had folded his arms, squinting a little in the early spring glare. Behind him, the powder pink blossoms of a cherry-plum tree were drifting upon the breeze.

“Um. I just wanted to explore a bit?”

“You’re such a terrible liar. Toph put you up to this.”

“...no?”

“It’s pretty obvious, buddy.”

Zuko sighed, dropping the act with some relief. “Alright, where are we?”

“We’re near the university.”

He had suspected as much. “Look, we don’t have to…”

Sokka was looking into the middle distance, down Wenxue Road. The shopfronts were still splashed with New Year red and the occasional cart tumbled along the cobblestones. “No.” He squared his shoulders and Zuko felt his heart break a little at the sight, seeing him steel himself against the ghosts he must be seeing there. “No. I’ll show you around. You deserve to know.”

The guard kept a respectful distance as Sokka led Zuko down a block, left turn into an alleyway. “I used to live here. Fifth floor. I was sharing with two other guys… It was so cramped. We didn’t even have a proper kitchen. They’re fire hazards, you know, when you live in a timber structure…”

Zuko thought back to the infernal *cup noodle* business he had mentioned, all those months ago. Sokka led him back onto the main road. “And up there, the University of Ba Sing Se. Intellectual capital of the world.” The road veered uphill. They came upon a walled garden, its paths twisting upwards through rock features and trickling waterfalls, shadowed by the budding fan-shaped leaves of ginkgo trees. Then, tall and imposing, the main building rose before them on the crest of the hill. A battalion of stone pillars held up the xieshan roof, its eaves sprawling across the sky to arc at the corners.

The campus was near empty for the New Year holidays. Sokka led him inside. The grand entrance was flanked by great stone lions, looming over them as they passed. It was such an old building, no doubt older than the palace where Zuko lived. “I spent three years after the war at home, helping with the reconstruction,” Sokka said softly, spooling through memory. “Before we left with Aang, I had a role. I was the oldest male in the village. I was the warrior. It was the only thing I trained for my whole life. And then the war ended, and then everyone came home… and I wasn’t.”

In the gloom of the university’s labyrinthine hallways, Zuko could only make out the limned edge of Sokka’s profile. Their footsteps tapped against the marble floors, preternaturally loud. “When it was just me, Katara, the women and the children, everyone was indispensable to the village. When the warriors came back, I helped where I could. I carried my weight, or I thought I did. But everyone else had just slipped back into their roles from before and I—I just couldn’t figure out where I was meant to go. They squeezed me out. I was… superfluous.” He gave a small chuckle, a wry, sad sound. “Then Suki and I broke up. Probably for the best. It was hard to keep it up when we were so far apart. But I was like, a typical eighteen, nineteen-year-old, if you know what I mean, and… literally nobody else in the village was my age.”

“So you wanted to come here.”

“Yeah. Why not? I talked to my dad. He thought it might be a good idea too. Broaden my horizons, give me some *direction*. In Ba Sing Se, the biggest city in the world.” Sokka craned his head up, looking sightlessly at the shadowed ceilings. “I didn’t have a conventional education by Ba Sing Se standards. They don’t really count older women yarning in an igloo as *school*, even though I understood as much as the next candidate about how the world worked. But they let me in anyway, cos I was a *war hero*. I mean, can you really say no to the guy who stopped your country from getting burned down?”

Zuko stayed silent. He felt the cold press of the darkness on either side of them. With a flash of his hand he signalled for the guard to stop following them. But Sokka, lost in his tale, didn’t seem to notice him at all. He wandered deeper into the bowels of the university. “I had to work so hard just to keep up. On top of my regular classes, I had to take bridging courses. Relearning things I already knew, just in the orthodox Earth Kingdom way. I was the only Southern Water Tribesman in the whole university. There were people from the North Pole, sure. But I was the only Southerner, the first in decades. At the time, I thought, this was an opportunity, I’m going to change the world. I’m gonna put us on the map. I’m gonna prove that the South has just as much to offer, intellectually. No one had studied the Southern Water Tribe at the university in decades. Then of course, I ruined it all by being a massive *slut*.”

It shocked Zuko, the violence of his words. Sokka stopped at an ornate wooden doorway. Zuko lit a fire in his hand and read the plaque above it: *Department of Engineering*. “So here’s where it all began,” Sokka said, almost conversationally. He pushed his way inside. “I was nineteen when I came here. It was amazing. Parties every other night, everyone woozy on hormones and down to fuck. I probably slept my way through half the undergrad cohort. Anything that moved. It was so— easy. To make people want me. Helped that I was famous. I just lapped it all up like a desperate little polar bear d—”

“Don’t talk about yourself like that,” Zuko burst out. He reached for Sokka’s arm, but he pulled away.

“Why not? It’s true. I did all that dumb shit.” He drifted through a door that yawned into cavernous black; Zuko’s flame showed it was a deep lecture hall, shutters drawn tight along the walls. “In my third year, I calmed down a bit. Settled down. I thought, this is where I really wanted to be. Got a boyfriend, then a girlfriend.” Zuko remembered that one: a boisterous Ba Sing Se-born earthbender called Jingtao. They only met once. “When I started my thesis, I ended up with the academic of my dreams as my supervisor. Professor Chen.” He waved to a portrait on the wall, shadowed until Zuko moved his fire up. A man in his forties in an academic’s hat, sharp facial features, a long queue disappearing down his back. Up ahead, Sokka wandered up to the stage and vaulted onto it. He went to the lecturer’s low desk, settling into the seat, propping his elbows up and putting his chin into the apex of his fingertips. “I saw a bit of myself in that man, or a bit of who I wanted to become. He was younger than the other academics, relatable. He made everyone laugh, but he knew his stuff. Back to front. He was from the south of the Earth Kingdom, along the coast, and his ancestors had connections with mine. That’s why he agreed to take on my project. Even in undergrad, he was my biggest supporter.”

“And then?” Zuko prompted, when Sokka’s eyes glazed over.

“And then the rumours started.” He started to grin; it was awful, humourless, twisted by the moving shadows of the flame. “I was oblivious for so long. *Years*, it turned out. But weird things were happening. I got rejected for a travel grant, even though my thesis was the only one about a foreign country. I didn’t get any offers to assist other professors’ classes, even though every other student in my cohort did. I had to elbow my way into them. A few students were just… openly hostile to me too, even though we’d never interacted.”

Zuko thought about Sokka walking through Fire Nation streets, where by rights he should be an enemy, how instantly he had made himself likeable. “What happened?”

“Oh, it’s very simple. Someone had looked into my admissions marks… Someone else looked at my body count. They started saying that I’d only gotten where I was because I slept with Professor Chen.”

“But that’s not true,” Zuko said in shock. He knew it, in his heart: Sokka would never compromise his integrity like that.

“No one cares about the truth when you have the juiciest story about the war hero in your department, Zuko.” Then his voice changed, soft, regretful. “I found out so late. *Years* after. Too late to do damage control. I was the last to know. Someone let it slip one day and I spent weeks chasing that thread. It went so deep. Before that, no one told me. All my friends knew. Jingtao knew. *Professor Chen* knew.” Sokka spat on the ground. “The version he heard was that I’d gone around bragging about it. This kind, well-meaning, concerned student went to him. They said, *hey,* *Professor, Sokka’s been going around saying this about you. It’s really unprofessional, you should* *get him to stop*. And Chen said, *thanks for telling me, concerned student, I’ll talk to Sokka about* *this right away*. And he *never did*. He *never told me*. For *two whole years*.”

Anger, true anger, was incongruous on Sokka. He was shaking with it, like metal with lightning crackling through. “When I found out, I asked him why. He said he wasn’t sure whether it was true. But *everyone else* thought it was. I don’t even know how far it went. Everyone thought I was this slimy little whore, or a stupid liar sullying the good professor’s name. And he did nothing to stop it. Not even to save his *own* reputation. He had the power to stop it. He could’ve stopped it.” He slammed his hands down on the desk, a catharsis, a crack of sound echoing through the empty hall. It made Zuko jump. When he spoke again, his voice was a murmur of suppressed rage. “I went to the university administration. I told them what had happened, that Chen had failed to fulfil his duty of care towards me as a lecturer and as my supervisor. That the rumours had a material impact on me as a student. Handed in all the paperwork I had, signed a bunch of shit, found the student who told him to act as my witness. They said, we’ll investigate this. I had to switch supervisors, someone with a free slot who didn’t care about my work. Then admin got back to me three months later. You know what they said?”

Zuko could guess. “Yeah, that’s right,” said Sokka. “He was cleared. Not enough evidence, they said. Fuck the witness, I guess. They went up to Chen and asked him if it was true, he said no, ta fucking da. That took three months. When I requested the investigation, I had to sign a non-disclosure agreement. So I was effectively gagged. Could only tell my *close friends*, for *emotional* *support*. Never even figured out who started it. You know what the sad part is?” He sighed, slumping into his hands. “I think… I think I did have a bit of a crush on him. I admired him so much. He taught me practically everything I know. But I would never have done *that*.”

Sokka sat there for a long time. Zuko felt frozen to his spot, like the air around him would splinter if he moved. “After that happened, I just wanted to get out of this place as fast as I could. I holed up in the library and wrote for months straight. And then I went to Shu Jing.” He gave a wan little smile, sarcastic little jazz hands. “And here I am.”

Zuko knelt beside him, an echo of Sokka’s pain swirling in his throat. “If you want me to burn this place down, I’ll do it.”

“Nah,” said Sokka. “They get what they deserve for being a pile of shits together.” His face was grim. “Thanks for offering though. A year ago, I might’ve taken you up on that.”

“For what it’s worth,” said Zuko, “you’re the smartest guy I know. I know you got in on your own merit. And I… I’m glad you ended up at Piandao’s.”

Sokka cocked his head. “Yeah?”

“Yeah.”

“The last time I was there was when I lost all my self-confidence too,” he said, musing. “Maybe he’s just the solid, reassuring kinda mentor guy I gravitate towards.”
