“He’s a fuckload better than that *Chen*,” said Zuko with feeling. “So you came to Shu Jing to… retrain?”

Sokka frowned. “Isn’t it obvious?” Zuko shook his head. “I’m joining the White Lotus. Piandao wants to initiate me.”

“Oh.”

“Yeah.” He looked like he wanted to say something more, but then he nudged Zuko. “Hey, this place stinks. Wanna get out of here?”

---

Sokka took him to a stand beyond the campus that sold jianbing and out in the cold, they gorged themselves silly. It was like telling Zuko the story had knocked down a dam inside Sokka, and everything was flooding out.

“Piandao really helped me come to terms with it,” he told Zuko between bites. “I think it made a big difference for an outsider to look at everything that happened and say, *that was fucked*. Makes me feel like I didn’t just overreact to everything.”

“Didn’t anyone you know here support you?” said Zuko.

“Obviously your uncle and Toph. A few other people in the cohort thought it was screwed up but —” He huffed, his breath coming out as a cloud. “I think a lot of them didn’t want to renounce Chen.”

A flare of anger lit up in Zuko’s chest. “What? How?”

“I think they just wanted to think the best of him,” said Sokka dully. “You haven’t met him. If you did you’d understand. He’s just so… magnetic. He’s a good teacher. He taught a bunch of us for many years. He got a lot of goodwill. It took *me* a while to come to terms with his role in all this.” Zuko reached a steadying hand to squeeze his bicep, trying to convey through the touch the comfort he didn’t know how to voice. “And *you* think it’s fucked too. I’m glad I told you.”

*Why didn’t you tell me earlier, though?* Zuko thought. He supposed Sokka needed his time, but it seemed like everyone in their group, perhaps with the exception of Suki, had known about this. But he wished he could have been there to give Sokka that reassurance when he needed it the most.

“It still makes me angry when I think about it a bit more,” Sokka continued. He led them back down the road, heading for the monorail station.

“You have a right.”

“Yeah. But I don’t really have the energy anymore. I just figure, you know, people can say whatever they want about me but if I *know* I’m not that?” He spread his hands. “When people get to know me, then they’ll see the real me that isn’t defined by lies. At least, that’s what I tell myself.”

“So… the White Lotus, then?”

“Yeah. Piandao’s just showing me the ropes. Connections and intel, that kinda thing.”

“Can you tell me any more about it?” said Zuko, thinking about the time he’d loitered for hours outside a closed door waiting for his Uncle’s meeting to finish.

Sokka looked at him out of the corner of his eye, a lazy smile tugging at his mouth. “No.”

“So, you’ve really left engineering?”

Sokka sighed. “I can’t. For my sanity. Not right now. Every time I— Like I told you, everything I know—” His fingers etched indents into the paper packaging of his jianbing. “It’s their loss.”

"It's their loss," Zuko echoed.

They went back to Uncle’s. Druk leapt onto them, twisting circles around Zuko’s neck. “Hi, hi, hi,” he murmured into the soft scales in the dragon’s belly. “Baba missed you too.”

“Hey,” said Sokka, giving Zuko’s wrist a little squeeze. He turned. “I’m pretty knackered, actually. I’m gonna catch some sleep.”

“Sure.” He was probably tired from the party last night and the emotional outburst today. Zuko brewed him a cup of tea but when he took it into their room, Sokka was asleep already, mouth open and smushed against his pillow. He set the cup beside the pillow, feeling his heart squeeze with tenderness. What did he want? He wished he could shield Sokka, generous and good humoured, from all the hurt he never deserved. He didn’t know how.

Uncle came back while Zuko was trading sparks of fire with Druk, unpacking tins of takeaway food. “Sokka’s sleeping,” said Zuko.

“I can save his portion. You boys had fun?”

Zuko thought back to the party, the gloomy university, and grimaced. “It was fine.”

Uncle spooned aside the guards’ portions, then ate with Zuko at the table. He had bought thick udon noodles with a clear seaweed broth, topped with a few slices of deer-pig belly. Uncle gave the meat extra char with a well-placed sear of flame.

“Sokka told me why he left Ba Sing Se,” said Zuko. “You knew all along, right?”

“Ah yes. It was a sorry tale to see unfold.”

“When he told me, I wished…” *I wished I had been there. I wished it had never happened. I* *wished…* “He was carrying all of that.”

“Great trials can come to us when we least expect. You have been keeping yourself well?”

Zuko shrugged, noncommittal. He slurped up his udon. “You’ve developed very Earth Kingdom tastes, Uncle.”

Uncle chuckled. “I like the food here.”

“Don’t you miss the food in the Fire Nation? Spices?”

Uncle shrugged. “Ba Sing Se is my home now.” They were treading the grounds of a familiar argument now, and Zuko was never one to give it up.

“You know you can come back and visit, right?” said Zuko. “Druk will miss you if you don’t come and see him. What if he forgets you?”

“He is a smart dragon, he won’t forget.”

Zuko looked at him over his bowl; Uncle slurped away, oblivious. *Won’t you miss—*me*?* he thought. “Can’t you come back just for a bit?”

“You know why I don’t, nephew.”

After the armistice, Zuko had consented to putting the top figures of the Fire Nation on trial for war crimes, a show of good faith. The worst and most unrepentant offenders were imprisoned, like his father. Zuko and Azula had their sentences commuted on account of them being children; Zuko had to pay reparations to Kyoshi Island out of his own pocket, while Azula’s deteriorating mental health saw her institutionalised. Uncle was in a tricky position: he had besieged Ba Sing Se, yet he had also saved the city. Kuei had ultimately asked for his royal title to be stripped from him, so that he could no longer act as part of the Fire Nation royal family. Zuko had been outraged, but Uncle assented.

“That doesn’t mean you can’t visit. As an ordinary person.” *I want you to see what I’ve done with* *the place*, he didn’t say. *I want to see whether you approve.* “I don’t want to have to wait another year to see you.”

Uncle chewed thoughtfully at a slice of deer-pig belly. “We’ll see.”

When Zuko was a teenager, running all over the world in search of the Avatar, he’d been so eager to shed the deadweight of his uncle. Now, it was a struggle to even hang out with him for more than a week each year.

“Mother and Ikem are fundraising to run plays again,” Zuko tried.

“That’s nice. You should go watch them.” The finality in his tone made Zuko relent.

---

The next morning, Zuko was feeding Druk breakfast when the door to his and Sokka’s room opened. He looked up, frowning. Over the past week, he had become familiar with Sokka’s refusal to get out of bed before the sun had risen completely out from the horizon. And yet he was standing at the door, leaning against the frame, fully dressed with his satchel slung over a shoulder. Zuko left the dead rat for Druk to barbecue and stood up, rinsing rat bits off his hands at the sink.

“Hey,” said Sokka softly into the dawn. Everyone else in the apartment was a firebender and awake, so there was no reason for him to keep his voice down.

“What’s wrong? Why are you dressed?”

“I’m going home.”

Zuko frowned, wiping his hands on his sleep trousers and walking over to him. “That’s not until tomorrow.”

“No, not to the Fire Nation,” said Sokka. “Like, Wolf Cove. *Home* home.”

Something twisted in Zuko’s guts, cold and ugly. “Not *forever*,” said Sokka, perhaps seeing it in his face. “I’ll come back. I just need to visit my dad. There’s a cutter with my name on it waiting in the harbour.”

“But—” But what? He thought something had shifted after yesterday. But, but a trip like this had probably been scheduled long in advance. It would have nothing to do with Zuko at all. “Why didn’t you tell me?”

“Pretty sure I mentioned. No?” He squeezed Zuko’s bicep then let his hand fall, dangle by his side. “Hey, it was fun. I liked hanging out this New Year.”

“Yeah,” Zuko breathed out, trying not to let disappointment cloud his face.

Sokka stooped to scratch Druk’s back. “Be good to your baba, baby dragon. Bye-bye now.” He straightened again. “I’ll send Hawky along. When I come back.”

Zuko hugged him before he could think it through. He let the warmth of his inner flame soak through his clothes. He released Sokka quickly, before he forgot how to let go. “Say hi to your father from me. Travel safe.”

He could feel Sokka watch him and turned his flushing face away, hiding behind the curtain of his undone hair. Sokka put his satchel on the ground. “Here.” Zuko could see him stretch and shift in his periphery, and then Sokka handed him the boomerang in its sheath.

“What? No way.”

“Take it.” He folded Zuko’s hands around it. “You could do with the practice. I’ll come back for it before you know.”

He went off to say goodbye to Uncle, to the guards. Then he left. Zuko stood there for a long time, the boomerang heavy in his hands. Then he went into the room and shut the door behind himself. He put the boomerang under his pillow. He rummaged in his satchel for the scar ointment and shoved his pants down before he could think through what a bad idea this all was. He smeared the ointment over his dick in hard, punishing strokes, stuffing the corner of Sokka’s blanket into his mouth. Everything smelled like him. Tears beaded in his eyes when he came. He wiped everything on the blanket, pulled his clothes back on, bundled Sokka’s bedclothes into a basket, and marched out.

“Breakfast, nephew?” said Uncle mildly.

“I’m taking this to the laundry first,” he said, and slammed his way out of the apartment.

---

Councillor Kang was inaugurated as Zuko’s first Grand Chancellor on the day of the Lantern Festival, the first Grand Chancellor since the days of Sozin’s father. He knelt before Zuko on the dais as the Sages put the hat of office over his topknot. He was a stern man who dreamed in numbers and ledgers; it was the first time Zuko had seen him smile so brightly.

The party his bureau threw for him lasted well into the night. On the street, children ran about with animal-shaped lanterns. Somewhere out on the harbour, a mass of sky lanterns was drifting high into the night like an illuminated beast waking from slumber. Zuko accepted a bowl of festive tangyuan, made his polite greetings, and drifted onto the balcony. It was hot, the air thick with moisture; a thunderstorm had been brewing all day. He bit the tangyuan open. The black sesame filling spilled into the curve of his spoon. He could see the fullness of the moon, her raiment of colour where the light scattered through the cloud cover. Down in the South Pole, was Sokka watching the same moon? He had said it was summer down there, the season of neverending days: could they even see her?

Then the cool change was gusting in like a benediction. The rain sluiced down, the sky lanterns winked out, the children ran indoors, the lanterns left outside melted into sodden ribbons on the pavement. Zuko watched the bolts of lightning fork down, stark against the night sky. If they hit this pavilion, he thought, he could redirect them. Then the Grand Chancellor found him.

“Your Majesty!” He had clearly been at the sugarcane wine; he came up and slung an arm around Zuko. “I must thank you. A promotion! At my age.”

“No better person to do it, Chancellor.”

Chancellor Kang took the seat next to him. “I promise Zikri will do a great job in my old portfolio. He’s a bit of a protégé of mine.”

“I wasn’t worried.”

“What are you doing out here in the rain by yourself? Come, join the party.”

“Just a moment, Chancellor. Taking in the scenery.”

Chancellor Kang watched the lightning play across the sky with him for a while. “Can you do, you know. That?”

“No, never could,” said Zuko. “Only person in my family who couldn’t. I can redirect.”

“Funny how that happens,” said Chancellor Kang, hiccuping. “I’m the only one of my siblings that isn’t a firebender. But now I’m the Grand Chancellor. Heh, heh.” He got up and shuffled back inside.

“There you are,” said Osha, bursting onto the balcony. She must have seen the Grand Chancellor leaving. “Why haven’t you joined the party?”

“Just watching the lanterns. Well, the storm now.”

She approached, step by step. “You’ve been so weird after the trip. I thought you’d be happy, seeing your friends again. Did something happen?”

“No. Can we go home?”

“We can. I’ll make your excuses.” She gave him a lingering look of concern, then swung back inside.

Zuko took his hairpiece off in the palanquin and felt the tension release from his temples. It dropped him off in the courtyard, near-empty in the night. The rain had eased a little. “Wait here,” he said to Osha, who ducked under a covered walkway. Since the morning Sokka left, he had kept the boomerang tucked into this sash; now, he pulled it out and threw it. The angle was wrong, it went down. He could hear her groan as he went to pick it up. It was something to do with the wind. What had Sokka said again? He would have to adjust. He threw it again. It sliced through the night and came back into his hands.

“Happy?” said Osha, turning to go back inside. He let her take off the pauldron and vambraces in his room and when she left, he turned off the lamps and crawled into bed. On the bedside table was the dragon lamp still. He lit it and watched the tongue of flame burn. How could you miss someone you only saw once every month, just because they were halfway around the world, as opposed to the other side of the country? He had spent every night in Ba Sing Se sleeping next to Sokka on the floor.

He heard the tap of talons against the timber floors; a long shape nudged open his bedroom door. “Here, boy,” he whispered, and Druk climbed whuffling into bed next to him. He was so warm, much too warm to be cuddling with their combined heat but Zuko put his arms around the little dragon anyway and hid his face in his neck.

## Chapter 10: Alone Again

“I call the first Council of the Year of the Dragon into session,” said Zuko the next day. The boomerang sat tucked in his sash, where it drew no comment. “The Council welcomes a new position and a new member. Grand Chancellor Kang.”

The councillors bowed towards Chancellor Kang, seated at Zuko’s right hand. “The Grand Chancellor shall be my partner in rule. When I am not present, he will effectively act as Fire Lord. I thank the Council for its wisdom in making this decision.

“Next, the Council welcomes Councillor Zikri, who shall act as the Grand Chancellor’s replacement in his old portfolio.” At the end of the table, Councillor Zikri stood up and bowed. He was a young, thin man with a jutting clef in his throat, perhaps only a few years older than Zuko. At the bureau, he had risen so quickly in the ranks that the streak of pure talent was unmistakable.

“It is the Year of the Dragon, and the dragons have come onto our side. Let us make use of this year wisely, councillors. Councillor Hara, I believe you have updates…”

The latter half of the first month put Zuko’s reconstructed Council through its paces. The new Councillor Zikri demonstrated his skill when he completed the renewal of the loan contract with the Northern Water Tribe; he set off for the Cranefish Republic to renegotiate trade agreements with the new administration. Councillor Hara met with representatives from the Northern Water Tribe to discuss mediation regarding Yu Dao. The former colony itself was agitating for a public vote in the manner that the Cranefish Republic had demonstrated—albeit with serious political candidates. A typhoon struck one of the southern islands, a volcano on an uninhabited island erupted. Aang sent a hastily transcribed conversation with Avatar Roku’s spirit. Zuko, a little sheepish about his outburst in Ba Sing Se, penned a long letter in reply; then, he left Aang’s message for Rasim to decode. They took Druk to a large-scale demonstration of firebending before a collection of old masters from across the archipelago. The Grand Chancellor’s first duties took Chancellor Kang to a motor factory, and Zuko caught sight of the headlines he made in the Yitpao; then Zuko visited the verdant tea plantations on Pasir Hitam, terraced into the side of a dormant volcano.

Sokka’s birthday also came and went. Zuko stood in the lush black soils of the tea plantation, looking eastwards to the island that housed Shu Jing—a sightline away, in clear conditions—and let the boomerang slice through the rain.

He went back to Caldera and took his Osha-enforced break days. Nowadays, with the Grand Chancellor shouldering part of his work, he was permitted two in a week. He had lunch with Osha and Toh-ki, picking through his food. Osha gave him a long look and he watched as she flashed her gaze to Toh-ki, who tilted her head in reply. He felt the ache of longing, radiating deep in his belly. He forced his rice down. What did he want? It was maddening. He wanted Sokka to tell him what to do, how he felt, using only his eyes; he wanted Sokka to bring entire worlds into existence and then destroy them with a single glance. How was he in the South Pole? Did his leg hurt in the cold? At night, when the skies were clear enough, he had tried to search for the constellations Sokka had pointed out, but he had forgotten them and the only stars he could make out were the ones the sailors had taught him as a teenager, the mansions of the Vermilion Bird of the South. For so many months, he had denied his feelings towards Sokka; now that he was gone, he finally let all of them soak into him, everything that had built up over the months, a rag marinating in wine then set ablaze. He wanted, he wanted.

“Pardon me for saying, Your Majesty, but you look miserable,” said Toh-ki when Osha went to throw out the komodo-chicken bones. “Still sick?”

“Not like that.”

As Zuko had come to realise, Toh-ki and Osha were both direct people, though that directness manifested quite differently. Now, Toh-ki looked at him with the unblinking eyes of a badgerfrog, then said, “Chin up.”

Then they went back to Shu Jing.

Zuko let himself admit that he preferred when medical leave was just a polite veneer to satiate his desire to see Sokka.

“Physically, you seem to be doing better,” said Dr Khoo after inspecting his meridians. She pulled off her gloves, frowning still. “Something is lingering.”

Zuko pulled himself upright, knotting the ties of his clothes back together. He had to leave Druk in Caldera to give firebending demonstrations and without the dragon’s familiar weight around his neck he felt lonelier than ever.

“Do you give… advice, Dr Khoo?”

“Medical advice?”

“Um… life. Life advice.”

Dr Khoo looked at him over the top of her spectacles for a long time, until Zuko shifted in his seat. “Forget—”

“In cases such as these,” she said, “I would not necessarily give advice. However, we can discuss some of the patterns of thinking that may be causing things to weigh on your mind. Perhaps awareness of these would allow you to understand the root of your stress.”

“S-sure,” said Zuko, not really understanding. “You won’t tell anyone what I say, right?”

“That’s right. It is part of my oath as a medical professional.”

“Oh. Good, good. Great.” He went quiet. Dr Khoo looked at him evenly and he realised she was expecting him to say something; that she would give him the time to figure out his words. “I think I like my friend. As more than friends. I thought I’d get over it, but then he told me something that happened to him and… I think it made me want to… But I think I felt so much closer to him, after he told me. And then he went back home and I’ve been missing him like crazy ever since. It’s not like I even saw him that often before.”

“It can be distressing for a friendship or relationship to be interrupted by distance.”

“That’s the thing, though,” said Zuko. “He said he’s coming back. Probably soon. He even left me his—” He touched the boomerang at his hip instinctively. “My friend, a different one, said I was repressed.”

“Are you repressed?”

“I don’t know. Maybe. I haven’t dated anyone in a long time.”

“And you want to date him?”

“Yes. No. I don’t know. I don’t know if he wants me. I don’t know if I should be wanting him.” Zuko twisted his fingers together. “What happened to him, a bunch of people…” *treated his* *honour like a plaything, because of his sexuality?* “And he left, right after he told me. The next day. I don’t want him thinking I’m just another… I’m sorry. I’m not making myself very clear. I just, it’s a lot.”

Dr Khoo hummed. “Let’s start from the beginning. Tell me about your relationship with your parents.”

“That’s—” said Zuko. “That’s public knowledge.”

“I want to hear it from you.”

So he told her. He told her about his father telling him, *Azula was born lucky, you were lucky to be* *born*. He told her about his mother vanishing without a trace for years, only to resurface with a completely new family. He told her about finding out that his father was going to kill him without a shred of hesitation, just because his grandfather had asked, until his mother intervened. Azula screaming from an airship, *I’m about to celebrate becoming an only child*; Azula’s eyes glazed and empty the day the nurses told him she had lost her bending; Azula disappearing into the cloisters of the Sagehood. Uncle, melding seamlessly into a city on the other side of the world. The terrible cat-and-mouse game he and Mai played, where they would break up and get back together, and break up and get back together, and break up and get back together, until one day she stopped taking him back.

“Sorry,” he said tightly. The unexpected flood of emotion was threatening to burst through his carefully constructed dams. He allowed himself a moment to pull himself together. “This is just venting now.”

“No, don’t be sorry. Thank you for sharing this with me.” Dr Khoo sighed, tapping the end of her brush against its stand. “This all sounds very difficult. You must have felt so hurt.”

The simple acknowledgement was almost enough to set off the waterworks. Someone else agreeing that it was fucked up. He blinked hard.

“Your past experiences have led you to feel that when someone you care about leaves, they won’t be coming back. Perhaps you even feel it has something to do with who you are. But let me tell you something, Your Majesty. Usually, when people leave, it doesn’t say anything about you; it is a personal decision. If they blame it on you, that is their attempt to deflect their responsibility over their own actions.”

Zuko nodded miserably, unsure of what he was agreeing with. “But it’s not like that with So—with him, isn’t it? Shouldn’t I give him space? After everything he went through, he doesn’t need me and all my—feelings.”

“Are you giving him space, or are you afraid of what might happen if you push further? These feelings of abandonment seem to have an impact on your capacity for forming new relationships. You are worried that opening yourself to love will leave you vulnerable to people abusing what you have offered in good faith. Based on your past experiences, this is the lesson you have learnt; this is what your mind has learnt to keep you safe.”

“So,” Zuko said, “you’re saying that this is just how my brain works now?”

“The great thing about our brains is that they can learn new, healthier patterns,” said Dr Khoo. “Not everyone treats those close to them like this. Think about your friends. Perhaps I shouldn’t believe everything I read in the Yitpao, but I believe you maintain close friendships with the Avatar and the friends you made in the process of defeating your father?”

“I just spent New Year with them…”

“Very nice. And what about your dragon, Druk? Your bodyguard outside?”

“She’s an—employee.”

“And yet she comes with you here, every time.”

“She makes me take my breaks.”

Dr Khoo smiled. “Perhaps you can use their steadfastness as examples of people who love you, who will not falter. As for this man you love—”

“*Like*,” said Zuko quickly.

“—sorry, *like*. Maybe he needs his space right now. But if he says he is returning, then why not give him the chance to prove your instincts wrong? Give him the opportunity to keep his word.” Zuko touched the boomerang again, and her eyes drifted down to it. “Pardon me, Your Majesty, but what is that curious item you are carrying today?”

He might as well show her. He slipped it out from his sash and took it out of the sheath. “It’s a boomerang. It’s a kind of weapon from the Southern Water Tribe.”

“How compelling. I have never heard of it. How does it work?”

He let his hand curl around the handle, a familiar grip now. “When you throw it, it comes back to you.”

---

Piandao’s estate was oddly quiet without Sokka’s energy, the sanctuary it had been built to be. Zuko drifted like a cloud through the manicured garden until the downpour chased him inside. He poked through the house. Dr Khoo’s words percolated through his mind, drifting over each other until they became senseless. He didn’t want to think. He wished Druk were there for him to hold. He missed Sokka’s voice, let himself miss the too-close proximity Sokka always kept with him.

Zuko stumbled into Piandao’s library, a light-filled room with windows that stretched to the ceiling; a few books had been left on a reading desk. He sifted through the titles and was surprised to find Sokka’s name on one, a slim stitch-bound book printed in movable type. It was titled, *Out* *of the Ice: Revisiting Engineering and the Landscape in the Southern Water Tribe*. He flicked it open, a little guiltily. There was an acknowledgements page, blandly thanking Professor Chen and a Professor Zhou for their supervision. No trace of his anguished state of mind while completing the work. Zuko tried to read the thesis itself but he didn’t have his spectacles on hand and the academic metalanguage threatened to give him a headache. He slid the book back into the pile and slipped out.

There was a set of stairs leading to a second level of the house, which he had never really entered. He made his way up. The floor was clean but obviously disused: a low round table, a pile of floor cushions. A White Lotus meeting area? Had Sokka attended any gatherings here? He heard a bird’s cry, oddly close, and followed it to a doorway off to the side.

It was Hawky’s room. The floor was covered in sheets of scrap paper filled with crooked calligraphy, presumably to catch bird droppings. A makeshift perch had been set up in the middle of the room and the messenger hawk was perched there. He gave another cry.

“Hi,” Zuko said. “Do you remember me?”

Hawky ruffled his feathers and turned, casting Zuko a look from the side. Of course Sokka had left the bird here. He hadn’t brought Hawky to Ba Sing Se. Zuko held his hand up for the bird to inspect, but Hawky was still not keen on being touched. He put his arms around himself and watched the bird, then watched the rain through the open window.

He heard the floors creak behind him and whirled around, fire gathering in his fist on instinct. “Oh, it’s you, Sifu,” he said. He made the sign of the flame and bowed.

“I see you found the hawk’s room,” said Piandao from the doorway.

“Sorry, I was curious. I’ve never been upstairs.”

“Of course. And there is not much to do in an old man’s house when it rains like this.”

Piandao turned around and started towards the stairs. Zuko, unsure of what to do, trailed after him. “Your zither club at the New Year gala. That was… really nice.”

“Oh yes? Thank you.” Piandao glanced back.

“You should have told me you were coming to Caldera. The bureau never tells me anything. I had no idea until Sokka was in the palace.”

Piandao hummed with a little amusement. “Your uncle was a great admirer of the zither.”

“It seems like his thing.”

“Let me show you my recent hobby,” said Piandao, taking him out to the porch. On the table sat a thicket of potted orchids, some spindly, others quite mature. Piandao took a pair of delicate shears and started to prune. “Aren’t these magnificent? I am but a novice at this point.”

“Right.” They were all in bloom, stems pendulous with the fleshy petals. He watched as Piandao’s shears made their way around a plant.

“Forgive my presumptuousness, Your Majesty, but I must ask something. Were you wearing your father’s old robes during the New Year?”

Zuko flushed. “You noticed?”

“I’m sure most people didn’t. I have a good eye for these kinds of things.”

“It’s accounts stuff,” said Zuko, resigned. Of course Piandao, esteemed gentleman and master of the literary and martial arts, would have noticed. “When I became Fire Lord, I was still growing and it didn’t make sense to make a new set of ceremonial robes for me every time I sprouted an inch. Especially while we were paying reparations. Then the recession happened and it didn’t make sense for me to get new robes while my people… while everything was too… expensive.” He fiddled with the edge of his sleeve. “All of my personal expenses are funded by my estate now —what’s left of it, I should say. Most of my everyday clothes are my own, but I don’t think I could budget in a *proper* new set of robes.”

Piandao hummed, snipping away. “I see your thinking. Still, I do regret that our Fire Lord must wear the castoffs of a man who has done him the deepest personal wrongs. Your Majesty, if you will allow your old teacher one indulgence: let me sponsor a new set of ceremonial robes for you.”

Zuko gaped. “No. No, no, I can’t let you do that.”

“Why not? I am unaffiliated with the court, I have no heir, so I would have no ulterior motive to gain personally from this. I think I already have enough of your goodwill by inviting you to my home regularly.” He flashed Zuko a knowing smile, then switched his shears for a spray can. “I want this nation to be represented well through its head of state. And perhaps the aesthete in me wants the Fire Lord to be clothed beautifully.”

“Sifu, do you know how much a full set of ceremonial robes costs?”

“Of course. I *do* pride myself on being a man of culture.”

“It’s too much.”

“Nonsense.” Piandao punctuated his words with a misting of water over the flowers. “Did you not sponsor that lotus-flax atelier as part of the Cultural Revival Grants? Give them something to truly sink their teeth into.”

“They’re not even based in the capital—”

“So fly a seamstress out to the palace to take your measurements.” Piandao put down the can. “I don’t hear any good reason why you shouldn’t have these robes. I can produce some sketches of what I have been envisioning though, of course, the final design should be left to the professionals.”

“At least let me pay for part of it,” said Zuko. Now the idea had been planted in his mind, it started to germinate and even if he wouldn’t admit it aloud to Piandao, he realised it was something he wanted.

“If His Majesty insists.”

“He does.”

---

Before he left Shu Jing, Zuko tried to draft some letters with half a mind to send them on through Hawky.

*Hey Dear Sokka,*

*How’s the South Pole? How is your dad, how is your village? When are you coming back? Tell* *them hi from me.*

*Guess what, I’m in Shu Jing, and I found Hawky here. So I thought I might send you this. Had to* *see Dr Khoo again and she said that I have abandonment issues. and she said I was doing better.* *Piandao said he wanted to get me new ceremonial robes. That’s exciting. It’s raining like mad and* *there’s nothing to do and I swear the only thing that keeps me sane is your boomerang. I keep* *thinking about what might have happened if you went on that airship home with me.*

*I wish I could say everything I wanted to say to you but I can’t even find the words.*

*Why did you give me your boomerang?*

*Would you be angry if I told you*

*Are you*

*I*

*Fuck.*

Would Hawky even get there in time? What if Sokka were on his way back, and the bird just missed him? What did he even have to say, anyway? He burned the paper up in his fist and let the ashes flutter out of the window.

---

A week later, Zuko was back in Caldera, getting his measurements taken in front of a mirror. The seamstress worked with curt hands, stretching the tape across the body before barking the measurements to her assistant.

“And this design, Your Majesty, that’s what you were thinking?” she said when she was done, tapping the sheaf of paper where Piandao had doodled a quick design.

“Something to that effect. I don’t have much of an eye for this, but my old swordmaster had some ideas. He’s much more knowledgeable about the arts.”

She inspected it for a while. “The ideas here are a real departure from the previous style. Is that something you’re happy with?”

“Yes. That is what I want.”

“Well then.” She gave a flick of the hand to the assistant, who carried over a file and a roll of plain material. “I think we could push the ideas here *further*.”

“Further?”

“Yes, Your Majesty. Look.” She unwound the material and draped it over his body, plucking the fabric into folds. “Loose with movement built into the cut. Flowing sleeves. Your current robes are structured, militaristic, yet you want to send a different message. Your Majesty, do you want to keep wearing a pauldron as part of your official robes?”

“Not really,” Zuko admitted.

“Good call. It is heavy, no doubt; not entirely suited to our hot climate or our current national philosophy. What we can do instead is create an impression of those sharp shoulders”—she reworked the position of the fabric—“but use cloth, rather than armour.”

Zuko studied the silhouette in the mirror. It was so different from what he was used to wearing, but somehow the cut seemed to bring out something he’d never glimpsed before in himself. Not the kid wearing his father’s clothes, not the teenager thrust prematurely into stewardship over an entire nation. Fire Lord Zuko in his own right, that elusive figure. “It looks good.”

“This way, there is some visual continuity between the old robes and the new. However, that military stiffness will be gone.” She handed the cloth to the assistant to rewind and opened the file, pulling out small samples of fabric. “Are you familiar with lotus-flax at all, Your Majesty?”

“I believe my mother had some vintage pieces in her wardrobe, when she was still Fire Lady.”

The seamstress nodded. “That makes sense. Industrialisation stifled the art, which values handiwork and craftsmanship. But no fibre in the Fire Nation is superior to it.” She let him feel one of the squares: the fabric had a sheen to it and was smooth and cool to touch. “We extract and weave the fibres of the lotus-flax plant by hand. The result is similar to spidermoth silk, but much more breathable on the body. Ideal for us living in the tropics.” She flipped to a page of dyed samples. “Your current robes have a lot of black. It is a flattering colour, but seems incongruent with the outlawing of the black flame.”

“Fire as a source of life, rather than destruction,” Zuko said. He had said the same thing when he passed that decree. “The character for black depicts a field razed in war.”

“Exactly. Perhaps we will keep some black as an anchor, but a focus on red and gold would be ideal.” She picked out two samples of the iridescent lotus-silk. “We will dye the panels with the design in addition to embroidery. We work with some of the best craftspeople in the nation, and their talents cannot be rushed. The process will take several months but with luck, we will have the robes ready for your birthday celebrations and the coronation.”

It was much sooner than Zuko had been expecting, and the prospect of having new robes on his birthday thrilled him. “That sounds very good. It is an honour to have your atelier work on my robes.”

“Your Majesty, the honour is ours. I have one final request.”

“Yes?”

“May we sketch your dragon? It seems to me that he must be depicted on these robes.”

A juvenile Druk on his robes. It would be like a time capsule to look back on and remember him as a baby. He smiled. “Of course.”

After the seamstress left, he spent a long time inspecting himself in the mirror, looking past the surface flaws to chase the glimpse of the man he had seen when she had arranged the cloth on him. *You should know what you want*, Toph had said. Zuko wanted to be that man. The Fire Lord free from his father’s legacy, the man unstifled by the weight of his robes. He wanted to shrug off the pasts that cloaked him: the history of his nation, the dysfunction of his closest relationships. He wanted to act without hesitation, to act in spite of the flaws that ghosted his every action. He wanted to be brave enough to open himself for love again, and he wanted to trust that it would not be snatched away the moment he embraced it.

---

Hawky came early in the second month, when the oppressiveness of the hot-wet started to ease into drizzle and patches of sun. Zuko unrolled the letter, trying not to spark the paper alight with the energy trembling at his fingers.

All it said was this: *I’m back.*

## Chapter 11: The Ignition

“Are you alright?” said Osha. There was no small amount of alarm in her eyes as they skirted around the town centre of Shu Jing. “Do you need, like, calming tea? A sedative?”

It was dusk. Zuko had made Osha arrange the trip as soon as he could manage, and they headed to the airship port right after the close of Council for the week. He felt like he could run all the way up to Piandao’s estate without stopping for breath; he could jet the whole way there and still have fuel left over to launch himself into the stratosphere. It was the first time in months that he had come to Shu Jing for the explicit purpose of leisure. He wouldn’t be seeing Dr Khoo at all. He could spend all that time with Sokka and, and—

“I’m perfectly calm,” he said. His hand flexed by his side, sparks glittering at his fingertips.

He tried not to be dismayed when the lotus gate opened and it was Fat’s face that peered out from the gloom. “Oh, it’s you,” he said. “You know where to go.”

The hallways were empty when he and Osha made their way to their rooms. Wan light filtered through the courtyard. Zuko pushed his way into his room, an increasingly familiar space, and put his things down, let Druk out of his basket. Then footsteps came outside the door.

He whirled around. Sokka, darkening the doorway like a vision: the shaved sides grown out a little, the stubble along his jaw now gathering at his chin. He looked so good, so much more handsome than the version that existed in Zuko’s memory, as though his brain didn’t have the capacity to render all the finer details. Druk chirruped.

“Here, catch,” Sokka said, flinging something at him. Bewildered, Zuko caught. Dual dao, the real deal, not practice blades. Sokka unsheathed his own sword, the black one forged from the heart of a meteorite, and lunged.

Zuko barely had time to react. He brought his blades up to parry, a *clang* of metal ringing in his room. The dao had to be something from Piandao’s own collection; the heft was different from his own and he had to adjust to their weight. An arc of rainbow flame reflected on the metal—*Druk*. Zuko hit the ground and rolled out the door. Sokka pursued, eyes narrowing with determination. Another arc of flame, before Zuko twisted and kicked the door shut. Sokka was above him. He ducked away. Slice, block, twist, slice again. The adrenaline in his nerves, already aquiver, welcomed the outlet. Leaping down into the central courtyard, a spray of white pebbles. Sokka’s swordsmanship had improved since their last spar. Impeccable form, impeccable discipline, except for the unnameable *something* glinting in his eye. Zuko backed into the hallways, letting the dark swallow him, and the blows rained down in a whirlwind of black.

They slashed their way into the reception room, dancing around the furniture. By some miracle, nothing broke, though Sokka sliced the head off a potted orchid. “*Outside*!” yelled Fat from somewhere far away. Zuko pushed into Sokka with blades crossed and gained the upper hand, forcing him out into the garden in a screech of metal. The sky was ablaze. Sokka was looking at Zuko, and only Zuko. Whirling with the momentum of his dao, Zuko wove between the calculated attacks of the sword. They battled atop a table, they battled atop the retaining wall. Sokka vaulted into the garden and Zuko was in pursuit. Whirling robes, whirling blades. They fought their way across the meandering paths, over the bridge that arced above the peacock-koi. The sunset lit Sokka’s teasing smirk in orange.

Heart beating in his palms, against the wooden handles of his dao, Zuko pressed harder, gritting his teeth. Sokka was fighting back lazily, almost toying with him. The blue beads in his braids and earring glinted in the setting sun. He twisted the dao aside with a loop of the sword. Zuko kicked his feet out from under him as his centre of gravity shifted. Sokka went down. Zuko stood over him and held the point of his dao to his throat. His lungs screamed for oxygen.

“Do you surrender?”

Under him, Sokka eyes travelled up the line of the blade. A flash of pink: his tongue flashed out to wet his lips. A quirk of the eyebrow. “No,” he said, and there was a scramble of movement. The dao clattered onto the ground. Sokka hauled an arm around him and put the black edge of his sword to Zuko’s throat. Zuko held his breath. He could feel Sokka’s chest heave against his back.

“I win,” said Sokka into his ear. The words vibrated in his diaphragm. “And I’m taking this back.”

Zuko felt the boomerang plucked from his sash. He kept his throat tilted up, eyes forwards.

“Now you have to give me something I want,” said Sokka.

Zuko’s throat worked. The edge of the sword came away a little. “Is that how it works?”

“I’m the one with the sword to your throat.”

“OK. I have your birthday present in my bag.”

“That’s not what I want.”

“You haven’t even seen it yet.”

“I know what I want.” Sokka pressed closer. The moment pulled taut, then came apart, fraying. “Tui and La, Zuko, are you gonna make a guy wait forever? I know you like me.”

The jig was up. Sokka stepped back. Zuko could hear him sheathing the sword, then there were hands at his shoulders, trying to turn him around. He stayed rooted to the spot.

“Come on, Zuko,” said Sokka, laughing a little. “Wait, come ooon. I was just gonna tell you I like you back.”

Zuko did turn around, slowly. His heart was thumping hard enough to break a rib. Sokka was grinning. Up close, Zuko could see the freckles dotting his nose. “You like me?”

“Yeah.” Sokka put his forearms on his shoulders, their solid weight. His fingers came up to brush a strand of hair behind his ear.

“Wait,” said Zuko. His mouth felt disconnected from his brain, emotions swirling unmoored through his body. It was strange to be touched like this, to be touched like this by *Sokka* who was usually all affable tugs and shoves. “How did you find out, though?”

“I dunno,” said Sokka, still smiling, still looking at him. “You were kinda obvious. Did you wanna keep asking me dumb questions?”

“They’re not dumb. Why did you just fight me?”

“I have a better idea. How about I kiss you?”

Zuko shut up. Sokka leaned in, brushing noses; a plush mouth caught against his. A hand came to cup the scarred side of his face and Zuko was caught in the headiness of the touch, the ebb and flow of each kiss. His hands hovered over Sokka’s back, then gripped solid muscle. He never thought this would really happen, he never thought it would happen so soon. He was unpracticed, yet the rhythm of kissing came back to him with Sokka taking the lead. The sky around them was darkening and all he could see was the sweep of Sokka’s eyelashes. The assertive brush of Sokka’s tongue against his, his teeth catching against Sokka’s bottom lip on an upstroke, a hand pulling his hairpin free to tangle into the strands. It was happening, it was actually happening.

“This wasn’t how I pictured this going,” Sokka murmured between kisses.

“I haven’t done this in ages,” said Zuko. “Anything, with anyone, in years.” It was too many words to keep kissing, so he leaned back into the circle of Sokka’s arms.

“I don’t mind.”

“I should warn you. I’m the ruler of this country. There’s no privacy, I—”

“And?”

Zuko thought. “I only have one eyebrow?”

Sokka looked at him. “I… know.”

Almost belatedly, the self-consciousness was creeping in. It all felt too good to be true. What kind of cosmic coincidence was this? What was the catch?

“Zuko.” Sokka shook him lightly. “You know me. If there’s something to think about, then I’ve thought about it from every possible angle. I spent the whole damn trip home just *thinking*. Look, I’m sure there’s some insane Fire Lord stuff I haven’t accounted for but—I honestly wanna give this a go. This, us.”

Zuko’s head was a cloud. “You’re… serious about me?”

“Come on, man. I told you I like you. Give me some credit.” What did Dr Khoo’s say? *Give him* *the opportunity to prove your instincts wrong.* “Look, if you didn’t wanna actually go out with me, you should definitely break my heart now before I get too attached, haha.”

Zuko couldn’t have that. He hauled Sokka forwards by the shoulders and kissed him. The angle was all wrong but Sokka responded at once. “Sorry, I didn’t want it to come out like that,” he whispered into Sokka’s mouth. “I do like you. A lot. I can’t turn it off. I want to give it a go too.”

Sokka stroked the side of his face. He looked so fond, heartbreakingly fond. “We can talk about all this later. I want to,” said Sokka. “But I wanna take you on a date first.”

---

They took a tuk-tuk from town the next day, chugging over the mountainous country. The previous night at Piandao’s had been torturous. With the feelings between them so coltish, they had wanted to stay discreet in front of the other inhabitants. But Zuko had been alight, and he was certain everyone could see the glow from his face. Smilingly, they had endured Fat’s lecture about sparring inside the house, then Osha’s about leaving a worried firebreathing dragon around wooden furniture. At dinner, each time he had met Sokka’s eye over his bowl of rice, it was like they were trading a spark of electricity between themselves, a live wire stretched between them. Now, Zuko let himself press close to Sokka in the back of the tuk-tuk, marvelling at the fact he could. He was wearing the veiled hat, Druk ensconced in his basket. The sun was coming in and out from the cloud cover. The tuk-tuk veered over the rolling green to the harbour town, dropping them off at the edge of the town. From the top of the hill, Zuko could see its harbour bristling with fishing sampans, the rock islets dotting the bay.

“This way,” said Sokka, waving him away. He took them along a seaside path over the cliffs where the wind gusted all around them, bending the palm trees above. Zuko took off his hat and let it sling around his neck, the gossamer veil swirling; he let Druk out as well and the dragon gambolled through the grass behind them. Below them, the waves draped the rocks in lacy foam.

“I found this place when Fat made me come down to buy fish,” Sokka said. Distractingly, his hand was dangling free by his side. “Practically nobody comes down here.”

Zuko reached out and grabbed the hand. Sokka just squeezed it, as though it was something they did all the time, as though Zuko hadn’t spent the last minute psyching himself up to hold it.

They picked their way down a steep hillside to a pocket beach.

“This is nice,” said Zuko. “I can’t believe you found such a private spot.”

Sokka dumped his bag onto the black sand then pulled his clothes off until he was down to a pair of short swimming pants. *You’re allowed to look now*, Zuko told himself as he wrestled with the instinct to avert his eyes. “Remember when we were at Ember Island? You destroyed my sand sculpture of Suki.”

“I do not remember any sculptures of Suki.” Zuko pulled off his shirt and then Sokka was on top of him, barrelling him into the cold water. Zuko squawked, instinctively heating the water around him.

“Did you just—*firebend* the water?”

“It’s cold,” said Zuko mulishly. Sokka was knee-deep in the waves; Zuko hooked his ankles around the backs of his knees and pulled him into the water. It was Sokka’s turn to squawk.

“See?” Zuko put his arm into the water around him and let it warm up.

“I will admit that it’s nice,” said Sokka, “but a wholly inauthentic way to enjoy the beach.”

The warm water swirled away. Zuko let himself adjust to the water temperature. On the shore, Druk flickered his tongue and decided to get in, his body making sinuous ripples through the water. Sokka was further out trying to catch the waves, letting them barrel into his chest and wrest him backwards before charging forwards again. “Come over,” he called, bobbing in and out of the water. Zuko waded to him. Sokka caught his wrist and they kicked through the water together, letting the waves toss them around like clods of kelp. The sun winked out through the clouds; Zuko turned around and Sokka was looking at him, his loosened hair slick with seawater, something unreadable in his eyes. He let Sokka reel him in. He tasted the salt in his mouth, he savoured the scratch of stubble against his chin.

“I get to kiss the Fire Lord,” Sokka singsonged when he pulled back.

Zuko rolled his eyes. “By the *spirits*, Sokka—”

Sokka squeezed him around the middle, twirling him through the water. “Imagine if I told fifteen-year-old Sokka he’d be taking the Fire Lord on a date. To be fair, he’d probably think it was your dad.”

Zuko buried his face in his hands, but he was laughing. “That’s so wrong.”

They drifted back onto the sand. Zuko caught sight of the tip of the boomerang in Sokka’s bag. “Can I throw?”

“Uh, not over the water.”

“I’m good at it now.” Zuko fished it out and demonstrated. The boomerang caught the sun as it spun out over the waves. He leapt up to pluck it out of the air and bowed with a flourish.

“Someone practised.”

They flopped onto their towels to dry off. Zuko realised that he was famished. Sokka had packed lunch, wrapped in banana-lotus leaves: coconutty rice with komodo-chicken curry, half a boiled egg each. They ate with their hands and rinsed them in the tide. Zuko fed Druk a chunk of meat when he returned from the water and chuckled when the dragon slithered back into the sea after his bite, off to chase some otter-puffins. “I never expected he’d like swimming so much,” he said, “but he must’ve swum to that island where we found him.”

“I swear his little horns are getting bigger.” Sokka lay back on his towel, flinging an arm over his eyes. Zuko copied him, staying a respectable distance away on his own towel, but Sokka turned his head to crack open a quizzical eye. He flung open an arm. “What are you so far away for? Come cuddle.”

Zuko moved his towel and tucked himself into Sokka’s side, puzzling out the fit: nose under his jaw, head on his shoulder, arm skimming around his chest. Friend becoming lover. Sokka’s arm came down to pull him snug, a grounding weight. The sensation was incredible, Zuko realised, touching another and being touched in return. He hadn’t felt it in so long. He had missed it so much.

“This is the best first date I’ve been on,” he murmured into Sokka’s neck.

“Were the other ones dinner dates?”

“I guess. Except with Mai, where we had the dubious honour of starting to date on a warship.”

Sokka laughed. “Dinner dates are the *worst*,” he said. “It’s just you, a stranger, and all the awkwardness you can build up in two hours. The Sokka secret ingredient for a good first date is dopamine.”

“Right,” said Zuko. “So that’s why you confessed your feelings for me by attacking me with a sword.”

“What’s the matter, Your Flameyness? Not up for a bit of *swordplay*?”

Zuko felt himself flush, turning his face away. He felt fingers skimming across his back, tracing the shape of his spine, his shoulder blades, his ribs. “I can’t believe you said I was *obvious*.”

Sokka snickered. “You gave me flowers, dude.”

“I didn’t even mean to buy them for you. I just acquired them, and passed them onto you.”

“*So* romantic.”

They fell quiet and listened to the rush of the sea. Sokka started humming; Zuko found his thumbs rubbing circles into his skin.

“Let’s play a game,” said Sokka lazily. “It’s not a game, actually. I want to see if we’re on the same page about stuff.”

Zuko opened his eyes, feeling his heart beat faster.

“*Relax*, dude.” Sokka’s arm pulled him back down and readjusted its position. “You’re so coy. Coy like the fish, haha.” Zuko groaned. “We’re just gonna talk about it. You wanted to, yesterday.”

“Changed my mind. I like cuddling.”

“No, listen here.” Sokka rolled him over, bearing down from above to peck him on the mouth. It lit a thrill through Zuko, even as he screwed his face up. “I’ll ask you a question, and we both have to answer it. Alright?” Sokka settled on top of him: weighty, real. “I’ll go first. Hmm. If you could have a snack right now, what would you eat?

“Really?”

“Answer the question, Your Flameyness.”

Zuko snaked his arms around Sokka, thinking. “Coconut juice. Your turn.”

“Fried fish skin. You ask me now.”

“Um… What’s your favourite kind of tea?”

“Longjing, the way your uncle makes it. Mwah! Yours?”

“Da hong pao.”

“What’s your favourite firebending move?”

“Dancing Dragon.”

“*Other than* the Dancing Dragon.”

“Oh.” Zuko thought. “Probably a fire stream.”

“Eh,” said Sokka. “I like when you breathe fire. It’s kinda sexy.”

Zuko pulled back to squint at him. “You know *Aang* can do that as well? My uncle? A good number of firebenders? Do you think *they’re* sexy when they do it?”

“Don’t ruin my fantasies,” said Sokka.

A wet, slippery weight twisted around their legs: Druk, back from his swim. Zuko let him crawl between them, reaching down to scratch his scales. “If you could choose any element, what would you bend?”

“Earth. Before I met Toph, I would’ve said water, though.”

“I’d wanna bend water.”

“What do you like about me?”

Zuko felt his face flare. *Keep it cool, keep playing the game.* “Um. You’re easy to be around. You’re smart, you’re funny. You seem to just… get it.”

“Damn, you like my personality? What about my insane good looks?”

“Shut up. Um. I think you’re good looking. As well.”

Sokka kissed him on a flaming cheek, laughing. “I like your dedication.” He wound a strand of Zuko’s hair around his finger. Zuko’s face was so hot he felt like he was going to emit smoke through his skin. “I like that you always strive for the right thing, and I think you’re an amazing dragon dad. Plus I think you’re super sexy and cute, which helps.”

He had to be blushing so hard. “I’m not sure you can be sexy *and* cute at the same time,” he said.

“Mm, you managed it, though.”

Zuko scrunched up his face.

“Why are you so embarrassed? Hasn’t anyone told you you’re cute before?” Sokka pressed.

“No?”

Sokka gave a dramatic gasp, flopping backwards onto his own towel. Dislodged, Druk huffed and crawled off into the sand. “No way. What an absolute crime. A travesty.”

“I don’t think Fire Lords are *supposed* to be cute.”

“Well, you didn’t get the memo.” Sokka reached over to poke his nose, then released him from his torment. “You ask now.”

“What do you hate eating?”

“Durian.”

“No way. You just haven’t had it in dessert.”

“Urgh. I can’t think of a worse thing to put in my dessert.”

“You’re so wrong. I don’t like salted eggs.”

Zuko tucked himself back into his side. “What are you proud of?” Sokka asked.

Zuko made a noncommittal sound.

“Rules of the game, you have to answer every question.”

“You just made that up.”

“I made this whole game up.”

Zuko said finally, “Getting my own dragon.”

“That’s something to be proud of. You had to win him over. Not everyone can do that.” Sokka sighed. “For me, maybe… going home and seeing they’d built the sluice system I invented.”

“You did?” Zuko felt the movement of Sokka nodding. “That’s pretty cool. What did you do in the South Pole?”

“That’s not a question both of us can answer.”

“I’ll tell you what I did while you were gone?”

“Fine. Hung out with the family. Got stuffed full of whale and seal blubber by Gran-Gran. Dad says hi back, by the way. Went on some hunts, checked out some of the new infrastructure. Took the polar bear dogs out for a day trip.” He sighed. “It’s so different now. Feels like it changes so much every time I go back. It’s cool, but.”

Zuko squeezed him around the middle, and he squeezed back. “I got measured for new ceremonial robes. They’re a gift from Piandao.”

“Wow,” said Sokka. “That’s really nice. I’d love to see you in them.”

“Yeah. Finally won’t have to keep wearing my father’s hand-me-downs.” He bit his lip. “I missed you.”

Sokka’s hand came up to cup his face, thumb brushing against Zuko’s bottom lip. “Yeah. Missed seeing you too. Spent the whole time thinking—” He cleared his throat. “What do you value most in a relationship?”

Zuko reared up a little, turning to look at Sokka. His eyes were closed. “Just tell me what you think.”

He lay his head back down on Sokka’s chest, tiptoeing through his words. “I think… I think, security.”

“Nice,” said Sokka. “For me, trust.”

Zuko was quiet for a while. Answering the question had left him feeling raw, an oyster-clam with its shell shucked open. He let Sokka card his fingers through his drying hair, crisping with salt. “Hawky or Druk?”

“Hey.” Sokka’s hand came back down to hold his chin, tilt it up. “Ask me something that matters.”

The questions jostled around his head. *Why did you choose me? When you looked at me, what did* *you find? When you realise you don’t like me anymore, what will happen?* “What was your ex like?”

“That’s a good one.” Sokka heaved a big breath. “Headstrong. Or stubborn. Really smart. Also, a massive backstabber.”

“Mine was…” Zuko reconjured Mai in his mind. “She liked things her way.”

“Do you have any dealbreakers?”

“I haven’t really thought about it.”

“I mean, with Jingtao it was like, lying by omission to me for years. More generally, stuff like abuse, unfaithfulness…”

Zuko drummed his fingers against Sokka’s ribs. “Those too, I guess.”

“You should think of some. Doesn’t have to be now. You should know what you want.”

There it was again. “I think I just want you.”

Sokka gave a humourless little huff. “Don’t let me take advantage of your accommodation, Your Majesty.” He never called Zuko that. The title sounded strange coming out of his mouth, Sokka pledging fealty to someone he had all the power in the world over. He sat up and took Zuko’s face in his hands, skimming his thumbs over his cheekbones. “Think about how you want a partner to treat you. Promise me, the next time I ask, you’ll have an answer for me.”

“OK,” said Zuko, taken aback a little. “I’ll think about it.”

---

That night, back at Piandao’s, Sokka knocked on his door while he was in the bath.

“Just a second,” Zuko called. A frisson flickered through him. He climbed out, heated his skin to dry himself, and wrapped himself in the waiting bathrobe before sliding the door open. Sokka crowded up against him, kissing him deep. It was a wonder how the muscle memory of kissing was coming back to him so easily, in a full day.

“Hey,” said Sokka. “Did I interrupt your bath?”

“I was finishing anyway.”

He backed Zuko onto the bed. Druk was lying there and Zuko had to push the disgruntled dragon off to one side. Sokka climbed into his lap to chase after his mouth, one hand twisted in his wet hair, the other mapping the exposed triangle of his chest. Zuko surged against him, a clash of tongue and teeth.

“Slower, slower,” Sokka murmured. He demonstrated: sucking on Zuko’s bottom lip, a wet slide of tongue, a swell rather than a tide. Slow, exploratory. His breath ghosting warm over his mouth.

Zuko copied him. They kissed in this yielding way; Sokka pressed him back into the mattress and Zuko tipped them sideways. He was flushed, lips shining with saliva, his pupils devouring the blue of his irises. Zuko leaned back into his mouth. He could feel his body responding but the sensation was distant, content to enjoy itself in the moment.

“I believe you had a present for me,” said Sokka.

Zuko had completely forgotten. “I thought you didn’t want it.”

“Changed my mind.”

“*This* present isn’t enough?” Zuko said, catching his lips again.

Sokka kissed him back, automatic, like something he couldn’t resist. The feeling of being wanted. Had it always been this heady? “No, I’m insatiable,” he said, but he was grinning, and he was looking right at Zuko’s mouth.

Zuko bit his lip. A little reluctantly, he climbed off the bed and dug through his satchel. The gift was in a little lacquered wooden box and Sokka’s eyes lit up when he saw it. “Happy twenty-fifth,” Zuko said.

Sokka opened it. “La’s *fins*.” It was a silver pocket watch, etched with the face of the full moon. Reverently, he flicked the cover open.

“It tells the time in the middle, but the outer rings are a calendar. See, this one tells you the month, this one tells you the date according to the phases of the moon,” said Zuko nervously.

“Thank you. This is incredible.” Sokka reeled him in for a kiss then went back to poring over the watch face, tracing the ring with the moons.

“I wasn’t sure whether you had a watch already,” said Zuko.

“Are you serious? This one knocks any old clock out of the park. It’s ingenious. You know I’ll probably try to take it apart, though.”

Zuko rolled his eyes with some fondness, hauling Druk into his arms as Sokka twiddled with the mechanism.

“This is so sick. By the way, I hope I didn’t freak you out earlier today,” said Sokka. “At the beach, when I started asking you stuff. I probably should’ve explained a bit more.”

“It’s alright,” said Zuko, though it had alarmed him, like being made to reveal a pai sho strategy at the start of the game.

“No, I mean.” Sokka flopped over onto the bed, clutching the watch to his chest. “I didn’t really expect to date again at this point in my life. Then I ended up returning *your* feelings and… Basically, I wrote up this giant list while I was in Wolf Cove, pros and cons, what I wanted, what I might be getting myself into.”

Zuko held Druk closer. Sokka was staring up into the canopy of his bed. “You know how badly my last relationship ended, though,” he continued, speaking more to himself than Zuko. “I thought, maybe, I should try to do it right this time around, be on the same page early on. Especially since we’re already friends, and we have all the same friends too…”

“What actually happened with your ex?”

“Just all the stuff when I found out about Chen,” said Sokka. “She was really dismissive about it. I guess the writing was on the wall before that. Once she decided something, she tended to think it was the only correct opinion.”

“I don’t think I have enough experience to know what I do or don’t want in a relationship,” said Zuko.

“Hmm?”

“I haven’t… I haven’t dated anyone since Mai.”

“That’s alright.”

But next to Sokka, he was a complete novice. “I got set up on dates a couple of times, before the Council agreed to the surrogacy thing. They were awful, I was just so self-conscious the whole time. And I kept thinking about…” *…about what Mai said when she left.* He shook his head. “You know the whole dating-the-Fire-Lord thing isn’t a walk in the park, right.”

“I did figure. Anything I should know now?”

“Well… the press would probably nose into your life when they find out. The Council has to approve you. Eventually, you’d have to have lessons in court etiquette and stuff. You’d have certain public-facing duties, mostly to support my rule.”

He could see Sokka purse his lips. “Come up here,” he said, lifting an arm up. Zuko went to him; Druk got squished between them, grumbling a little. Sokka pressed a kiss to his head and tickled the dragon for good measure, under the chin where he liked it best. “Look, as much as I want a plan for everything, I have to admit I don’t always have all the answers. But if you wanna try and make it work, then we can figure it out together. Cool?”

It was like Zuko’s inner flame had drifted into his throat, burning up the words there. He clung to Sokka and, robbed of speech, he nodded.

## Chapter 12: Frosty Relations

### Notes

#### Chapter Notes

CW: Some discussion of police brutality.

### Text

Zuko passed the next week or so effervescent with joy, going about the palace with the springing step of an airbender. He spent his waking moments daydreaming about Sokka’s kisses, the imprint of his big hands on his body, replaying their date in his mind. After all the anguish of his crush, this, this. The thing that made romance worth it, the stuff that the playwrights dealt in. That slippery thing, finally in his grasp.

Sokka sent a letter soon after he arrived home, so soon he must have sent Hawky hot on the heels of the airship. *Come back*, was all it said.

For the first time, Zuko detained Hawky before he left. He pulled out a random scrap of paper: *Can’t yet.* He let the ink dry and tucked it into the impatient bird’s message tube, then sent him on his way, watching from the window as he melted into the sky. Making a messenger hawk fly hundred of miles for four words; it was ridiculous, but there was no one around to judge.

The reply was swift, Hawky more disgruntled than ever.

*Hi cutie,*

*Went out with Piandao to do some painting. We saw a super chunky lizard, reminded me of your* *baby. Fat says it’s called a common basalt skink. Ran away when I tried to get close and paint it,* *but I’ve enclosed a close approximation.* &#91;It was enclosed, but apparently Sokka’s animal painting skills had not improved at the same pace as his landscape painting. Zuko stifled a giggle.&#93;

*Thinking of where to take you on another date. The mayor’s daughter’s friend opened a dessert* *shop and I keep thinking about which sweets you’d like best. Tried the cendol today, yummy yummy* *yummy. Will have to sample the rest of the offerings, all in service of the Fire Lord’s tastebuds of* *course…*

*Can’t wait to see you again, I keep thinking about how much fun it was last time. Not to sound* *clingy but don’t make me wait a whole month before I see you again! I’ll be counting down the* *days on my watch.*

*Sokka xxxxxxxxx*

Zuko blushed into his dinner. To know that Sokka walked through Shu Jing and thought about him, that he had fun the way Zuko did at the beach, that he wanted to see Zuko again as soon as possible. He brushed a finger over the signature: Sokka’s name written out rather than stamped with the seal, the trail of kisses. Mai never did anything like this, she was against anything sappy.

So he tucked the letter into his robe and let his heart thump against it through Council the next day. His first reply to Sokka was honest, he couldn’t take another break to visit Shu Jing right away. The Northern Water Tribe had finally agreed to mediate the situation at Yu Dao and by some miracle the Earth Kingdom provincial governor had said he would attend. Zuko and Councillor Hara spent days in briefings to prepare for the journey.

*Have to take a trip to Agna Qel’a, wish us luck,* he wrote before they departed. *Wish us luck. I’ll* *try to come as soon as I get back.*

He looked over the letter. He wanted to impart a little of that giddiness that had buoyed him through the week, give Sokka a taste of just how much he had missed him without seeming completely desperate, return a touch of the affection imbued in his letters. So he added a post script: *I light your little dragon lamp every night before I sleep.*

---

The journey to the North Pole took a full day and night. The accounts bureau had let them charter a smaller airship to make the journey north and for the first time in a long week Zuko was left alone with his thoughts. He wandered through the decks with Druk at his heels, mind circling around Sokka, then what it really meant to date him. Some part of him had recoiled at Sokka’s serious turn on their first date but now, the questions were simmering. In his fantasies, their getting together had been the denouement. He had thought little about the *after*. But now the sparks had caught, it was time to see whether they would build a fire. Then eventually, Zuko thought, if that eventually came, they would have to start telling people. Their friends. Toph and Aang and Katara. Osha. Piandao. The entire world. Would Sokka want to stay in Shu Jing? Eventually, *eventually*, he would have to be absorbed into the court. How would the Fire Nation react to him dating a Water Tribesman? Zuko could think of a hundred rebuttals if he wanted to. But before all of that, Sokka would have to survive all of Zuko’s idiosyncrasies.

Then there was the question that Sokka had posed to him on the beach. How *did* he want to be treated by a partner? It had been something he had tried to avoid thinking about but he could not avoid it forever. Inevitably, his mind circled back to Mai, his sole reference point. On the whole, despite the uncommon circumstances surrounding the start of their relationship, he did love her: the final breakup caught him out of the blue. But he had the benefit of hindsight to admit to himself that their ways of dealing with conflict hadn’t been healthy for his emotions.

What else, though? He needed an expert, he decided.

“Osha?” he said, sticking his head around her door.

“Hmm?”

“You free?”

“What’s up?”

He slipped inside. He shut the door and folded himself onto one of the floor cushions. Druk climbed into his lap, batting at his loose sleeve. “I hope this isn’t invasive or anything, you can tell me to shut up if you want…”

“Sure, what’s up?”

“Um.” He tugged on the ends of his hair. “What do you like about being with Toh-ki?”

“Huh?”

“Don’t worry!” he blurted. It was stupid, of course Osha would be weirded out. He never talked about this kind of thing with her. “Forget I said anything.”

“Nonononononono. Sit back down, boss.” She made an impatient gesture. He lowered himself and Druk back onto the cushion. “Sorry. Just did not expect that. Right, no, let’s chat. What brought this on?”

For a moment, Zuko debated telling her the whole story. But the thing between him and Sokka was newly hatched, perhaps too vulnerable to be shared just yet, so he settled on a half-truth. “I was… thinking back to some of the stuff that happened with my ex. Doing some reflection.” Then, to lend some more legitimacy to the inquiry, he said, “Dr Khoo and I touched on it a bit, when I saw her?”

Osha seemed to buy it. She nodded, considering. The start of her employment had coincided with the death throes of that relationship, so she had never known Mai that well. Zuko hoped that meant she would be objective. “Well, for me, I think Toh-ki is my best friend. I like having her around, we get along, we fit each other’s rhythms.”

“*Rhythms*?”

“Yeah, of course. You know, everyone grows up with a certain rhythm of going about things in their day. How you take care of yourself and your household, how you eat, how you organise finances, how you communicate… You don’t always mesh with someone else’s rhythm.” Osha shrugged. “You have a rhythm. I had to learn it on the job.”

It seemed like such a far cry from what Aang had said about Katara, not being able to imagine a life without her. Zuko thought back to Mai, the very particular way she liked to have her hair and her concealed shuriken, how she wrinkled her nose if he left his clothes lying on the furniture. “So… Mai and my rhythms didn’t match?”

“Maybe? But it’s not about matching rhythms. It’s about compromise and finding ways to make them work together.”

Rhythms and not rhythms, compromise and making rhythms work together? Zuko was bamboozled. There was nothing in *Love Amongst the Dragons* about this.

The confusion must have been written all over his face, because Osha seemed to take pity on him. “I didn’t really know your ex that well, so maybe I’m wrong on this. But in my experience, long-term relationships usually end because there’s a point where someone just doesn’t want to keep compromising.”

“So you always have to compromise.”

“Look,” said Osha quickly, “I don’t think not compromising is always a bad thing. Toh-ki’s ex, before *we* got together, was a fairly selfish person—the way she tells it, at least. Seemed just absolutely certain that everyone else in their life was the problem and completely uninterested in self-improvement. After years of compromising for them, she just… had enough. She didn’t feel like herself anymore in that relationship.”

Never forget who you are, his mother had always told him. With a pang, he recalled Mai’s parting words to him, how she had gripped his hand hard enough to rearrange the bones: *I can’t be true to* *myself when I’m with the Fire Lord*. She had spent years giving herself up for the demands of his station. And now she was islands away, a lifetime away—the life she had traded him for.

“Yeah,” Osha was saying, more to herself than to him. She put her chin in her hand. “I think, in a marriage, you have to be prepared to do some self-betterment. But that should be a two-way street. Ideally going in the same direction. Does that answer your question?”

“Right,” said Zuko. *Did* that answer his question? Osha and Toh-ki never came across to him as particularly sappy personalities, but the way Osha had phrased all of this sounded downright… *unromantic*. Zuko wasn’t stupid, he knew at some point the sheer power of love alone wasn’t enough. But it stumped him, how any of this might make Sokka want to stay with him more than Mai had.

“So Dr Khoo’s treatment has been working?” said Osha.

“Yes?” It felt like an abrupt change of topic.

“It’s good to work through stuff from the past. You seem way happier lately.”

Because of Sokka. All because of him. He felt the fluttery heat rising up the back of his neck. “Yeah, it’s working,” he said. “I’ll just—” He gestured towards the door, casting for a quick excuse to duck out of her scrutiny. “Lunch?”

---

The airship landed on a structure of ice just beyond the harbour of Agna Qel’a. It was still dark. Light was only starting to return after the sunless winter. The air bit any scrap of Zuko’s exposed skin it could find, and he chased it away by letting the breath of fire warm his body. Druk too was feeling warmer around his neck. Zuko had a baby dragon-sized fur coat made for him ahead of the trip but he didn’t seem to need it: the soft scales under his throat glowed faintly in the dark.

The welcoming party herded their group onto a barge, which at the waterbenders’ instigation glided towards the great ice gates of the city. Zuko had been here a few times since his accession but his mind always strayed to the first, when he had *swum* in through the frigid waters in pursuit of Aang. In retrospect, it had been so deranged.

Chief Arnook met them at the steps before his palace of ice, high above the avenues of water. “Fire Lord Zuko. Councillor Hara. And the last of the dragons, if my eyes do not deceive me.”

“Thank you for receiving us, Chief,” said Zuko, making the sign of the flame and bowing. Beside him, Osha presented the chief their gift: an ornate box containing the nation’s finest teas, fresh fruits, and an exquisite headdress. “The Fire Nation is grateful for the ongoing grace and friendship you have shown us.”

“Come inside. We shall commence the discussions when the sun rises from its slumber.”

The palace was much warmer inside than outside, insulated by the thick walls of ice. Zuko let himself fall into a fitful sleep before the sun pulled at his core. This far from the equator, the sunrise was not its usual rush of energy. Rather, it was a slow, lapping tide and he let himself roll slowly out of the bed of furs, trying to chase away the exhaustion of the long journey.

When they gathered in the meeting room, Zuko let his eyes roam over the triptych of tapestries hanging there. The full moon shining over a baby with luminous white hair; the baby now a young woman, kneeling beside a pond; then the woman clad in rippling white robes, drifting in the sky. Sokka had talked about her before, his first girlfriend who turned into the moon. Zuko had been there himself, as the moon winked out of the sky and colour leached out of the night, then came flooding back while he fought Zhao. If she were mortal still, she must be about his age.

Druk’s hiss beside his ear shook him out of his reverie. The Earth Kingdom’s provincial governor had entered with his retinue, gaze fixing on Zuko. Sourly, Zuko recalled the veiled insults the man had thrown at them last time they had met, at Yu Dao after his birthday, the ongoing pain he was inflicting upon the former colonies. The Earth King, of course, nowhere to be seen. Zuko sent a brisk nod in the governor’s direction. Beside him, Councillor Hara and her envoy stiffened. “The daylight hours of springtime are short still,” said Arnook, taking his seat at the head of the table. “Let us not delay.”

Taking Arnook’s lead, the mediation ground onwards.

“Our envoy, who has been residing in Yu Dao for a few months, has grown increasingly concerned about the situation there,” said Councillor Hara crisply. “The Earth Kingdom’s political interventions appear to us to be a violation of the agreement, primarily Sections 1.6 and 2.3 on political autonomy.”

“Chief, will you believe the lies of these colonisers?” said the governor. His angular features betrayed no expression. “Yu Dao belongs to the Earth Kingdom. How can we intervene in a city that we own?”

“The Earth Kingdom does not own the city, your king signed the agreement of a fifty year transition period. The people have risen up against the machinations of Ba Sing Se for months. Street protests weekly, acts of violence carried out against civilians by the guards in green.”

“Civilians? Rioters and dissidents, you mean. *Poisoned* by their former masters.”

Zuko swallowed back the fire leaping to his throat. Druk, never one to hide his feelings, growled. Gratifyingly, Zuko noticed the frown that flashed across the stony face of the governor. Arnook held up a hand. “Stick to the facts. The Earth Kingdom, the Fire Nation, and the former colonies signed the agreement over five years ago, correct? Yu Dao was transferred to the political jurisdiction of the Earth Kingdom alongside its sister city, the former Cranefish Town, and guaranteed political autonomy for fifty years. Governor, how is the current system of organising the legislature compatible with the agreement?”

“Of course it is compatible,” said the governor. “As an Earth Kingdom city, it should be no surprise that the legislature supports the rule of King Kuei.”

“And yet you were happy to let Cranefish go,” Councillor Hara pointed out.

“We amputated the charred limb.” *Breathe, breathe, breathe*, Zuko told himself. “Yu Dao can still be reformed.”

“Because,” said Councillor Hara, “its economic production represents twelve percent of the economic production of the whole Earth Kingdom?”

“Councillor,” said Arnook in warning.

The governor swivelled to fix her with his unchanging gaze. “How typical of the progeny of Sozin to think in such crude, exploitative ways. The people of Yu Dao are of earthen blood.”
