Zuko lifted his chin. “Regardless of blood, governor, the people of Yu Dao are calling for their liberation while facing armed guards in the streets. Their minds and hearts are loyal to their own city. Moreover, the Fire Nation has a responsibility to uphold the agreement we signed together.”

“Facing guards in the streets? Chief, look at the ashmakers play at benevolence. You have forgotten when you choked our rivers with blackened bodies?” He looked Zuko right in the eye, as though he were Zuko’s equal; bristling, Zuko looked back. “The Earth King will not shed tears over a handful of bruised seditionists.”

“Governor, the Fire Nation has undergone intense reform under Fire Lord Zuko,” said Arnook. It was impossible to determine his tone. “The Fire Nation pays you ongoing reparations as a sign of penance. His Majesty is merely trying to enforce the agreement that the Earth King signed as well.”

“Ah yes, that meaningless piece of paper they forced upon us after a century of humiliation. Now, *Your Majesty*, you think to intimidate us with your puny dragon?”

Druk hissed, tongue flickering. Arnook opened his mouth again, but Zuko spoke first. “Enough. That meaningless piece of paper still bears the seal of His Majesty the Earth King. As long as the cinnabar stains that document I am sure he would expect you to pay his office its due respect.” He nodded towards Arnook. “Chief.”

“Thank you, Your Majesty. Allow me to propose a solution.” An assistant passed him a file, which he slid between the parties. “I have heard the people of Yu Dao agitate for a public vote in the manner of their sister city. Rather than the more dubious system implemented by Yee Kwan, my aides wish to propose something more considered. Here are the drafts…”

Councillor Hara met him in his room after sunset. Dinner was reindeer stew but Zuko only managed to force down a few bites before his stomach mutinied. “Thank you, Hara. You spoke well today.”

She sank down onto a chair, bundling the furs thrown over it around her lap. “I know it’s not the solution you were thinking of.” Arnook’s plan was to gather the competing political collectives and unions at Yu Dao, ask them to put forth their own candidates, and offer a vote among the people to fill the legislature with winning members. “It does sound much more thoughtful than what the Cranefish Republic did.”

“It’s not that,” said Zuko. Hangups about the notion of a popular vote aside, he did find it more sensible to vote for individual candidates rather than one party out of two. “It’s just ridiculous we’re talking about them without any Yu Dao residents at the table.”

“The local activists were the first to raise this solution. My envoy has been documenting their wishes.”

Zuko shrugged. He knew why they hadn’t been able to invite any Yu Dao representatives. The provincial governor had refused to attend any talks with “seditionists” present. The current mayor of the city was a writeoff, yet another pawn of the Earth King.

“Your Majesty, you cannot take that awful man’s words to heart,” said Councillor Hara. “You *know* he says all that rubbish to try to stop you from acting. From doing the right thing.”

Zuko didn’t answer. “Are we ever going to find the right solution?” he asked dully.

Councillor Hara’s mouth flattened into a line. “I don’t know,” she said. “The vote would let everyone there weigh in on the issue. But your forefathers threw these places out of balance. As we’ve learnt the past year, it’s not just a matter of restoring the old balance. We’re going to have to forge a new one. And that’s never easy.”

After she left, Zuko sat alone in the dark for a long time, letting Druk’s heated scales warm his hands. The governor’s words rang in his mind. As much as he bristled at the accusations, it was true: how could the Fire Nation escape from its role in violating Yu Dao in the first place? If Sozin had never invaded, they would never be here. They didn’t make manuals for things like this and Zuko was flying blind. It was a conceit to have ever thought he could find the right solution for this city.

---

When he saw Sokka again at Shu Jing, it was a sight for sore eyes. Under Fat’s watchful eyes, he let Sokka take Druk’s basket to his room with a delicious brush of fingers. Once inside, Zuko returned the enthusiastic kisses pressed against his mouth.

“You’re here,” said Sokka, a smile blooming across his face.

“I didn’t make you wait a month.”

“O magnanimous Fire Lord.”

Druk clamoured to go outside; they reluctantly separated to supervise his wanderings through the garden. It was half drizzling, half sunny, flakes of rain beading bright in Sokka’s hair and eyelashes. The hot, soupy atmosphere was loud with cicada-crickets. Zuko demonstrated a little game Rasim had devised, tossing dead leaves to let Druk incinerate them midair with bolts of fire.

“I got you this in the North Pole.” He fished the little pouch out of his sleeve when Sokka took his turn with the leaf.

“Cool,” said Sokka. He opened it immediately. It was apparently a Southern Water Tribe thing to not go waffling on with the *no you’re too generous*es and *you didn’t have to*s, something Zuko had taken a bit of time getting used to when the Fire Nation resumed diplomatic ties with the South Pole. Sokka fished out the little earring, oohing and ahhing as he swapped it out for the current one.

“It’s made of reindeer antler,” said Zuko.

“You’re too sweet.” He scanned quickly for Piandao or Fat, then dropped a kiss on Zuko’s cheek. “How was the North Pole?”

“Awful.” Zuko tossed Druk another leaf, watching it get zapped by dragonfire. He didn’t want to waste his time with Sokka talking shop. “There’s a tapestry of your old girlfriend in the meeting room. That was nice.”

“Princess Yue? Oh. That’s lovely.” Then the wistful look on his face was replaced by something cheekier. “Wait… you’re the second royal I’ve dated?”

“Hopefully I won’t be the second celestial object.”

Sokka gave him a look of abject horror, which broke into a fit of laughter. He tangled his fingers with Zuko’s, coming around to stand toe to toe with him. “Listen, cutie,” he said, and Zuko flushed. “I have a date planned.”

They left the estate before nightfall, leaving Druk under the supervision of Fat. It was dark by the time they arrived at the city centre. Zuko pulled the hood of his cloak low over his eyes and stuck close to Sokka as they navigated the streets.

“Wait here,” Sokka murmured when they stopped in front of a busy restaurant. Zuko stood with the scarred side of his face towards the wall as Sokka ducked inside, then he was beckoning Zuko inside. It was stuffy and packed inside, conversation and the clashing of crockery racketing up a din. Behind the cashier was his portrait, looking sternly over the heads of the patrons. Zuko grimaced and kept his head low, feeling Sokka’s sturdy hand grip in his to lead him through the crowd. Then Sokka was pushing him up a flight of stairs, then down a narrow corridor of peeling blue paint.

“In here!”

It was a small, private dining room. Zuko undid his cloak, flinging it over the back of a rattan chair. “Perks of being friendly with the proprietor,” said Sokka, grinning. “Now we won’t be disturbed, and you don’t need to eat with the hood on. I can order outside as well, so you won’t be seen.”

Zuko hugged him gratefully. “You are the best at planning things. Is this a special kind of restaurant? I thought you weren’t into dinner dates.”

“It’s not really a dinner date, I have something planned after.” He tossed Zuko a wink. “Secret for now! I do love the food here, though.”

It was all the kind of rich vernacular food that the palace kitchens didn’t usually serve. Sokka intercepted the waiter outside and brought in the trays: char kway teow brimming with wok hay and flaky roti canai, extra-spicy sambal on the side. Zuko swallowed Dr Khoo’s pills before digging in.

Something about his tone made Zuko’s prickliness subside. He wasn’t going to force Zuko to tell him and to be honest, he was still wondering over what Osha had said. “She was talking about… rhythms? She said her wife’s rhythm meshes with hers. And sometimes your rhythm becomes stagnant and the other person wants to leave? I dunno. I was just—” He took a deep breath. “All I could think about was Mai. How she felt like she couldn’t be herself with me.”

“Uh, let me try to decode all that,” said Sokka drolly. “Look, I think Osha’s probably talking about how her lifestyle and values match up with her wife’s? That’s an important thing to consider. I mean, if you value closeness, it’s hard to keep dating someone who lives half a world away.”

He made a little sound of confusion. “Why are you saying that like you broke up more than once?” Then, “Wait, you did?”

“Look, I mean. You guys were pretty young and just uh… not at that level of emotional maturity. Or healthy communication? Plus you were in a pretty transitional moment in your life. “Right,” said Zuko. He broke Sokka’s gaze and hid his face in his chest, breathing in his warm boy smell. “I’m scared I can’t change. That I’ll always be stuck in my ways,” he murmured into the fabric there, because it was easier if he weren’t looking into Sokka’s eyes.

“Nonsense,” said Sokka. He gathered Zuko close. “Out of anyone I know, you’ve done the most changing ever.”

As Councillor Po had told it, this was perhaps the most significant of the Grants: a whole architectural venture that would restore one of the most sacred places in the nation.

These days, Crescent Island was only inhabited by the Sages and the construction workers. It did not lie along the airship routes. Zuko’s entourage journeyed to the final stop before the airship crossed the sea to the Earth Kingdom; from there, they boarded a ship to sail across to the island proper. The sea spray kicked up by the prow, the ship crashing along the waves made Zuko almost nostalgic. He could see a shadow of it in Osha’s eyes too. Before they hopped the islands by air, the Fire Nation had been seafaring people.

They passed the uninhabited isles that lay on the stretch of sea, too small to house anything but rocks and birds and scraggly plants, leading like a trail to Crescent Island. Councillor Po exclaimed at one teeming with snapping urvogulls. An Earth Kingdom merchant junk sailed past, with the distinctive sails that looked like the fins of an unagi. The island rose before them at first a haze in the distance, and before long its convex curve of rippling black rock loomed over them. The ship made its way to the northernmost point of the crescent, where the pier awaited.

A pair of shrouded acolytes received them and led them up the ridgeline. Druk, after a few experimental steps, decided to clamber onto Zuko’s shoulders. It made for a difficult journey up. Steam from the old volcano’s vents scudded over the pathway. The old pagoda temple was no more, of course, but rising in its place was something distinctly different. It seemed the eruption had taken a chunk out of the top of the ridge or the engineers had terraced a part of it, because the new temple was a lot *wider*, lower too, hugging the contour of the landscape and black as the stone it was built on. Most of it was covered with bamboo scaffolding and Zuko could see groups of workers climbing up and down the frames. As they approached, the smell of incense descended upon them like a fog.

“Your Majesty, Councillor Po,” said a man who had to be the Head Sage of the temple, coming forwards from the entrance to make the sign of the flame and bow. “How gracious for you to visit this humble temple. Accompanied by the last of the dragons, no less.”

Druk let out a curl of smoke from his nostril. “The honour is ours, Head Sage,” said Zuko, inclining his head. “Please, show me what you have achieved here. Let us pay our respects to the fire spirits.”

They followed the Head Sage inside. He went first, Zuko’s entourage followed, and the acolytes brought up the rear. Two crowned dragons flanked the entrance. “The temple is a pentagonal structure, ringed on the outside by the ritual rooms and dormitories,” the Head Sage explained. “The middle contains a courtyard and the hearth in the centre. Naturally, that was the first part of the temple we constructed.”

They stepped through the shadowed entrance and into the courtyard. It was not the most worshipful atmosphere with the workers shouting in the background and the clanking of hammers and chisels. But the holy fire was crackling in the temple’s hearth and wasn’t that how the earliest firebenders had worshipped the fire spirits anyway? Without fancy temples and altars, just the simple hearth of the home from which life sprang forth. Zuko added his flames to it, and Druk copied him with his own colourful burst.

“Truly amazing, that dragon’s flame.” The colours reflected in the Head Sage’s eyes.

“This place is very different from when I was last here,” said Zuko.

“Your Majesty came to Crescent Island?”

“During my banishment. I had, ah, pursued the Avatar here. I watched when the original temple was destroyed.”

Everyone around him made a noise of surprise. “Your Majesty was—?” said the Head Sage. “This one was not aware of that.”

It always fascinated Zuko, in the aftermath of the war, to see which myths had left him out and which others erroneously slipped him in. “I ran when I saw Avatar Roku. The next thing I knew, the temple was collapsing around me.”

“Yes,” said the Head Sage, leading the way back inside the outer structure. “It seemed Roku was determined to destroy his own temple. But Crescent Island remains a sacred site and the Sagehood is determined to restore a more appropriate version of worship here. His Majesty can see we have changed the design to reflect the forms that the Sun Warriors inspired.”

“The High Sage at Caldera served at the old temple here, is that correct?” said Councillor Po.

“Ah, yes. He was the Avatar’s recommendation for the reformation of the Sagehood.”

The floors were covered in sackcloth and covered in dust or piles of building materials. The acolytes that accompanied them silently marked out a less hazardous route through the walkway. Zuko gathered up the hem of his robes, wishing he had worn more practical clothes. They passed a number of labourers in the long hallway, painting and carving, hammering and plastering. The labourers turned to bow briefly in their direction before turning back to their work. “Where do the workers come from, Your Holiness?” asked Councillor Po.

“Some are from local islands. The spirits also blessed us with the services of some earthbenders.” He gestured towards the great stone walls. “All the terraforming would have taken years by our own manpower. They lay out most of our foundations and basic structures.”

More collaboration between the nations; Zuko nodded approvingly. “Which sections are yet to be completed?” he asked.

“The dormitories were completed first so the Sagehood could resume honouring the spirits here,” said the Head Sage. He opened a set of doors that led to a long corridor. “The acolytes have their cells here.”

It was plain and dim, though clean enough. The Head Sage showed them a small dining hall and other mundane facilities. Outside was a small loamy field, apparently converted from the volcanic rock by the earthbenders so that the Sages and acolytes could grow their own food. Already, a few melons and root vegetables were thriving there, tended to by a small cluster of acolytes. There was a chamber where Roku’s new statue, once it had been cast in bronze, would rest. “There isn’t much complete,” the Head Sage said apologetically as he wrapped up the tour. “But we are very thankful for the additional funds that your Grant provided, Your Majesty. It would have been a shame to let this sacred site, at the very *limit* of our nation, lie vacant.”

“Of course,” said Councillor Po excitedly. “The annals from before the war say that this temple was frequented by sailors.”

The Head Sage nodded. “Yes, this used to be a safe harbour. It would be an honour to revive that practice.”

“Just one final question, Your Holiness,” said Zuko, shifting Druk into a more comfortable position on his shoulders. “The bureau mentioned that this was all initiated by an acolyte, is that correct?”

“Ah.” The Head Sage adjusted his sleeves. “Indeed, it was. This acolyte conducted extensive research on the history of this site and worked with architects and engineers to design this site. It was quite impressive work.”

“From an acolyte, no less. Are they here? May we communicate our thanks?”

The Head Sage was looking distinctly nervous now. Osha straightened, a hand sneaking up to the hilt at her hip. At his ear, Druk growled, ears flattening against his skull. “What is it?” said Zuko, more sharply than he had intended.

The Head Sage mopped his brow. “Ah, this acolyte, Your Majesty… They are very talented, certainly one of the— But this one is not certain whether they would like their identity to be… Your Majesty, you must understand—”

“Don’t bother, Your Holiness. Let me handle it,” said a woman’s voice behind them and Zuko’s blood froze, before his heart started beating again, slamming between his ribs. A voice he could never fail to pick out from the hubbub of a crowd, a voice he would recognise from the ends of the earth, a voice that had matured with age but retained its honed edge. He turned around.

The acolyte who had accompanied them was pulling her shroud off in a single movement that seemed to last an age. Since he last saw her, she had cropped her hair, she had lost some baby fat around her cheeks. But the years did not erase the imperious way her—*their*—amber eyes fixed her gaze on him as she shook the fabric back, habit drilled into them from birth.

Azula. After all this time, Azula.

## Chapter 15: Pent Up

Zuko spent a long time rooted to the spot, gaze locked with his sister’s. His breath felt constricted in his chest, beneath the place she had scarred him, the starburst scar from her lightning. After the months he had spent skinning his knuckles on the gate of the Sages’ convent, after the years of silence that ensued, it was hard to believe this was her, really her, Azula in the flesh, standing before him as a young woman of twenty-three. On Crescent Island, this whole time. Why had she revealed herself to him now, of all times?

For years he had thought about the day Azula might return to him. Now that she was here, he didn’t know what to do.

“Leave us,” said Zuko.

“Your Maj—” said Osha. Only the tip of her blade remained in the scabbard.

“Just a few paces behind, Osha.”

They went. The High Sage was hiding his mouth behind his sleeve, eyebrows slanted with worry; a final glance towards Azula, as though he wanted to say something to her, and then Councillor Po was shepherding him around a corner and out of sight.

Azula kept her expression blank, letting Zuko search her face. With the hair cropped close to her scalp, her head looked strangely small, almost birdlike. The smallest twitch of the eyes, a flicker in her throat. If he didn’t know better he might have thought she seemed almost—uncertain.

“So,” she said, a forced casualness in her voice. “Nice dragon.”

Beside his cheek, Druk gave a fiery little snap. “*Azula*.”

She smiled, more grimace than smile, all teeth. “Surprise. I’m here.”

She looked so different. He couldn’t get over it. The natural sharpness of her features was dulled by the frumpy acolytes’ robes. Her demeanour too was different. When she was at the institution, she had always maintained an aloofness when he met with her, even while the nurses detailed to him the ups and downs of her emotional state as it progressed year by year. When times were particularly bad, there would be a sort of—lifelessness behind her eyes. It was the worst when the nurses told him her flame had gone out. It had haunted him all the way home. Before they released, he had observed some of her old spark again. But none of this *doubt*. Azula was never one for doubt.

“Didn’t you want to see me?” she said.

“Didn’t I—?” Zuko’s voice spluttered to life. “Azula. I knocked on those convent gates for a *year*. You just— You— And now you’re what? A princess playing as an acolyte, playing as an architect?” Beside his ear, he could hear Druk rumble, sensing his emotion. He lay a steadying hand on the dragon—steadying himself or Druk, he didn’t know.

“Let’s walk. I’ll… I’ll explain.” She turned and went a few steps. “Well? Are you coming?”

Zuko exchanged a look with Osha. “She can come as well,” said Azula.

“Better not try anything funny, Your Highness,” said Osha, letting the sword slide back into its scabbard with a *clang*.

“It’s *Sigu* now,” said Azula lightly, going down the corridor. “The Head Sage tried his best with the tour, but he really doesn’t know what he’s talking about. Allow me.”

So they enacted this awful charade, Zuko trailing after his estranged sister as she took him through the building again. “Did you know, the time when you and your precious friends were here, that wasn’t even the first time Avatar Roku had destroyed the Crescent Island fire temple,” she said. They were back at the entrance hall; now that Zuko was looking he could see a rough charcoal sketch adorning the walls. Azula traced it with a slim finger. The nails were blunt. “There will be a mural here depicting these events.”

Zuko exchanged another look with Osha. “And… how did he first destroy it?”

“He mastered the Avatar State.” She circled a scribble on the wall. “His first time. Half the walls came down.”

“So they rebuilt it.”

“Yes.” Some of the old confidence was seeping back into her voice. “The temple was always built on an axis that caught the sun during the solstices and equinoxes. He rebuilt part of it with earthbending. The annals said he dug underground tunnels, but the earthbenders we hired couldn’t detect them. The eruption must have destroyed them. Then there were the later additions, after his death: the gems that refracted the sun would shine onto his statue.”

“But this temple is very different in design.”

“Yes,” said Azula. She led them back to the courtyard. “As His Holiness said, we are modelling it on an older design, one recorded in the annals of the third dynasty.”

“So you are a historian now, as well as an architect.”

“I am an acolyte.” She went ahead, circling the hearth where the mingled flames of Zuko and Druk still crackled. “The island was not always a crescent, you know. There was quite a bit of remodelling to fit the temple on this little ridge. This island used to be part of a larger volcano, and what’s left is this rim after the eruption. The caldera lies under the sea now.”

Zuko wasn’t stupid, he knew what a caldera looked like. He lived in one. “I thought you were going to explain.”

“Hush, brother. Always so bull-headed.” She led them back inside, walking back to the acolytes’ cells.

“And you stay here now?” said Zuko, eyeing the small, bare quarters.

“Why the tone of surprise, brother?”

He bristled. She was poking at him. The Azula he knew would never let herself be surrounded by anything besides the most luxurious items. Even in the institution, she had a beautiful room, furnished with her possessions shipped over from the palace. The possessions she was allowed to keep in there, at least. Now, it was like she was waving her newfound asceticism in his face, *you* *don’t know me anymore, Zuzu*. “So you wanted to be a Sage.”

“Wasn’t really the first pick, I have to admit.”

“You sought me out because you want to come back to the palace now.”

“No.”

Then what did she want? She took them to the anteroom. “Roku’s statue will be here, of course,” she said, flinging a hand to the side. “We’re debating whether the room beside it should be set aside for visits from the Avatar. This site has had bad experiences with two of them, after all. But here—” She went to the wall, and to Zuko’s shock, a flame sprang to her fingertips. So her flame was back now, he thought, back and no longer blue—orange, like the flame of lesser mortals, like the flame she produced as a child. But Azula was paying him no mind. She lit something on the wall and the fire travelled like a thread along some path of oil, illuminating the shape of a dragon’s snarling head.

“Your flame is back,” said Zuko. There was no way she would’ve found the Sun Warriors and the firebending masters, or for her to have been educated by someone who had observed Druk. That meant her flame had come back to her, that she had found her sustainable fuel. “What do you want? After… after all this time?”

Azula was quiet for a moment, her back turned to him. “The fire spirits blessed me again.”

“So now what?” said Zuko. Around his shoulders, Druk sensed his emotion and curled tighter. “You have your flame again, you want to do—what?”

“I couldn’t grow when you were hovering.” *Azula always lies*, but everything in her voice sounded too honest. She wasn’t looking at him; the firelight traced the shape of her profile. All around, the earthy smell of frankincense was blossoming in the heat. “You have to understand. It made me slide back into all my old habits.”

That stung. “But you want to see me again now?”

“I want to take the risk.”

Zuko had often imagined how Azula might reach back out to him. A letter by hawk, an appearance at the palace gates in grand robes befitting a princess, ready to reassume the role she had been born into. He had imagined his own reaction too: tears of joy, embracing his long lost sister, welcoming her back into the fold. But this Azula, in the drab acolytes’ robe, wavering between her old derision and a more unfamiliar, *knowledgeable* figure—he didn’t know what to make of her. He couldn’t shake the feeling that he had been roped into playing a game of pai sho not as a player, but as a piece.

“I don’t know, Azula,” he found himself saying. “I think I need more time.”

Her face shuttered. Her throat worked. “Let me show you the ceilings of the vestibule.”

So they went on, and it was like she had never shown the soft parts of her belly at all. Pointing out decorations and where the wood carvings would go, which bits would be gilded in precious metal. Zuko took the emotion swelling in his chest and compressed it into a ball, small as it would go, and ignored where it sat lodged beside his heart.

“You put so much effort in,” said Zuko, eyeing the blank spots that she was promising would be filled with the greatest craftsmanship the archipelago could offer. “What if it erupts again?”

Azula looked at him then. Her gaze was level, level where Zuko was roiling inside. “Destruction never stopped us from rebuilding what belonged here.”

When Zuko’s party finally left, the Head Sage chased them halfway down to the pier. For his station, it was undignified. “Your Majesty,” he said, panting. “Please.”

Zuko drew himself up imperiously. “What is it, Your Holiness?”

“About Sigu Azula, please understand.” He bowed low, the sage’s cap almost toppling from his head. “Please, Your Majesty, this humble one asks that you not punish her.”

Zuko looked down his nose at the man. He stayed silent, because silence would be his weapon.

The Head Sage continued, a nervous babble. “She has been a valuable asset to the Sagehood. One of the most talented acolytes to have come through our gates.”

“Of course,” said Zuko; he would expect nothing less from the girl who conquered Ba Sing Se aged fourteen.

“Your Majesty, she has been instrumental in revitalising the Sagehood. The spirits touched her. She has changed. This one can vouch for her. You’ll come back, won’t you? She wants—”

Zuko let out a breath of smoke. “I’ll consider it, Your Holiness.” He turned with a sweep of his robe, and stalked the rest of the way down.

---

“Do you want to talk about it?” said Osha, when they were back onto the ship, the sea spraying high on either side.

“Not really,” said Zuko.

---

The relief that washed over Zuko when he saw Sokka almost knocked him off kilter. Osha, with her foresight, had organised a detour for him at Shu Jing while the rest of the party travelled back to the capital. But they hadn’t accounted for an audience, an audience that was trading… significant looks.

From Piandao: “This is certainly not the first time two students of mine have fallen in love.”

“Hey loverboy,” said Osha to Sokka, and then dissolved into a flurry of laughter.

And Fat, with a completely impassive expression, said, “Your Majesty, you may stay in Sokka’s room if you wish.”

“Yeah, everyone knows,” said Sokka mildly, coming around to loop an arm around Zuko’s shoulder to peck his flaming cheek. “Wanna get out of here? I’m gonna run an experiment.”

On the way to Shu Jing, Zuko had some hazy intention of making Sokka fuck him until all thoughts of Azula had leaked out of his brain. But with everyone nosing in, it was easier to leave his bags with Fat and trail out of the estate after Sokka.

Sokka had brought along a little cart, towing some large object covered in an old sack. “Missed you,” said Sokka, smiling into the breeze. “Piandao had me doing White Lotus correspondence all morning, ugh. You been good?”

“Fine,” Zuko said, tangling his fingers with Sokka’s. Druk tramped beside them, sniffing at the grass. “What’s this?”

“Been working on this baby.” He gestured to the cart with their linked hands. “You won’t happen to know what *harnessing the power of the volcano* means, right?”

“In what context?” said Zuko.

“Mechanics? The industrialisation right before your grandfather’s rule? Found it in an old book…”

“I thought you meant firebending.”

“Shame.”

They went to a hillside not far from the hot springs, fogged with all the steam gusting out of the vents. Sokka unloaded the cart, which held some sort of turbine that Druk flickered his tongue at.

“Do you want me to help?” said Zuko, hovering.

“Yeah.” Sokka checked his pocket watch, then handed him a notepad and a stick of graphite. “I’ll call out some numbers and you just need to fill in these charts. Sit a bit away.” He pulled on a pair of goggles and gloves, and pushed the turbine over a vent. “I’ve already run a control with just coal.”

It was an uncommonly dull process. Zuko jotted down the numbers that Sokka threw at him. The smell of sulphur and flitting memories of Azula made his head a cloud, and he twitched when Sokka came back up to collect his notes.

“That… worked, right?”

“Yeah,” said Sokka, but he looked unhappy as he scanned the numbers. “It’s not as effective as I thought.”

“There’s a reason why we switched to coal.”

“And the *reason* is because you colonised coal-producing colonies and imported that at reduced cost. And you no longer control their economies, so it no longer makes sense to do that.”

He was distracted the whole way back, mumbling under his breath and occasionally stopping to scribble something down in his notepad. Zuko trailed behind, frustration mounting. Druk wove chittering between his ankles. When they finally arrived at Sokka’s room, Zuko grabbed him and crashed their mouths together.

“*Mmf*, hello!” said Sokka.

Zuko licked forcefully into his mouth before pulling back. “I need you to fuck me.”

“Right now? Are you—“

“I’ve practised, I’ve been practising. I can go and clean myself now…”

“Um, Druk is literally right here.”

“I can put him with Osha. Come on, please.”

“There’s no rush.”

“I *need* it.”

Sokka grabbed him by the wrists, holding him back. He looked at Zuko, really looked at him. Alarm gathered between his eyebrows. “Are you OK?”

“What do you mean,” said Zuko brusquely. “I’m fine. Come on. Don’t you want it?”

“You’re *really* bad at lying,” said Sokka. “Tell me what’s wrong. Are you mad?”

Zuko blinked. “What? No. Not at you, at least…”

“Who are you mad at? Council stuff?” Sokka seemed to sense his hesitation, because then he said, “You don’t have to tell me if you’re not ready.”

“I just want you to fuck me until I can’t think about it,” Zuko said mulishly. Now that Sokka was halting his advances, he started to feel a little stupid, a lot embarrassed. He leaned in again, but Sokka’s grip was firm.

“I don’t think we should do that.”

Annoyance flared. “You don’t understand—”

Sokka’s eyes narrowed. “Believe me, I do.”

Zuko wrested himself free, sinking grouchily into Sokka’s bed. Sokka sat beside him, mouth a hard line. “I hate that you always make me talk about stuff. It’s just like at the beach. You keep making me.”

Sokka looked incredulous. “The—the *beach*? Wh— Zuko, you said you were OK with it. I literally asked you back then. I can’t believe— And, and anyway, I *said* that you didn’t have to tell me.”

“But it won’t make sense if I don’t. So you just want me to tell you anyway,” said Zuko bitterly, steeling himself for the retort. “And you’re one to talk, you never told me about the rumours for *months*. And everyone else knew. I was the last to know.” He felt it all flooding back, everything he had fucked up with Mai. He felt like he was watching everything happen in slow motion with no way to stop it. He didn’t know how. Sokka would snap back, and Zuko would snap back, Sokka would see him for who he was, and then dump him. Everything that he had feared coming to this. Then Sokka’s hand came down on his, hard.

“Come on,” said Sokka, voice controlled. “I don’t want to fight.”

Something in Zuko snapped. He didn’t want to fight either, he realised. He looked at Sokka, and then his image blurred with tears. Sokka’s hands came up and held him as Zuko heaved into his shoulder.

“There, there.”

Zuko cried harder. He was being so kind, even though Zuko had just acted like a total jerk. He clenched his hands in the fabric of Sokka’s shirt and felt Druk twisting up into his lap, wrapping around his waist, warm and solid.

“I saw Azula,” he hiccuped. “I saw Azula and I don’t know what to do.”

He felt Sokka still. “What?”

“She was at Crescent Island. Apparently she spearheaded the whole project. I just— I don’t know, I—”

“Shh, shh, hey. It’s OK. I can’t believe— I can’t imagine how you must’ve felt.”

“I don’t know. I don’t know, I don’t know how I—”

“Hey, you don’t have to know right now.”

“She talked to me about *architecture*.” Zuko shook his head. “It just felt so, so—surreal. Like it wasn’t her. She didn’t feel like…”

“Do you… know what she wanted?”

“I don’t know.” He took a deep breath, forcing the air into his heaving lungs. Druk nudged against him, whuffling, and he put his arms around the dragon. Sokka produced a cloth from somewhere, dabbing his eyes and nose. “She didn’t want to come back to the capital. I think she wanted me to see her again.”

“You don’t have to. If you’re not ready.”

“But, but… I wanted to see her again for so long. And now she’s here, I don’t even… Isn’t that so stupid?”

“It’s not stupid.”

“I just thought, I thought—” What had he thought? Azula would always evade predictability, it seemed. “I thought I’d always welcome her back with open arms. Like my uncle did for me. But I can’t. I’m nothing like him.”

“Well, your uncle’s a special soul. I’d feel pretty weird if Katara blanked me for years and suddenly came back.”

Zuko blew his nose wetly. “I feel like I don’t know her anymore.”

“It doesn’t mean you don’t love her,” said Sokka.

That was the crux, wasn’t it? That was why it hurt so much. “Why are you being so nice to me? I was such a jerk.”

“Cos I care about you, silly.” Sokka sighed. “Hey. I’m sorry you felt… obligated to share stuff. Especially if you weren’t ready.”

“I thought… that’s just what you wanted.”

“Look. I did, alright. But what I want doesn’t always have to be what you want. Just because we’re dating doesn’t mean you don’t have your own boundaries. Just—tell me, alright? If I do something you don’t like. Communication is important. Don’t just bottle it up and get mad at me after. I won’t lie, that was kinda hurtful.”

Zuko had been trained in bottling up all his life, bottling up and then exploding at the worst times. “I’m sorry I snapped at you. It’s not that I didn’t want to tell you. I just—“

“—didn’t know how to say it?”

“I guess.”

Sokka hummed. “I’m sorry I didn’t tell you about the uni stuff earlier. I guess I didn’t know how to bring it up either.”

“I shouldn’t have said that, Sokka. That was really, really…”

Sokka shushed him. “We’ll work it out.”

Was it really OK? Was everything forgiven, just like that? “I’m sorry.”

Sokka was quiet for a moment. “It’s been over a year since the Ba Sing Se stuff. I should be over it, right?” He withdrew his arms, hugging himself around the middle. “It’s not like I’m ever against having sex with you. But I don’t want it to become what it was like for me when I was there. Sometimes I’ll—”

“Like what?”

“I dunno.” Sokka flashed him a grin, humourless. “Liking that people wanted me?”

Zuko took his hands, folding their fingers together. He didn’t quite know what to say, but he wished that Sokka wouldn’t beat himself up over this, he wished that Sokka knew that Zuko wasn’t upset by it and it didn’t change how Zuko felt about him. “I’m sorry I said it,” he settled for. “It was insensitive. I don’t think it was your fault. I really don’t.” He huddled close. “Are you alright getting back into that stuff now? The experiments and all?”

“I think I don’t want them to take that away from me anymore,” said Sokka. “I’m… figuring out a way.”

He sounded uncertain. They ended up embracing, touch in lieu of speech, Sokka threading his fingers through Zuko’s hair, Zuko tracing the line of Sokka’s spine, Druk looped heavy around both of them.

“You don’t have to,” Zuko said into his sternum. After crying, he felt wrung out. “She’s not your sister. You don’t have to deal with her if you don’t want.”

“Yeah. But we’re an *item* now, aren’t we?” Sokka squeezed his arms, bracing. “You don’t have to do it alone.”

*I could love him*, Zuko realised; it hit him like a wall of moving earth. It was so soon, *too* soon, and he shoved the thought away to inspect later. Instead, when he found his voice again, he said, “I want to go public with you.”

“Really?”

“If—um, if you want. You’ll have to meet the Council and stuff and I think someone will run you through protocol and actually I never really considered all the. Um. I mean, you don’t have to—”

Sokka kissed him. “Alright. OK. Yes!” he said. “Yes. Tui and La. Yes. Let’s do it.”

---

“Um, Osha,” Zuko murmured at dinner. “You don’t suppose I could get another appointment with Dr Khoo?”

She searched his face. “You alright?”

“Yeah, it’s just about—Azula.”

“I’ll look into it,” she said. A moment of hesitation, and then she gripped his shoulder. “Dunno if she’ll have a slot free right now. But I’m glad you have someone you want to talk to.”

Sokka cornered him after dinner, pressing a kiss against his hair. “It’s good you did that. Proud of you. Feel better?”

Zuko put his arms around him, breathing. He smelled like the camphor incense Piandao was always burning, plus his own deeper muskier scent. “Yeah. I’m sorry again for—“

Sokka tipped his head back, pressing the pad of his thumb against Zuko’s mouth. “Hey, don’t need to apologise again. Look,” he said, his voice dropping, “I can take you up on your offer now, if you still want.”

“My—? *Oh*. Right.” Zuko bit his lip. “You still want to?”

“If you do as well. Only if you feel better.” Was Zuko imagining it, or did he look a little nervous too? He tilted up and pressed a kiss to Sokka’s cheek.

“I do want.”

---

“Baby dragon’s good?” said Sokka, when Zuko slipped back into the room after prepping himself.

“Yup, tucked in with Osha.”

Zuko tugged the thin robe tighter around himself, uncertainty setting in, but from the bed Sokka held out grabbing hands and he went gladly into them. Straddling Sokka’s lap, bearing down to kiss him, feeling Sokka’s hands rake up his body to the collar of his robe to tug it off his shoulders. Sokka pressed a slow, open-mouthed kiss against the scar Azula had left on his chest, as though he wanted to imprint himself over it, supersede the hurt.

“You’re pretty,” he breathed. “And you, you fucking *practised*. The thought of you fucking yourself for weeks to prepare for my cock, *fuck*.” He lathed his tongue over a nipple, making Zuko jolt. “Did you like it? Did you think of me when you put it up yourself, when you—went to choose it? Did you go out and pick a dildo you wanted? Did you compare them to my cock? The Fire Lord out in the markets, comparing which dildos you wanted to sit on?”

“I, ah—” Zuko gasped. “I got Osha to buy them.”

Sokka stopped, levelling him a flat stare up the line of his body. “Seriously?”

“I told you, I don’t shop.” He pushed up, rolling Sokka under him, laughing a little. There was so much he wanted to say but the words were lodged in his throat, all disarticulated. He kissed Sokka and hoped he could taste the sentiment on his tongue. *You care so much about everyone. You hold* *so much in your heart. You’re too good to me. You make me want to be better.*

“Spoiled,” Sokka whispered. He tucked a strand of hair behind Zuko’s ear and he looked fond, impossibly fond, smiling wide and not even trying to act seductive. It hit Zuko again—this was his boyfriend, his smart and funny and handsome boyfriend, who was first and foremost his friend. “Are you gonna show me those moves?”

“No,” said Zuko. He sat back, robe pooling at his waist, pouting a little. Sokka raised an eyebrow. “I’ve done enough work. I want *you* to open me up.”

The eyebrow went higher. “You bossy little—” Sokka tackled him down and hiked up a leg, fishing around for his ointment. He flicked the container open, looking Zuko right in the eye. Zuko’s breath caught in anticipation. “Do you think Piandao and Fat ever explored each other’s bodies like this?”

Zuko was appalled. “Get off me.”

Laughing, Sokka dodged his flailing leg and pressed a lubed fingertip to his hole. It was a full body shudder that ensued. “You sure about that?” he said, smirking.

“OK,” said Zuko. His voice came out raspier than he would have liked. “I could be convinced otherwise.”

---

After Sokka wiped them down and threw the condom into the wastebasket, Zuko started to giggle.

“What’s so funny?” said Sokka, mock-offended. He discarded the towel and climbed back into bed, all beautiful exposed skin and muscles, and tucked himself under the arm Zuko held out. He nuzzled in.

“I dunno.” Zuko let himself luxuriate in the sensation, the sloppy ache of his asshole, the sated exhaustion settling into his limbs, the fizzing lightness sparking in his core. It was good, it was fucking good, he should’ve known and hell, if he had known earlier, he would’ve spent all those pathetic celibate years getting fucked out of his mind. It was just so different with a guy, a guy who knew what he was doing with his dick, compared to a dildo he had to control himself, getting held and held *down* while—Zuko groaned. “Oh noooo.”

“What?”

“I have to tell my mother.”

“About?”

“Us. Being boooooyfriends.”

“That’s… fine?”

“No, she totally thought we were together when you came over at New Year. She’ll be so *smug*.”

Sokka snorted. “You know, I kinda got that vibe.”

“*What*?” Zuko pouted. “How did you—”

“Tui and La.” Sokka made a strangled noise then tackled him into a cuddle, weighing him down with his limbs. “You’re so fucking cute. I can’t believe getting dicked down has got you so loopy. How did you last so long without getting fucked?”

Zuko didn’t know either. Now he’d had a taste of this, he wanted it forever. Could the Fire Lord spend the rest of his days taking dick instead of doing politics? Could he negotiate with the Council to let him sit on Sokka all throughout their meetings? World peace. “I’m not loopy. I’m *happy*,” said Zuko against his forehead, petulant.

Sokka made another strangled noise. “I’m keeping you.”

“You already did. You’re my *boyfriend*.”

Sokka sighed, chest heaving against Zuko’s chin. “True that.” And Zuko heard the smile in his voice.

## Chapter 16: Reap

“You’re back,” said Dr Khoo. “How are you feeling, Your Majesty?”

“Sorry,” said Zuko, settling down into the cushion. He had been fretting on the airship ride to Shu Jing, and now he let the soothing scent of the incense wash over his nerves. “It’s been a while.”

“Don’t apologise. Any doctor wants to see their patients as little as possible, because it means they are in good health.”

She chuckled. Zuko shifted. He had skipped their last scheduled appointment because he felt good, the first time in a long time. But after everything that had happened with Azula, he was now taking his longest leave in, well, forever, really. For the first time, he was fully leaving the nation in the hands of the Grand Chancellor. “You really helped me last time with the, um, talking. I think I want to do more of that.”

“I’m glad to hear that it helped you. What troubles you today, Your Majesty?”

“There’s… something else now. I don’t know what to do, really.” He took a deep breath. “I saw my sister again. Azula. Or, I guess, she stopped hiding from me.”

“Oh.” Dr Khoo’s brows knitted, her hands came heavily down into her lap. “My my, Your Majesty, so much has been happening to you lately.”

“Yeah.” Zuko let his shoulders droop. Inside the warm, dark confines of the clinic, it felt safe to let the exhaustion show. Beside him, Druk whined. The dragon’s chin settled on his thigh, heated and thrumming. “Also, Sokka and I got together and it was—well, it’s been really good, actually.” He ducked his head, flushing.

“Oh? Congratulations, Your Majesty.”

“But Azula coming back threw everything off kilter. We had a—a fight.”

Dr Khoo hummed. “Very understandable, when emotions are rough.” She dipped her brush into the inkwell. “Tell me a bit about what you’ve been feeling.”

Zuko took a deep breath.

“I always imagined what it might be like if—*when* she came back to me,” he said. “I thought I would always welcome her back with open arms, like my uncle did for me after I… after I betrayed him. But it’s just been hard to accept. I didn’t know how to feel when I saw her.”

“That’s very understandable, Your Majesty. You must feel conflicted about why she might have come back to you after so long without communication.”

“Yeah,” said Zuko. “I do. She told me she couldn’t grow while I was there, that she could risk seeing me again now.”

“Risk?”

“That’s what she said.” Zuko breathed out in frustration, a little smoke coming out of his nostrils. He twisted his fingers into Druk’s short mane. “After all those times I visited her at the institution, and she just accuses me of holding her back.”

Dr Khoo nodded, a little frown coming between her eyebrows. “That must have been hard to hear.”

“Yeah,” said Zuko. “It just felt like—I dunno. Like she couldn’t accept that I wanted to see her get better.” He looked away.

“Hmm.” Dr Khoo tapped the end of her brush against her desk. “Do you feel responsible for her recovery, Your Majesty?”

Taken aback, Zuko said, “What do you mean?”

“Exactly that. Do you feel you must be an instrumental part of her recovery?”

“I—I—” Zuko stuttered. “My uncle, he was there for me all while I—I renounced the ideology of my father. I just thought, I thought, Azula has no one else… All her friends, well…”

“That might be so,” said Dr Khoo. “But did she want that?”

“I—” Zuko cleared his throat. “I didn’t want my uncle around all the time either, when I was in exile. But then I was grateful for it, when I looked back.”

She hummed. “This is a working theory. But perhaps your sister needed space from you. You were a frequent visitor while she was institutionalised, she had no way to—pardon me—evade you. Perhaps she had never intended for you to be part of her journey of healing. Sometimes, people do make that choice. When she was released, she was free to choose.”

Free from *him*. The hurt pulsed in his core. “Azula was always the brilliant one.” Burning brighter, burning hotter. “*She* was meant to be Fire Lord. I wasn’t supposed to be anything, ever. And I just… took it from under her nose.”

“You don’t need to seek forgiveness from her for the way history unfolded, Your Majesty.”

“My uncle said it was my destiny,” Zuko said. “But I never thought Azula’s was… *this*. Her flame isn’t even *blue* anymore. It looks so… normal. So ordinary. She was never meant to be ordinary.”

“But she controls her life again, and the Sagehood is the path she chose. If she is revealing herself to you again, know that it is of her own accord, Your Majesty.” And Zuko thought, actually thought about how Azula was at Crescent Island. She had been knowledgeable, in command of the project. Something to occupy her, somewhere she could exercise her talents. Even the Head Sage seemed to like her. “You don’t have to accept the invitation. If you don’t feel up for it yet, *you* also have the right to have space.”

“She’s my sister.”

“And that need not change.” She set aside a sheet of paper and turned to regard him fully. “How has your digestion been treating you through this tumultuous time?”

“My—” Zuko felt his stomach. A little bloated, maybe, but none of the gurgling and pain and fullness that had plagued him before. “It’s—” He hadn’t thought about it in so long, he realised. *Because* it hadn’t bothered him. “It’s been… fine, actually.”

“Very encouraging, Your Majesty. That is a good sign.”

“Yeah,” said Zuko with confused wonder. “It worked.”

“Of course, Your Majesty. Sometimes treatments do.” Smiling, she made a final note on her sheet of paper, then moved it to the side for the ink to dry. “There is a suggestion I would like to make.”

“What is it?”

Dr Khoo hung up her brush and folded her hands together. “This talking therapy has been helping you, correct?”

Zuko nodded.

“To be honest, it is not really my area of speciality,” she said, “but I think it would be beneficial for you to continue. There is a doctor I can refer you to, a doctor of the mind. A Northern Water Tribeswoman, who fortuitously moved to the main island in the last few years. Not quite in the capital, but close enough.”

Zuko took a deep breath. He trusted Dr Khoo, that was why he didn’t mind sharing these things with her. Could he manage it with someone else? Would it be the same? “I don’t know…”

“No pressure at all, Your Majesty. But this specialist is much more skilled than I am in these matters.” She smiled thinly. “This particular strand of medical knowledge from the Fire Nation didn’t survive your forefathers, I’m afraid.”

She wrote the referral for him and he accepted it with nervous hands. “If that is all, Your Majesty, this may be the last time I see you,” she said.

He would miss her, he realised. He made a mental note to ask Osha to send a fruit hamper of his appreciation. “Thank you for all of your services, doctor,” he said, making the sign of the flame and dipping his head low. “You do something special here.”

“No trouble at all, Your Majesty. I want to see you happy and well.” She bowed too and smiled in her placid way. “You have a lot of heart and great fortitude. Guard these qualities well.”

Zuko nodded though he didn’t quite understand, and stood to leave.

“Will you be seeing your sister again?” Dr Khoo called after him.

Zuko paused. He had been mulling it over, but his heart was still torn. “There’s something I need to do first.”

---

“Everyone’s looking at us,” Zuko hissed.

“Just don’t look at them,” said Sokka. “Or pretend they’re naked.”

“I’m not going to *pretend* that! They’re my subjects!”

In the royal box of the local theatre in Hira’a, it was impossible to evade the hundreds of eyes swivelled in their direction. Zuko felt like an insect on display. Like the absolute pain he was, Sokka swooped in close to murmur, “I know who I’d rather imagine naked.”

“*Shut*—”

On his other side, Kiyi kicked his leg. “Shh. The performance is starting.”

They shut up. Around them, the lamps extinguished and plunged the theatre into darkness. Sokka’s hand shot out to hold his. A blare of horns and drums, and then the stage curtains swung apart. They watched as Zuko’s mother—no, the *Dragon Empress*—took the stage, launching into the shrill opera of the opening chorus.

It was a good production. The staging was done just right, making the most of what had to be a small budget. There were interludes with wayang puppets, which Zuko thought was a tasteful touch. The tiny theatre was packed and the audience surged to its feet to applaud when the actors came out to bow, and then Mother and Ikem were linking hands and pointing towards the box, and everyone turned around to gawk at the Fire Lord and applaud in his direction.

After the last of the audience members trickled out, they wended their way backstage. Unlike the emptying stands, it was abuzz with activity. Stagehands hurried to pack away the props for another evening, costumes were strewn everywhere. Zuko ran an appraising eye over Sokka, making sure he looked presentable.

“Is that—” he said. Sokka looked over. “Is that my vest?”

Sheepishly, Sokka ran his thumb over the lapel. The garment was the customary deep Fire Nation red, the lapels gold and embroidered with dragons—that was the giveaway. He looked *good* in it. “Yeah. I kinda stole it from you when you last visited.” He was flushing. “You don’t mind, do you?”

“I don’t,” said Zuko, and he *really* did not.

“You can steal something of mine if you want.”

“I’ll hold you to that,” said Zuko, eyes lingering.

“*Do* hold me.”

“Oogies,” Kiyi complained.

They found Mother’s dressing room. She had changed back into her everyday clothes, and Ikem was pulling a myriad of pins out of her hair. “Kiyi, boys,” she said.

“That was fantastic,” Sokka enthused. “So much better than the one at New Year! I totally see why Zuko loves this play so much.”

“Thank you, Sokka.” Ikem pulled out the last of the pins and she got up, coming over to take his hands. “Let me properly welcome you into the family—which we didn’t get to do last time,” she said, with a pointed look at Zuko.

“That’s kind of you, auntie,” said Sokka with affected bashfulness.

“You just assumed, Mama,” said Zuko. “I would’ve told you if we were dating. Like now.”

“Now, now,” said Sokka, “you can’t blame auntie if you were so obviously infatuated with me over New Year that she thought we already *were* together.”

Zuko’s entire face went hot. “I was *not* obvious!”

“You were,” said Kiyi, deadpan.

Mother and Ikem chuckled. “And how have you been lately, Sokka?” Mother pressed.

“Not bad. Just at Piandao’s. Running a couple of experiments, Zuko knows all about it.” Sokka jostled his elbow against Zuko’s.

They were all smiling. “Shall we?” Ikem said. “I’m sure we’re all tired tonight. There’s dessert waiting for us at home before we go to bed.”

They traced the route back to the modest house Mother and Ikem kept just beyond the Hira’a town centre, shrouded by trees, almost a part of the forest. Mother’s sanctuary where she hid from Zuko’s father, in those terrible years of his reign. It was a poky place. He had insisted, a few times, that he could have the house rebuilt for her, renovated, refurnished—she always refused. There was a room set up for him and Sokka. Two separate bedrolls. That was typical of Mother. And there was dessert as promised, silky honey-sweet taho that did little to settle the nerves building in Zuko’s core. He found Sokka’s grounding gaze, then cleared his throat.

“Mama, can I talk to you a bit? Outside?”

He left the rest of his bowl for Sokka. She followed him out onto the deck, frowning. “What’s the matter, son?”

“Mama, I saw Azula again.”

Mother did not speak for a long time. Then, she said, “Really?”

“You know how she joined the Sagehood… She’s at Crescent Island, leading the construction there. The temple.”

“I see.” There was barely any light. The moon could not shine through the surrounding canopy. He couldn’t make out her expression at all. “What did she want?”

“I don’t know,” said Zuko. “She didn’t want to come back to the palace. I—I didn’t know how to feel. I thought—”

“Azula isn’t like you, son. You are right to be cautious.”

He didn’t know what to expect telling his mother about Azula. Comfort? Understanding, maybe? Or just acknowledgement. “It’s not like that, Mama. She initiated the conversation. I just—I want to help her. But I don’t know what to do, what to feel.”

Mother was quiet for a while. “She wanted to be left alone. There’s no reason why that should not continue.”

An anger flared in his core, he felt the irrational urge to defend Azula. “Why would she come back to me if she didn’t want to reconnect?”

“What is there to reconnect? She did—so many things. She burned your chest with lightning.”

Zuko knotted his fingers into the fabric of his sleeves. “She was fourteen! She’s still my sister. Your daughter. Don’t you love her?”

“I do.” It was quiet, almost reluctant. Then, louder, “I love her by acknowledging where I cannot help her.”

It was then Zuko came to this sickening realisation: that, for all his resemblance to his father, it had never been him that his mother had looked upon and seen Ozai.

“She’s changed. She’s different. You can’t blame her for how she was as a kid,” said Zuko, a challenge in his voice. He wasn’t sure whether he believed himself, even. “I spoke to her.” “Alright,” said Mother. She was looking away, as though the talk about her eldest daughter bored her. “Then go back to see her.”

“I will.” He hadn’t made up his mind before, but now he knew what he had to do. He clamped down the fire that threatened to spring from his palms, he resisted the urge to turn and stalk back inside. “I will.”

---

“You’re back,” said Azula.

There was dirt smeared all over her hands; she was twirling a long pair of gardening shears that set his guard on edge. All around them, the wind was howling. It had blown off her shroud. Zuko noted the hint of genuine surprise in her voice, then her eyes flicked over to Sokka. “Oh, you too.”

“What’s that supposed to mean?” Sokka said.

“Of all your best buddies to bring along, brother, I would’ve thought you’d ask the Avatar.”

Zuko sighed. The sun was glaring down, beating heavy on his head, and he tried to chase away the beginning stages of a headache. “Sokka was in the area.” They had agreed to not tell her right away that they were together but knowing her, it was only a matter of time before she’d figure it out. “Is there anywhere to—talk?”

She twirled the shears again. “How’s your gardening?”

“My wh—”

Sokka’s hand came down hard over his shoulder. “Great, thanks. Lead the way.”

Zuko dismissed his guard, who left reluctantly. Azula took them to the vegetable patch and plonked a basket in Zuko’s arms. “Pick the ripe chillies. You know what that looks like, right? Red?”

“I’m not stupid!”

He plucked one from the plant. Druk traipsed off into the field, leaving big talon prints through the soil. Azula opened her shears and started to hack away at an overgrown vine. “OK, I’m here. Talk.”

“What, no architectural lectures today? Didn’t *you* want to talk?”

“Well, *you* came here.”

“So what was all that about, last time?”

“Maybe I was just sussing you out. How about that?”

They glared at each other through the leaves of the chilli plant. Sokka snorted.

Azula’s glare flicked towards him. “*What*.”

“Sorry,” he said, stifling another laugh. “It’s just, man. I just realised. This is probably how people see me and Katara. When we have a petty argument.” Azula glared harder. “Sorry, shutting up now.”

Zuko felt a mounting exasperation towards both of them. “I told Mama I saw you,” he snapped, yanking another chilli off the branch.

Azula’s expression went blank. “Right.”

“Is that why you stopped talking to me?”

Azula went back to snipping. “What did she say?”

“I’m not telling you.”

“*What did she say.*”

“You don’t want to hear it.”

“I’ll decide that.”

“And I won’t say.”

She sat back, mouth twisting. “Figures.”

“How can you even hold this over me?” said Zuko. He hadn’t wanted to argue coming here, he really hadn’t, but somehow Azula was finding all of his buttons and jabbing with abandon. “Father favoured you. He always did. You never had a problem with it when she was gone.”

“He didn’t.”

“Didn’t what?”

“*Favour me*, moron.”

Zuko was going to upend his chilli basket. “Are you serious, Azula? You just forgot everything he used to—“

“He. Didn’t.” *Snip, snip*. “By the spirits, Zuko, after all these years and you’re still so damn stupid.” A growl of frustration. *Snip*. “He used me. He never cared. You—” *Snip, snip, snip*. “At that accursed *place*, when they started to unravel me from the inside, I realised. And I was so— I never felt—” She sucked in a noisy breath, gathering the fallen branches. “You, you threw him in prison for the use of child soldiers! And you still don’t get it. Don’t get me started on dearest *Mother*.”

She set the sticks on fire, a conflagration that burned white and intense in her arms for a second before disintegrating to ash, flaking into the dirt. Then she started to cry. It was hard to watch, like he was seeing something not meant for him, especially since she was trying not to. Great hiccuping breaths, like she was trying to suck the sobs back into her lungs. “You always had Mother, and then Uncle. And always more your mother than mine, more your uncle than mine,” she gritted out between breaths. “This was a mistake. I thought at least—I’d always have Father. And then *they* made me realise…” She shook her head. “He always just—he was going to crown me, but he, he *used* me, and I—” She was starting to hyperventilate. Zuko looked wildly around to Sokka, unsure of what to do.

“Azula,” he said, unsure whether he believed it yet, wanting to speak it into existence. “Azula, you have me.”

“You still don’t trust me,” she bawled. “You even brought your, your *boomerang friend* to protect you.”

Um, what? He exchanged another glance with Sokka. An imperceptible raising of the eyebrows, a resigned shrug. “Um, Azula,” he said.

“*What*.”

His hand hovered, then gripped her wrist, the hand she was holding the shears with. The skin was buried under gloves, long sleeves. “Sokka’s not here to shield me. He’s. Um. My boyfriend?”

Azula stared. Her breathing started to slow. She looked at Zuko’s hand, then back and forth between him and Sokka, red eyed.

“Surpriiiise,” said Sokka, then winced. “I take that back.”

“You see?” said Zuko. “I didn’t come with a guard. I’m here for—for you.”

She said nothing still, but the alarm was starting to seep out of her frame.

“Um, what should I do? Do you want—a hug?”

“*No*. No.”

“Do you want me to—”

“No.” She wetted her lips. “No, just, just—stay there.”

They stayed like that, crouching in the loam, Zuko’s hand a lifeline on her wrist as she regained her breath. She scrubbed her face. It left a streak of ash across her cheek, making her look younger than ever.

“How did your flame come back?” said Zuko.

“Long story.” Azula looked away. “Early days in the Sagehood, spiritual encounters, all of that stuff.”

“And your—your lightning?”

Azula brought up her other hand and curled it into a fist. A cluster of blue sparks fizzled around her knuckles, then she sighed. “I don’t know. I could do it if I tried, I think. I don’t have the energy for it anymore.”

One day, he thought, she might tell him the whole story. “I’m sorry I didn’t understand,” said Zuko. “I don’t know if I do, still. But I want to.” He paused. “Are you happy? Here?”

“It’s good for now. For a long time I thought I wouldn’t be happy.” But then a softness was tugging at her lips. “I’m doing things that mean something. His Holiness likes me.”

“I’m glad.” He squeezed her wrist. “Truthfully, I don’t always get along with Mama these days.”

The only acknowledgement she gave to that was a blink. “I just. I wish you never sent me to that place.”

But there was a resignation in her voice. If she hadn’t gone there, would Zuko have ever met this new, self-aware Azula? This new Azula who felt wrong-footed, in whose murky depths Zuko was still trying to find his sister.

“It wasn’t my choice.” He paused. “But I could’ve done something about it. I could’ve found something you were more on board with. I should’ve asked. I’m sorry.”

“I’m sorry too,” she said, a mumble, as though she hadn’t meant to say it, the sound almost scattered away by the wind. Then louder, “You can’t fix me.”

Zuko remembered saying something like that to Aang, how he’d bristled at the response at the time. It made sense now. “Isn’t it just nice, though? Isn’t that why you reached out again?”

She was quiet for a while, then wriggled her hand free from his grip. Her fingers flexed around the handle of the shears. Was that a ghost of a smile, on her pale mouth? “Maybe.”

---

“Can I give you my two cents?” said Sokka as they sailed back.

Zuko was watching the shape of the island melting into the horizon, leaning on the railing. “What is it?”

Sokka shook his head, chuckling a little. He came up behind Zuko and looped his arms around his waist, his presence warm and secure. Beneath them, the dark water swirled in the wake of the ship. “You guys are such—*siblings*.”

Bewildered, Zuko said, “We are.”

“No, I mean.” He paused to laugh, breath puffing warm against the back of Zuko’s ear. “I don’t wanna like, minimise any of this. But it’s *such* typical sibling miscommunication. You have to fight a bit before you say what you mean. I mean, I can say that cos it’s like me and Katara. Watching you guys made a bunch of things make sense.”

“Hmm?”

“I mean, the stuff you mentioned, how she felt like she wasn’t growing around you… You both settled into this dynamic early on. You guys were pitted against each other growing up, right?”

“I suppose?”

“I figured. I mean, just seeing some of your interactions way back when…”

Zuko sighed. He toyed with the cuff on Sokka’s wrist. “I just can’t believe I never considered that she felt… alone. Not recently, I mean. But before. For the longest time, all I craved was my father’s approval. And she had it. I never had friends growing up either, she did. She had everything I didn’t. It’s just all this… this *reversal* that’s happened after, after…”

“The temple stuff seems good for her. An outlet for her talents.” Sokka hummed. “It’s hard to be vulnerable around someone you’ve been trained to see as competition,” he said. “You’re adjusting; *she’s* adjusting too, I’m sure.”

He sounded much too wise about all this, Zuko thought. “What about you and Katara?” he said.

“What about us?”

“Do you feel…” What was the word? Disoriented, impotent, resentful? “You seem so concerned about her and Aang.”

Sokka’s chin came down on Zuko’s shoulder. “It’s not that,” he said, in a tone that suggested he didn’t quite know how else to put it.

Zuko waited for him to speak. Like Dr Khoo, he realised, or perhaps Sokka for him on occasion. “I want to be an uncle. I think,” Sokka said haltingly. “I think I could be the coolest uncle, actually. I’d make them little toys that’ll explode and make Katara shriek while Aang laughs himself silly. It’s a bunch of things, I guess. I’m worried sometimes that Katara is living Aang’s life. She’s only twenty-three, and she’s gonna have a baby? And looking back on when I was twenty-three, man, I was so naïve back then. Even though obviously, I know she’s always been, y’know, pretty mature. But I always thought she’d, like, wanna train up the baby waterbenders in the South or something, pass on the legacy, *be* Master Katara.”

“You know she isn’t the type to just go along with someone else, if she didn’t want to,” said Zuko. “She’s a force of nature.”

“That she is.” Sokka huffed. He squeezed Zuko around the middle. “Maybe I’m just bitter. Maybe I just wish I had my life in order like she does.”

“You told them yet? About us?”

“No. Just my dad. Waiting for his reply.” He huffed. “It’s not that I think they’ll think it’s weird.” He meant Aang and Katara, Zuko knew. “I just, I dunno. Katara bagged the Avatar, are they gonna think I had to bag the Fire Lord?”

Zuko was taken aback. “No one’s gonna think that.”

“People who knew me for years jumped on the chance to think the worst of me,” said Sokka, faraway. “Isn’t it weird? I fought your dad, his army, *real* bad guys. And the people who boil my blood hotter are a bunch of scrawny academics in Ba Sing Se.”

Zuko didn’t quite know what to say. He wished he could reach into the eddies swirling in Sokka’s brain and redirect them into smooth currents, but he didn’t know how. He leaned back against Sokka, hands coming down to grip the arms around his waist.

“I’m rambling. Sorry. Just nervous,” Sokka mumbled into his shoulder.

“What about?”

“Bunch of things. Meeting your Council. It’s stupid. I want them to like me.”

“Of course they will. Everyone likes you,” said Zuko. Then he remembered when his last partner had faced the Council and the wall of responsibilities waiting for Sokka at Caldera. “Would you still want to stay with me after meeting them, that’s the real question.” He tried to make it sound lighthearted, jokey, but the tone fell flat.

“What do you mean?” said Sokka.

Zuko fiddled with his vambraces. “Mai didn’t want… I mean, I understand. It’s a lot to ask of someone. I don’t want you to live my life, either.”

“Silly peanut,” said Sokka. “Don’t plot how we’ll end before I’ve even squared up to the challenge.”

Heart seizing, the thought crossed Zuko’s mind again: *I could love him*. “OK.” He wove his fingers between Sokka’s, wanting to trust, wanting to believe that trust could be enough. “We’ll work it out.”

He felt Sokka inhale sharply behind him, then a hand was coming up to brush aside the sweep of hair from his back. A soft press of lips at his nape, against his spine. When he pulled away, Zuko felt the wet imprint he left. “Yeah. Together.”

Zuko knew; he had been briefed thoroughly upon his return from his travels. Chief Arnook had been of the opinion that putting the elections on a public holiday would maximise the voter turnout. The colourful carved dragon heads kicked up sea spray, the oars thrashed to the rhythm of the drums as the rowers surged as one. The energy throbbed over the waters.
