## Chapter 18: Yu Dao

### Notes

#### Chapter Notes

Content warning: This chapter contains depictions of police brutality.

### Text

The port of Yu Dao was blocked.

On the deck of their hired junk, Zuko was fuming. They had sailed all night up the coast from the airship terminus. He had barely slept the whole time. Before them stretched a rudimentary earthen bridge across the opening of the harbour, rising crude and jagged from the waters. Atop it stood a row of Earth Kingdom guards.

“This is *the most infuriating* thing that bear-loving bastard has ever done,” Zuko yelled, and shook the telescope he had borrowed from a random crew member at them.

“No dice, Your Majesty,” said the captain. “They aren’t letting anyone in or out.”

“I am the *Fire Lord*.”

“That’s all the more reason for them to not let you in,” muttered Osha.

Sokka caught his arm, pulling him back from the prow. “Come on, peanut,” he said. “We can find another port and get in through the roads.”

“They blocked the roads too,” Zuko’s secretary piped up.

“Ah. Forget I said anything.”

Zuko peered back at the distant haze of the city through the telescope. In the balmy morning of Yu Dao’s subtropical summer, he had to squint through the smog produced by the city’s refineries to make out the dense urban jungle. He could see in his mind’s eye the clashes between armed guards and common people, the plumes of smoke and dust kicked up in the city centre. He could see Councillor Hara and her envoy, huddled in a dark, damp cell. He was needed, he knew.

“Do you think I could swim that?”

“Don’t be stupid,” said Sokka. “They’ll throw a rock at you. And then you’ll drown in the strait.”

Sighing, Zuko stooped to pick Druk up by the armpits. “Why haven’t you grown your wings yet?” And the dragon whimpered.

Sokka pulled him out of the way, as much as it was possible on the busy deck of the junk. All around them, the sailors were marching up and down, throwing ropes to each other, climbing around the rigging. And amid the activity was Zuko’s entourage, himself and Sokka, Osha, his secretary, more guards, and a cluster of bureaucrats from Councillor Hara’s division, ready to burst into the city with righteous diplomatic fury.

“We’ll figure out a way,” said Sokka, squeezing his shoulders with reassurance. “We’ll think of something, OK? Are you alright? How’s the stomach?”

“Coping,” said Zuko. He shot another evaluative look at the earthen bridge. Maybe he could throw a fireball at it, make it collapse?

Sokka shook him. “Focus, OK? Let’s put our brains together.”

“Captain,” a voice called down from the crow’s nest, “spotting something to starboard. In the air.”

“What is it?” the captain called back.

“Where?” said Zuko.

Craning his neck, he swivelled his borrowed telescope in the direction the sailor was pointing. He focused the lens. Through the smog, there was indeed something. A large blob of white, a patch of brown at the front, like a minuscule cloud. “I know that,” he said, heart lifting. The spirits had smiled upon him. “It’s Appa.”

“Appa?” said Sokka. He grabbed the telescope. “Tui’s gills, it is.”

The smudge moved closer. Appa was flying in from the southwest, overland. Of course the tumult in Yu Dao had attracted the Avatar’s attention. “That’s our ticket into the city,” said Zuko. He raised an arm and sent a spark shooting high into the sky. At his feet, Druk cocked his head in interest, then sent up a spark of his own, which exploded high above their heads in his distinctive technicolour splash.

Up ahead, the Earth Kingdom guards were stirring, no doubt growing suspicious. “Did they spot us? Is he coming?” Zuko said urgently to Sokka.

“They’re too far away for me to tell.”

“Send up another one,” said Osha.

Zuko shot off another spark.

“I can see his face now!” said Sokka.

“We won’t all fit,” said Zuko, eyeing his entourage. “I’m taking Osha, Sokka, and my secretary. The rest of you, meet us in the city when you can get in.”

The bureaucrats made their protests; Zuko brushed them off. They might be necessary later, but they would be a nightmare if Zuko were heading into a siege situation. The sky bison was growing ever larger. The Earth Kingdom guards launched a couple of rocks in his direction, easily tossed aside by the earthbender on board.

“Back up a bit!” roared the captain. The junk slid out of the harbour, out of range of the rocks. Appa circled overhead. “Clear a space for the Avatar!”

Sailors and passengers alike scattered, one sail snapped shut. Appa landed on the cleared deck, sending a jolt through the junk.

“Aang!” Zuko cried. “Thank the spirits you’re here!”

“Hey!” said Aang sunnily, waving from his perch atop Appa’s head. Above them, Momo was flying in loops, chittering. “We were just flying in! Oh, and Katara wants a word.”

“What do you m—”

“Sokka!” she yelled, head popping up over the saddle. “Zuko! Both of you! When were you going to tell *me*?!”

Sokka, already grasping Appa’s fur to climb up, gaped.

“Tell you what?”

“This!” Katara disappeared, then reappeared brandishing a copy of the Yitpao, the one with the illustration from the Dragonboat Festival of them tucking into a bak chang together.

“How did you get that?” said Zuko.

“Forget how I got it! Why did I have to find out you two were dating through a NEWSPAPER?”

“W-well,” Sokka gibbered, “I told Dad first and you know how long Hawky takes to fly to the South Pole. And I was *going* to send him to you but then I had to go to the palace and—“

“Can this wait?” said Osha, wincing. She steered Zuko to the sky bison and pushed him up, then thwacked Sokka’s frozen, hiked leg. “This city is in big trouble. We’re on a tight schedule here.”

“No!” cried Katara. “Come explain yourselves!”

They started climbing. They accepted Katara walloping them over the head with the paper when they crested Appa’s back. Up ahead, the Earth King’s guards had started to raise more earth from the sea, heading in their direction.

“That doesn’t look good,” said Aang. “Hold on tight, everyone!”

Appa lifted up, making the junk bob again. “Thank you!” Zuko called to the captain as he grew rapidly smaller below. “Get to safety!”

Appa soared high over the green clad guards of Kuei. Momo clung to Osha’s head. Zuko’s secretary looked faintly ill. With barely a glance Aang was flicking aside the boulders the guards flung towards them. “This is bold even of Kuei,” he said conversationally, as though his wife were not trying to throttle her brother in the saddle. “You’d think he wouldn’t impede the Avatar, at least.”

“I’m sorry, Katara,” Sokka was saying. She dodged his placating arm and lunged at him again. Zuko noted with some surprise the obvious swell of her belly. But of course, it had been months since he last saw her. “It’s not that we didn’t want to tell you. But you know, with all the stuff and you know, he’s the *Fire Lord* and we’ve been friends for ages, *all* of us, and I just— We wanted to make sure, all right? Before going around and telling the world, and everyone we cared about.”

“So, how long?” she snapped. “Well? How long have you been together?”

Zuko counted in his mind. “Um. A few months?”

“*Months*?!”

Druk came between her and Zuko, hissing. “It’s OK, baby,” said Zuko, scooping him up. “It’s baba’s fault.”

“You didn’t think to say anything when you, I dunno, sent Aang his birthday present a month ago?”

“Don’t blame yourself, sunshine—”

“*Sunshine*?!”

“Katara!” exploded Sokka. “Come on! I said I was sorry, alright? You can be mad at me, but don’t have to act all *ugh* and *judgey* about it, don’t take it out on Zuko, cos I *do* actually give a shit about him, otherwise I wouldn’t be going through all this courtly rigmarole and whatnot! Ideally, this is gonna be a permanent kinda thing! He’s my boyfriend now, can you respect that!”

There was a loud, embarrassed silence.

“Good chat,” said Aang. “Um, for what it’s worth, I think you guys seem kinda sweet together.”

“I’m not *against* you being together,” said Katara mulishly. “I mean, it’s a little weird, I admit, but only cos we’ve known Zuko for ages and I never thought you guys seemed into each other. I’m— I’m *happy* for you. Just wish you’d actually *told* me.”

Zuko scooted up to her, putting a hand on her arm. “I’m sorry about that. I guess it’s all happened since Sokka moved to Shu Jing, and well—”

She gave him a look he couldn’t quite interpret, and then she was pulling him in for a firm hug. “Ah, screw it.”

Zuko met Sokka’s eye over her shoulder, the mingled relief and embarrassment there. He pulled Sokka in too.

“Sorry I, uh, got mad,” Sokka murmured. “How’s the niece? Nephew?”

“Squirmy,” said Katara. She eyed Zuko, apologetic. “Are we welcoming you to the family too?”

Zuko ducked his head. “I’m sure we can work that out after we deal with whatever’s going on down there.”

They had entered the harbour. The main island lay facing the city’s peninsula, which was attached to the Earth Kingdom mainland. The two shorelines were connected by ferries and underwater tunnels. Tall towers lined the shores, packing the dense population onto the meagre strips of land between the sea and the hills.

“What’s the plan?” Aang called.

“I’ve been chatting to the bureaucrats,” said Sokka. He reached into his tunic and pulled out his notebook, flipping to a page where he had drawn a rudimentary map of the city in graphite. He must have sketched it overnight, Zuko realised, heart swelling. “We just faced Kuei’s Earth Kingdom guards, the state-level military. They haven’t entered the city, but they’re blocking all the routes in. Probably a real pain for everyone living and working along the border. In the city, we’re going to meet the local city guards. They’re not to be underestimated. They’ve spent the better part of the last year honing brutal riot control techniques on Yu Dao’s protesters.” He tapped the map with his graphite stick. “There are a few usual zones of fighting. Firstly, we’ll need to get Councillor Hara out of custody. My best guess is that she’s held in the headquarters of the city guards. I don’t doubt that a bunch of other politicians and dignitaries would be held there. Might be best for Osha and secretary… Sorry, I don’t think I caught your name?”

“That works,” said Osha firmly. “We’ll need their legal expertise.”

“Cool. For Aang and Katara, you’ll need to be in the thick of it.” Sokka tapped a spot along the coast of the mainland. “The opening to the tunnel has been blocked many times before to try and cut off the island from the rest of the city. No doubt they’ll try that again this time. I think you’ll need to join the fight there.”

“Looks like there’ll be plenty of water,” said Katara, grinding a fist into her palm.

“As for me and Zuko…” Sokka looked up, eyebrow raised. “Fancy storming the legislature together?”

Zuko grinned at him, almost ferociously. “Nobody else I’d rather do it with.”

---

But as Appa flew low over the island, weaving between the tall buildings, they could see more clearly the chaos unfolding below. The city streets were deserted in some parts, hawker stalls usually teeming with diners silent and shuttered. For such a densely packed city, famous for its metropolitan bustle, the emptiness was eerie. In other parts, they could see patches of intense battle, black-clad protesters railing against a green wall of guards or barricades piled high in the middle of the twisting streets. These disputes seemed to grow more intense as they approached the city centre.

“It’s gonna be hard for Appa to land,” said Aang nervously. “Up ahead are the guards’ headquarters. That’s manageable, but the building of the legislature”—he motioned up ahead—“is a lot taller. And pointier.”

In the distance they could see it: a pagoda gleaming in the watery sunlight, looking unmistakably *pointy* with its tiered eaves and the large spike at the top. On the other hand, the headquarters they were approaching was a lower building than most in Yu Dao. Most of its levels were subterranean dungeons. The main structure was ringed by thick outer walls lined with metal. The gates were seeing the most activity, where clamouring protesters surged against a line of guards. Zuko touched the handles of the dual dao strapped to his back for reassurance.

Appa landed on the roof of the outer wall. They were impossible to miss. A ragged cheer went up from the protesters at the sight of the Avatar; the alarmed guards were now directing rocks and bolts of fire towards them. Aang diverted them easily. Katara formed a water whip out of thin air that dashed the rocks to dust, and the fire into smoke. Shielded, Osha and the secretary scrambled off the saddle.

“Wait.” Sokka grabbed Zuko’s elbow. His watch was open in his other hand. “We should disembark here. It’s not far from the legislature. Aang, Katara, you need to get to the other side of the harbour.”

Osha had jumped into the courtyard to fight through the handful of guards coming up to her. The sparring practice with Zuko had paid off, he thought approvingly, as she easily dodged streaks of fire to slash close. He shot a jet of flame at another attacker for good measure as he and Sokka leapt down with Druk in tow.

“We’ll find you when we’re done,” said Aang, clapping him on the shoulder. Katara freed a hand to wave, and then Aang blew a cloud of smoke at the guards before urging Appa upwards.

“Go!” Zuko shouted to Osha as the guards fell to their knees coughing. “Get Hara and the others.”

She nodded and grabbed the secretary by the elbow, then both dashed out of sight. The recovering guards turned their attention to Zuko, Sokka, and Druk, but they were already vaulting off the roof and onto the street on the other side. The main protest was on the other side of the building. The street was empty, save for the graffiti scrawled across the walls.

*Liberate Yu Dao. Fight for freedom. Stand for the agreement.*

“We have to go,” said Sokka, tugging him away. Zuko followed him downhill through the cobbled laneways, weaving through the packed buildings. A flare sizzled up behind them; some kind of signal, Zuko surmised, but he wasn’t going to stick around long enough to find out what for. The graffiti followed them down. *Yu Dao is not the Earth Kingdom. Vote out the legislature dogs.* The city was so quiet, an unearthly quiet that sent chills under his skin—save for the distant, unsettling clamour of *something*. Then the earth beneath them wobbled.

“They’re fucking *earthbenders*,” snarled Sokka, risking a glance behind them. The pursuing guards were surfing down the street, cobbles lifting beneath their feet like scales. *Ba Sing Se forgot us, we* *will never forget. You taught us peaceful protest is useless.* Sokka reached behind himself, for the boomerang, and there was a flash of silver. A thud behind them, a shout. Zuko ran on, not daring to look. He threw a handful of fire behind himself. The boomerang returned to Sokka’s hand. The earth wobbled again.

“We got one of them,” said Sokka through gritted teeth. “The other—”

He raised the boomerang again—

*Woooooosh*. A streak of rainbow flame knocked back their earthbending pursuer.

“*Druk*,” Zuko breathed, watching the guard skitter back.

“Come on!” Sokka grabbed his arm and then they were jerking to the right, through the arcade of a building. “They’ll keep coming.” More graffiti inside: *Who do you call when the guards are* *murderers? Father, mother, I am not a seditionist. Yu Dao is my home.* They rounded a corner, past more shuttered stalls and offices, and came to the top of a flight of stairs. *If we burn you burn* *with us*, said the walls.

“Here!” said Sokka, breaking Zuko’s stupor. “This is what I was looking for.”

He pulled Zuko away from the graffiti, down the stairs. It was an underground station, Zuko realised, for the city’s famous subway system, earthbending ingenuity meeting firebending mechanics in a place where land was a precious commodity. He had never ridden it before; as a foreign dignitary, his past travels around Yu Dao had been conducted in private vehicles. A first time for everything. He and Sokka vaulted over the ticket barriers—the bored teen behind the counter did nothing to stop them—and jogged to the platform. A cluster of black-clad protesters sat on a bench further down, wearing masks that covered their mouths and noses; they started whispering and pointing at Druk. Ignoring them, Sokka marched up to study the subway map as Zuko caught his breath.

“Two stops down.” He tapped the map and checked his watch. “Should be quick.”

There was a distant roar. Headlamps lit up the tunnel and then the train was barrelling into the station. So Yu Dao’s public transport did not stop, not even for a coup. The protesters filed on, and Zuko and Sokka hurriedly followed. Seats lined the sides of the carriage, and a few passengers were huddled on the opposite bench.

“Well, if my eyes don’t deceive me,” one of them said, a masked young woman, “Fire Lord Zuko. We meet again. Nice dragon.”

The doors closed, the train jolted forwards. Sokka and Zuko flailed for the railings; Druk wrapped his tail around a pole. “Do I… know you?” Zuko said to the woman.

“I spoke in your Council last year.”

He placed her at once. “Hoil—”

“Shh! Not out loud.” Hoilam’s eyes flicked to her companion, then her voice dropped low. “Listen. Everyone in the opposition who stood for election has an arrest warrant out for them.”

“You—you stood?”

She nodded. “I was inspired, actually, after I spoke to your Council. I wanted to do something *more*. I put myself forward as part of the social workers’ union.” Then she sighed. “It happened the day after polls closed, so quick that the Northern Water Tribespeople couldn’t even prepare for it. I suspected something would happen. Most did. But the scale was completely unexpected. We were overwhelmed. Many of my colleagues have been captured already. By the spirits, I managed to escape. It’s a relief to see you, actually. I didn’t think you’d…”

“We’re going to confront the legislature,” said Zuko.

Hoilam nodded. “We’re headed there too. We need to take back this city.” The train pulled to a stop, doors opening again. “It’s been like this for days. Everyone’s terrified. They burned all our ballots, we can’t even…”

“We’re here to help,” said Sokka. “And the Avatar. He’s here too. Tell us what to do and we’ll help you.”

“It’s going to be dangerous. You’ll need supplies. Ah Wai?”

The companion, Ah Wai, reached into his sleeve and pulled out a pair of masks, like what Hoilam and the other protesters they had seen were wearing.

“Save it for someone else. We’re gonna be recognised,” said Sokka flatly. “We stick out.”

What with Zuko’s scar, red Fire Lord garb, and the literal *dragon* accompanying them—he was right. Anyhow, Zuko hadn’t intended to be undercover.

“It’s not just for identity,” said Ah Wai, pressing the masks into their hands. “It’s defence. The guards have been using a kind of chemical weapon on us, a gas that impairs your breathing. But most importantly, it’s combustible.”

“Meaning?”

“Meaning,” said Sokka, turning wide eyes on Zuko, “no one can firebend around it. Or it’ll explode.”

---

The station of the legislature was much more crowded than the one where they had boarded. They shouldered their way to the street through the amassing black-clad bodies. In front of the exit, at the doorstep of the tumult, lay a group of prone shapes on woven mats, fussed over by other protesters. “First aid,” said Hoilam in an undertone, ushering them past. Zuko caught the eyes of one young man, red rimmed and unseeing, with a medic thumping his back and offering a sip of tea to his lips. Another had a trail of blood tracking down the side of her face. His heart seized.

And then they surfaced.

Black-clad figures were running back and forth. Bricks lay scattered on the ground, dug up from the pavement. The apartment windows above them were pulled firmly shut, and the only sign of life within was the fluttering laundry. There were rickshaws abandoned undercover. Plumes of white smoke gusted out of alleyways. The roar of voices, a crack like a cannon. At the centre of it all was the building of the legislature, set slightly uphill so its height became all the more imposing, its centipede-like segments scuttling high into the sky.

“That’s it,” said Sokka. He had put on the mask and the words came out muffled. A wrinkle came between his eyebrows. “Man. It’s rocking some terrible feng shui.”

Zuko followed his gaze, feeling oddly chilled in the summer air. How much thoughtless destruction had emanated from this building? What lay in wait for him there?

Hoilam and Ah Wai were surging into the crowd as though it were business as usual. After a year of protest, it probably was for them. They were strapping on wide-brimmed hats that looked like they were made of several layers of bamboo—protection against projectiles, Zuko realised. Behind them was the blue stretch of the harbour, and before them was the claustrophobic crush of buildings. The sea of people grew thicker as they pushed forwards, roiling black around them. He caught Sokka’s hand, so they would not be lost in the crowd. No one seemed to notice the Fire Lord had arrived; he became one of them. Passing shadows slapped safety hats onto his and Sokka’s heads. There were supply lines through the chaos, it seemed. Protest chants, some which Zuko recognised from the graffiti, were being bandied back and forth in the crowd. Some, along the edges, were holding placards. The protesters were making for something, presumably the gates of the legislature. Small rocks and clods of earth were raining down. Zuko could hardly see. Then there was the smell. Even through the mask, it was strong, borne on the white smoke drifting over their heads. Acrid and stinging, as though someone had concentrated bile and poured it out over the streets of Yu Dao.

“Is that it?” spluttered Sokka, trying in vain to flap the smell away. “That chemical?”

Hoilam nodded grimly.

“I don’t think Druk should be breathing this,” said Zuko, pulling up short. The dragon was weaving through the protesters’ legs, but his whiskers were curling and uncurling, almost anxiously. “Druk, can you—”

“Keep moving!” someone called from behind.

“What’s happening?”

He had disrupted the flow.

“Sorry, sorry!” He scooped up the dragon. He definitely bumped someone, who grunted. Druk clambered onto his shoulders, awkward and too large. Further upstream, Hoilam stopped too, expression obscured by the mask and hat. Her head was cocked.

But the spell that had captured the protesters of Yu Dao in their single minded focus was broken. The one closest to Zuko was looking between him and the dragon, and him and the dragon, and him and the dragon. Another peered close, eyes widening when they—apparently—noticed his scar. Sokka’s arm tightened around his shoulders.

“Is that dragon real?”

“Are you the Fire Lord?”

“Which side is the scar meant to be on again?”

“Yes and yes, and this is the correct side,” said Zuko. “Sorry, I—I’m trying to get inside the legislature. Don’t let me interrupt you.”

But it was like throwing a pebble into a peacock-koi pond. Others were coming to a standstill, and the effects were rippling outwards. There was a hush before the noise started to build again, coming in fuzzy and disorienting through his left ear. He held Druk close, leaned minutely against Sokka’s steady weight.

“What are you doing here?” cried one voice.

“You’re helping us?” someone else demanded.

“He’s going to save us!” A ragged cheer went up.

“Don’t be stupid,” another voice cut in. “He’s the one who made this mess.” And other voices grumbled their assent.

“Well?” The voices were rounding back on him. “What are you doing here, Fire Lord?”

It was so different from any confrontation back in the Capital, against the bereft former firebenders, or the bearers of the black flame. There were no guards between him and the people before him, no guards to whisk him back to the safe cloisters of his palace. Zuko swallowed. Then Hoilam’s hand came upon his arm, as though he were not the Fire Lord, as though there were no barriers between them.

“*Speak*,” she said. “Tell them.”

So he did. He drew himself up and raised his arms. It was hard to project his voice with the mask and the lingering odour of the gas, but he did the best he could. “Citizens of Yu Dao!” he called. “I have come to help you in your endeavour for political autonomy. My Council and I have worked together in the last year to make sure you could have your voice.”

There were jeers. “What good’s that done?” a youth’s voice called back. “Where are the results? All our ballots: burned!”

“He’s here now, isn’t he?” an older voice called back.

“I made the mistake of putting good faith in the Earth King and the provincial governor,” said Zuko. “They have violated the trust of the Water Tribe and the Fire Nation. But my forefathers created this whole mess. My great-grandfather founded the colony here, and in doing so threw everything into tumult. The wrongs end with me. They have to. I am here to end them.” He swallowed “I will end them.”

The protesters murmured among themselves. “How will you do that?” asked someone close to him. Zuko could see the man, a skinny figure more suited to literary pursuits than fighting in the streets. Yet here he was, fighting for his city and its identity, along with tens of thousands of his fellow citizens. “The agreement failed us all.”

“I’ll negotiate a new one,” said Zuko desperately. “The old one was defective. We will have a new one that represents everyone better.”

“Forget an agreement! We need to get inside that building first.”

Zuko said, “That’s our intenti—”

“Is that Sokka?” someone interrupted.

The crowd started to clamour again. Zuko looked sideways, over to Sokka’s steady presence beside him. He met Zuko’s gaze, a question in his eyes.

“Y-yes,” said Sokka, stammering. Zuko nodded in encouragement. “Yeah, I am.”

And—impossibly—an approving murmur rippled through the crowd.

“I’d trust the brother of Master Katara.”

“The brother-in-law of the Avatar!”

“He, Suki, and Toph brought down Ozai’s fleet by themselves! As children! If anyone can break through this siege, it’s him.”

“Not to mention his innovations! Did you know that in the South Pole…”

“R-right,” said Sokka. Zuko nudged him. “Katara and the Avatar are here too, across the harbour. They’re on your side. We’re all here to support your fight for freedom.” His hand found Zuko’s, the scratch of his glove against Zuko’s palm, and after a moment of hesitation he pumped their joined hands into the air. “The Fire Lord and I included.”

And somehow—*somehow!*—it worked. The balance was tipping away from suspicion. There were claps and cheers. Someone revived a protest chant. Even the more muted protesters were humming in agreement. The power of invoking the Avatar, that eternal symbol of hope. And Sokka himself. Zuko’s Sokka was his good-humoured, kind boyfriend, smart and full of care. That was the man who had captured his heart, and who would no doubt continue to enthral him. But he was starting to catch a glimpse of the Sokka who had enraptured his Council, who had been recommended to be his consort, someone who would be Zuko’s political complement: his connections, his talents, and the value of his famous deeds in the public consciousness.

Someone shouldered their way forwards. “You’re doing it on our terms,” they shouted over the din. “The provincial governor is inside that building. He makes all the decisions around here. During the election, he wasn’t even in Yu Dao—he managed to get in. He has a way of getting in and out of that building. We suspect he has constructed a tunnel and we have diggers going through the subway station to try to intercept it. Then we’ll close in on the legislature from the front entrance as well. We’ll wedge those corrupt dogs in.”

“I’ll help with the tunnel,” said Sokka. He shrugged. “Can’t come out of Ba Sing Se’s Engineering Department without knowing a thing or two about earthworks.”

“Your Majesty,” said Hoilam, “I think you should stay.”

Zuko nodded sharply. The protesters were surging again, re-energised in their attack against the legislature. “Take Druk with you,” he told Sokka. “It’s not safe for him out here. I can’t risk him firebending around the gas either.” He scratched Druk behind the ears. “Be good, OK, baby?”

Sokka shouldered the dragon. People were slipping past them, currents of black. Then Sokka kissed him quick through the mask, fabric on fabric. “I’ll come back and find you.”

“Stay safe.”

“You too.”

Then Sokka was following a group away from the towering legislature building, and then he winked out of sight.

## Chapter 19: Burn with Us

### Notes

#### Chapter Notes

Content warning: This chapter contains depictions of police brutality.

### Text

Zuko eventually pushed to the front of the crowd. There were so many guards, lined up in their khaki uniforms, locked in heated battle against the more intrepid protesters. “Those are the frontliners,” Hoilam had shouted into his ear. “They’re armed and they do most of the physical fighting in the movement.”

*Armed* was rich. They wielded makeshift weapons. One had a wok strapped to their arm as a shield, several others wielded umbrellas. A handful of luckier ones had swords or bamboo poles or bows and arrows. Only the earthbenders could bend, because the guards’ gas weapon had eliminated the possibility for firebending. Not all of the earthbenders knew how to bend in combat. Some had learnt quickly in the past year. Few of the frontliners, especially the younger ones, had experienced the war firsthand.

It was a far cry from the city guards who were armed to the teeth, protected from head to toe like armadillo-tortoises. It was nothing like what the city guards of Caldera would wear; no, these guards looked like soldiers. Besides the chemical weapon, they had real arms, clubs and crossbows and swords. The only saving grace was that the guards couldn’t use firebending either, but there were enough combat-trained earthbenders in their midst that it didn’t matter.

As Zuko spent time in the crowd, he began to unravel its logic. The frontliners engaged the guards in pitched battle on the streets in front of the legislature. There was a no man’s land, a street block before the building that was tugged back and forth between the contenders. The earth there was constantly moving. The frontliners threw bricks and pottery and earth. The guards wielded some kind of portable cannon, and they used it to catapult canister after canister of their foul gas at the protesters. Sometimes the two sides clashed. The frontliners tried to push the guards back, they tried not to be cornered by guards. That was bad news, if that happened; Zuko had to rush in with a handful of others when the guards picked off one of their own, battering her with clubs before they were pried off. They rotated as injuries or exhaustion took them. The other protesters assisted the fight with supply, or healing, or they demonstrated against the legislature. They passed messages and supplies. It was, terrifyingly, a well-oiled machine.

Now, it was growing dark. Above them, the famous neon signs of Yu Dao were winking to life. Zuko had always admired them during his visits; there was something about how they painted the nightscape in riotous colour, the herald of some future that was not yet in his grasp. But he could hardly stop to look when he collapsed a few blocks away from the legislature, wiping the sweat from his brow and cleaning off his dual dao.

“Your Majesty, here.” A stranger’s hands, offering him a cup of tea and a box of fried rice. It was far away enough for him to take the mask off. He drank deeply, then scarfed down the rice.

“Thank you,” he managed when he remembered his manners. “Any word from underground?”

The stranger squatted down beside him. The neon played over their skin in greens and pinks. “They’ve dug out a few potential routes from the subway station. There’s suspicion that the governor collapses the tunnel after himself after passing through.”

Zuko’s insides squeezed. The operations were contingent on the group making it through the tunnel and ambushing the legislature inside, and he wouldn’t see Sokka until that happened. Where was Hoilam? She had promised to meet him back here. Zuko ate more rice to distract himself from the unease. It was good, with little bits of salted fish sprinkled through. Still hot. The wok hay sizzled fresh on his tongue. There had to be restaurants supplying the protest effort. He hoped Sokka had time to grab a bite to eat, wherever he was.

“It’s a brave thing you did out there,” said the stranger.

Zuko shrugged. “It’s nothing. I know how to fight. I promised to help.” He sighed. “Would be more effective if I could bend.”

The gas had been everywhere, burning in his nostrils and throat and dusting the surrounds with white. The guards had a custom mask that allowed them to breathe. It looked horrifying, a kind of truncated tube jutting out of their faces, but it was no doubt much more effective than the cloth variants used by the protesters. A handful of frontliners had that heavy duty mask—they had likely been taken from the guards.

“I know what you mean,” said the stranger. “I’m a firebender too. The name’s Kar-hei, by the way.” They looked wistfully at their hands. “There are fewer of us compared to earthbenders here, so I feel extra useless without it.”

“You’re not useless, Kar-hei,” said Zuko. “You’re keeping me alive.” He smiled around his mouthful of rice, and they smiled back wanly. “Can I ask you something?”

“Sure, what is it?”

Zuko chewed up his rice, considering. “Why do you all wear black? I mean—” he hastened to say at their quizzical glance, “in the Fire Nation, we renounced the black flame because of its imperialist connotations.”

“We’re not wearing the black flame,” said Kar-hei quickly. Then, with some resignation, “It’s a rallying cry. The thriving city that’ll be a pile of ashes, because of Ba Sing Se’s greed.”

*If we burn, you burn with us*, Zuko recalled, and he shivered. He ate his rice.

“I didn’t expect you’d be like this,” said Kar-hei.

“Like what?”

“I dunno.” They fiddled with their mask. “I never got the impression you cared that much about us.”

Zuko stilled. He swallowed down the instinct to be defensive.

“It’s not personal, um. Your Majesty. You probably have more important things to worry about. We’re just a small city you don’t even have jurisdiction over, anymore.”

“No,” said Zuko. He thought back to his last visit to Yu Dao, how he had been itching the whole time for the talks to be over, for the situation to resolve itself by magic. Even that very first visit all those years ago, he’d been young and rash and happy to accept whatever resolution would be easiest to implement. Now, he was fighting among them, fighting against the result of the very agreement he had put the seal of his office on. “You’re right. I didn’t give this city the care you deserved.”

There was a crash somewhere, the earth shuddered. “I better run,” said Kar-hei, rising out of their crouch. “See if anyone needs me.”

“Thanks again,” Zuko said, but they had already darted off.

He sat awhile and watched the flow of bodies along the street. Before his eyes, the movement seemed to grow more frantic; a signal Zuko didn’t understand was being flashed across the lines. Something prickled under his skin. The ground shook again, more insistently. He shovelled in the last few mouthfuls of his meal and got up.

“Your Majesty!” Someone barrelled into him: Hoilam, with Ah Wai in tow. “There you are.”

“What’s happening?”

“No idea. But the guards in front of the legislature are *doubling*.”

Zuko followed them. “Where are they coming from?”

Hoilam didn’t respond, or maybe she did and Zuko couldn’t hear. He snapped the mask back on as the rumbling mounted, picking his way through the crowd. As the ground roiled, he felt insanely grateful for all the sparring practice he had ever done with Toph. With careful steps, they made their way back to the front.

The frontline was clashing. The neon lights picked out the gleam of the guards’ armour and the protesters’ makeshift weapons. If Zuko had thought the front of the legislature was full of guards before, it was nothing like now. They were shoving the protesters back, as though trying to clear the street in front of the legislature steps. *Something’s happened*, Zuko thought. *They’re worried* *about something*. Had the tunnelling team been successful? Had they broken inside? In the crush and chaos, it was impossible to pass any information along. He looked up at the façade of the building, but it betrayed nothing.

“Don’t push back!” Hoilam cried as she waded into the thicket, steadying herself over the ground with an umbrella. “It’ll crush the front.”

Zuko leapt in after her, unsheathing his dao. It was even more terrifying now he was in the thick of it. He felt the press of the panicking protesters on all sides. He sprang away from a lifting chunk of earth—*thank you, Toph*—and struck out at a guard with a raised club. The guard lashed at him. Zuko slammed the flat of his blade into their wrist and the club skittered into a crack that yawned open in the ground. A whistling sound, barely audible beneath the rumbling and the shouts; Zuko slashed in its direction and barely had time to shudder at the crossbow arrow as it fell, halved, onto the pavement. A gas canister clattered out of the gloom and fizzed open. The smoke bloomed out and the protesters scattered, coughing. But the earth beneath their feet tilted—it was a sliding, tumbling retreat. Zuko wedged his dao into a crack. The only ones who seemed steady were the earthbenders that had wedged themselves into the pavement, sending volley after volley of rock at their attackers.

“Who are the earthbenders here?” shouted Zuko, as loud as he could over the din.

There were murmurs all around, a few hands raised. “Listen to me. You need to steady the earth, keep it as smooth as possible.”

“We need to fight back, though!” one cried back.

“No, he’s right,” Hoilam piped up. “It’s way too hard to walk through.”

“Exactly,” said Zuko, latching gratefully to her. “It’s more important for us to have a clear path ahead. Then we can push back.”

There was a moment of hesitation. Zuko held his breath. It was a big ask, he knew; half of their most effective fighters would be diverted into steadying the ground. Then he felt the strain, the warring tension in the earth. The protesters’ earthbending competed with the guards’ and the shaking died down to a tremor. Still shaky, but *much* steadier.

“Alright! If you’re not an earthbender, pair up!” said Hoilam. “Protect your partner and focus on defence, then move forwards!”

A signal flashed through. The frontliners moved, the scattered formations coming together as each sought another to hold onto. They were finding their rhythm again. Encouraged, Zuko pulled his dao from the ground and readied himself to push forwards. Then he felt a slim arm link with his. “Hoilam?”

“Come on, Your Majesty,” she said, tugging him along. “I’m pairing up with you.”

No, no. He hadn’t expected someone to pair up with him. He was the interloper, here to pay his penance. “What about—” He pulled back. “What about Ah Wai?”

“That nerd?” She laughed, more huff than laugh. “He’ll be fine. You need a partner. Let’s go.”

She tugged again. He felt strangely warmed by her gruffness. She opened her umbrella. Zuko could hear the pebbles plink against its canopy. He reached around the shield to roundhouse kick a guard aside, an earthbender who was wrestling against the protesters’ efforts to still the ground. Zuko crashed his dao into a club as it honed in on another pair, then whacked a flying gas canister away from the crowd. A band of guards lunged out of nowhere, weapons drawn. Zuko fought them off with difficulty. They slashed at his clothes and in their heavy armour, it seemed impossible for Zuko’s dao to land any blows. At his back, Hoilam was roaring a battle cry, trying to dislodge the guards’ masks with her umbrella. Together, they succeeded only in driving them back, but then another pair of protesters tumbled in, walloping back with another umbrella and—curiously—a barbeque fork. More gas billowed, white and stinging. It was hard to breathe, hard to push through when he knew he would be diving headlong into more of that foul substance. But then from behind the frontline, he saw others still pouring into the fray. Arms linked, hands held, fording on back to back, wading in with the rest of them. Some of them were holding placards, others still held dislodged bricks. These were regular demonstrators, he realised, the ones who hadn’t intended to join the fight. And what could Zuko do but grit his teeth and slash back at the guards twice as ferociously?

And somehow, somehow, it was working. The guards were retreating. Triumphant, the throng drove them up the steps of the legislature.

Then Zuko slammed into something *solid*.

It ricocheted him against the people coming up from behind. He could hear Hoilam beside him hissing in pain.

He helped her up. “You OK?”

“What was that?” she said weakly, rubbing at her nose.

He blinked through the dizziness. Fences: great big wooden barricades had been levered up in front of them and secured into the pavement. He could see the unnerving, masked faces of the city guards over the top. Spread up the steps that led to the great entrance of the legislature, they appeared to be hovering in the air. Zuko could see their jeering eyes, mocking the protesters below.

It wasn’t a retreat. It was a trap.

If Zuko could use his bending, he would burn the fences to the ground. As if reading his mind, a dozen canisters came clattering down over the fences. The protesters leapt back as they cracked open, spewing gas between their feet and the barricade.

Above them, there was the sound of something slamming open. Through the white haze, at the top of the stairs, Zuko could make out the silhouette of a hulking figure. “Stop where you are, Fire Lord,” came their voice, warped by the gas mask.

He squinted. There was something long and red and *thrashing* in his hands.

“Druk!” screamed Zuko. He leapt up against the fences, pounding against the wood. The gas burned in his throat. His mind was racing. How did they capture him? Where was Sokka? And if they captured Druk inside the building, did that mean the tunnelling team had found the governor’s passageway? Where was the rest of them? “I’m here, Druk! It’s me!”

“Not one wrong move from you, Your Majesty,” sneered one of the guards over the fence, “or baby dragon’s going *kapow*.”

Zuko growled. Druk was struggling, squirming. There was a gap in the cloud. Zuko could see the whites of his eyes. Fire licked at the corners of his mouth. A *crack*, then a canister was hitting the pavement behind him, at the feet of the crowd, billowing out more acrid gas. And Zuko saw the moment Druk’s bulging eyes landed on it.

“Druk, no!” Zuko cried, but it was too late: the rainbow fireball sailed in an arc and collided with the white smoke. He braced for the explosion.

It didn’t come.

The fire burned bright, suspended in the air. Its shape stretched high, ribbons of rainbow dragonfire reaching up into the neon-lit night, then shimmered away. And after hours of the acrid gas burning in his lungs, Zuko smelled… clean air.

“The dragonfire burnt the gas off,” said Hoilam behind him. She took a shaky step forwards, towards the empty canister. “It didn’t explode.”

The guards were indignant, roaring above them. They set off more canisters, but that only proved the theory right. The gas crisped away under more blistering dragonfire. Druk flashed out of his captor’s hands, out of sight. Zuko pulled his mask off and let it flutter to the ground. *Just like when* *I met him in the rainforest*, he thought: the flame that did not burn. Then out of nowhere, the glimmering dragonfire hit the barricade before him and engulfed the frames with roaring red. The protesters, who had started to creep forwards, jolted back again.

Zuko put his hand into it.

It was hot, but Zuko had the sun inside himself. There were gasps all around, but he ignored them. There was a consciousness there, in the flame, and it seemed to be directed at him. *Baba?*

*My baby*, he thought. *You’re my best boy. So brave, so clever.*

A nebulous fear pulsed in the heat.

*Bad people are trying to hurt us. I’ll come and save you, OK? Just hang on tight. Be brave.*

A question, now.

*You’ve done so much. I don’t know what else you can do. Let baba handle this.*

Then Druk showed him the images. Fire glittering, encircling. Dancing, roaring, incinerating. Orange, blue, multicoloured; burning, sparking, crackling, smoking, exploding. The blacksmith’s furnace, the ridgeline signalling beacon, the controlled burn that would devour the fuel of a forest fire. Fire at his disposal, moulded for his purpose. Fire as refiner, fire as protector, fire as avenger.

*I see now.*

And Druk wanted Zuko to show him.

So he did.

He pushed his hand through the burning fence. The barriers fell in piles of ash around him, and he stepped through them. The flame that jetted out of his hand sailed in a technicolour arc and hit the first guard. He yelped and struggled, but he brushed against someone else and they caught on fire too. The firebenders around him tried to extinguish it. To no avail. They could not control it. They did not have the power. Only Zuko did. And it spread.

*Like this, Druk?*

The fire in his hands glowed in assent. He punched it at another guard, who was halfway through an earthbending manoeuvre. She cried out. She rolled onto the ground to extinguish it, but her colleague with the drawn sword couldn’t evade it quickly enough. The fire chased its way up his uniform, then engulfed the sword. The molten remains dripped onto the ground. Zuko blocked a volley of arrows with a glowing swipe, then breathed a stream of fire back that way. The firebenders lashed at him, but their red flames were no match for the fire of the dragons.

The fire made its way around the screaming guards. They were lit brighter than the neon lights that winked above. And all around Zuko, the protesters were alight with renewed fervour. They pelted the guards with earth. The emboldened firebenders lit up the street. Someone threw a brick, someone threw an umbrella. From afar, Zuko caught sight of a figure he recognised as Kar-hei, ripping off their mask and blasting it into the blaze.

“Stop, stop!” cried a booming voice.

It was the provincial governor, leaning on the great doors of the legislature at the top of the stairs. He looked harried, nothing like the unreadable, pitiless figure who had harangued Zuko and his staff in the past year. “Stop, you deranged ashmaker. Burning them to a crisp, just like your colonising forefathers.”

“No,” said Zuko, and he knew what a portrait he made, red robes grimy and torn, cuts in his skin, balls of technicolour dragonfire churning in both fists. “It’s as hot as any fire. They won’t burn. But their weapons and armour will.”

The governor licked his lips. But Zuko had spoken the truth. As it exhausted its fuel, the fires started to sputter out, leaving behind the pitifully naked, unarmed squirming bodies of the guards all over the stairs.

“Explain yourself!” snarled the provincial governor, but it was too late. The protesters, seizing their chance, surged. As one black tidal wave, they charged up to seize and restrain the guards. Their momentum carried Zuko along. He felt a nudge against his leg, then Druk’s sturdy talons making their way up his leg. He embraced the dragon gratefully, kissing his snout. More protesters ran up the stairs and grabbed the governor, locking his wrists with earth. Zuko shored up before him.

“You call me a coloniser,” said Zuko, stroking Druk’s tail as the dragon hissed at the man’s sweating face. “I can admit what my forefathers did was wrong. I can even admit that I made mistakes about the handover of the city, six years ago. But can you admit you’re doing everything you accuse us of?”

“Get out of the Earth King’s city,” said the governor, struggling against his bonds. “He will consolidate what belongs to him, and you and your ashen ilk best get out of his way.”

“You will address me as *Your Majesty*,” said Zuko coldly. “You pathetic man. You and the Earth King both. Gambling with people’s lives to pump your ego. You don’t own this city any more than the Fire Nation does, don’t you see that?” He leaned in close, until the insolence in the governor’s eyes changed to fear. “The Earth Kingdom was the victim of my forefathers’ crimes. But you are repeating the same crime on this city.” He shook his head. “Listen to the citizens. Everything has changed. We can’t keep trying to force things to go back to how they were before the war. We can only go forwards.”

“Watch out!” someone screamed, and Zuko barely registered the governor’s hands breaking out of the earthen cuffs before a flash of silver—

“Ugh,” said Sokka, coming down the steps to retrieve the boomerang from where it had glanced off the provincial governor’s back. He wiped it on his trouser leg before slotting it back into its holder. “What utter scum. You alright, sunshine?”

Sokka’s meteorite sword was drawn, shiny with blood; there was dirt and mud smeared all over his clothing, and he sported a busted lip. He was the most beautiful thing Zuko had seen all night. “You’re OK.”

On the ground, the provincial governor twitched. Around him, the civilian earthbenders were restrengthening his bonds. “I am,” said Sokka. Zuko wanted to hold him and kiss him and run his hands all over Sokka’s body to make sure all his bones were intact, but there were eyes everywhere. He settled for squeezing Sokka’s proffered hand. “Sorry I lost Druk for a bit. Only had to fight through a score of guards inside, but yeah.” He gestured at the open gates above. “The legislature’s all yours.”

“Not mine,” said Zuko. He gestured at the people around him. “Theirs.”

A breathless laugh came from behind. “In that case, I think these guys can take over from here.”

Zuko turned at the familiar voice. Osha and his secretary, looking significantly worse for wear, had pushed through the crowd. Behind them was the column of the legislature’s political prisoners: the candidates of the opposition that had stood for election, and a handful of blue-clad Northern Water Tribespeople. Hoilam gave a shout of jubilation and ran to them.

“Your Majesty.”

It was Councillor Hara and her envoy. Rumpled and scuffed, they had certainly seen better days— but they were free now, and they looked overjoyed to see him. Zuko squeezed their hands when they approached, too relieved for words.

Bringing up the rear, a few feet above the gathered crowd, Aang and Katara had flown in on Appa. Across the island, he knew the crowds that had gathered at the harbour crossing must be surging in, an unstoppable wave against the guards dotted all around the streets. The tyranny was being swept away, the people were taking back their city. The pair were too far for Zuko to shout his thanks, but he waved anyway.

---

The talks lasted well into the night. The crowd had long dispersed, streaming across the city to celebrate. Zuko could hear the distant crack of fireworks. In the legislature, Hoilam led the charge for renegotiations. The chained provincial governor and mayor made a ridiculous image, propped into their seats. The incumbent pro-Ba Sing Se politicians sat cowed, facing their freed opponents. A handful of remaining frontliners kept a stern vigil over the meeting.

Zuko stood against the wall. His arms were full of a clever baby dragon, who was now extremely sleepy after his big day out, and Sokka had slung a heavy arm over both of them.

“That was impressive, what you did today,” Sokka murmured. Katara had come by and patched him up, though his swollen lip hadn’t gone down fully. “Have you ever done that before?”

“It wasn’t me. It was all Druk. I felt him… *communicating* with me. In the fire.”

“Huh,” said Sokka.

“Crazy, right?”

“I can believe it. If it’s you.”

Zuko sighed. He watched the opposition candidates on the other side of the room browbeat the sitting mayor over the events of the past few days.

“You must be tired,” murmured Sokka.

“You too,” said Zuko.

“Why aren’t we going to bed, then?”

“Because,” said Zuko, “I’m watching over what’s going on here.”

Sokka chuckled as Ah Wai delivered a particularly scathing, deadpan comment on the Ba Sing Se gold sitting in the mayor’s bank vault. “Looks like they’re doing fine without you. You sent Hara to bed.”

“That’s different. She’s been in prison for the last few days. *I’m* responsible for this mess. My country, my own ancestors. I have to make sure it doesn’t go to shit again.”

“By watching?”

“Don’t be facetious.”

Sokka snorted. “Look, maybe you should,” he murmured. “Or, maybe you don’t need to decide what happens to them anymore. You’ve done your part. You used your power to stand up for their rights, you protected them. Now they’re free to decide the future for themselves.”

“I’m not deciding anything by just *watching*. I’m—taking responsibility.”

“You’re not exactly contributing either,” said Sokka archly. “Look, I know how much this means to you. But you can’t fix everything. And sometimes, some mistakes aren’t yours to make. There’s stuff that’s within your realm. You have a whole country. Focus on that.”

And then Zuko was thinking back to Azula, and that last conversation with Dr Khoo: *When she* *was released, she was free to choose… She controls her life again…* On the way back home from the Earth Kingdom, they would pass Crescent Island again, they should pay her another visit. “Well…”

“Go sleep, sunshine. I’m sure they’ll call you if they need you.”

Zuko met Hoilam’s eye across the room where she stood at the podium under the emblem of Yu Dao. She gave him a crisp nod, the ghost of a smile. *Thank you*, she mouthed, and then she was turning back to level a steely gaze at the provincial governor.

“Alright then,” he murmured to Sokka. Yu Dao would be in safe hands. “I’ll come to bed.”

## Chapter 20: Epilogue: ...And Escape

“What do you think?” said Zuko, inspecting himself from the different angles of the mirrors in front of him. A pair of seamstresses hovered at his waist, pinching the fabric together with invisible stitches.

Behind him, Azula leaned back in her chair. The front legs wobbled dangerously off the ground. In her plain acolytes’ robes, she stood out amid the palatial furnishings, but Zuko was gratified to note that even after so many years away, she had settled in comfortably: she was picking idly at a bowl of rambulongans, spitting the seeds with unerring accuracy into a spittoon. “Who expected you’d become such a peacock, brother.”

It was the closest thing he’d get to a compliment from her. If anything, it relieved him to hear that old snark, now defanged. It had taken a while, but he had managed to convince her to visit for the upcoming holiday. There were stipulations: she wouldn’t have to interact with their mother, she would stay at the temple, and the kitchens would *definitely* serve squirrel-fish, deep fried the way she always liked it. But she was here, of her own accord and everything, and that counted as a win in his books.

“*I* think you look great, boss,” said Osha from her perch by the doorway. “I’ll have a chat with the hairdresser later, but I was thinking—”

“Hey, sunshine!”

In the mirrors, Zuko could see Sokka’s reflection bounce into view of the doorway. Druk pounced on him first, twisting around his ankles. Since Yu Dao, the dragon had only grown, now the approximate size and weight of two ferret-retrievers. It made carrying him difficult, though Zuko was determined to keep doing it.

“Hi, little noodle,” Sokka cooed.

“Babe!” Zuko divested himself of the seamstresses; fabric trailing, he tumbled into Sokka’s waiting arms. Zuko held his familiar weight. “You’re here!”

“Right I am,” Sokka said. “And happy early birthday!”

After a busy couple of months apart, it was joyous to see him again. The second settlement of Yu Dao had been consuming Zuko’s days. Even the Earth King had finally deigned to make an appearance, bear in tow, though he had little choice after the major insults that had been dealt to two world leaders at Yu Dao in his name. Zuko did not doubt it would take a long time to heal that particular geopolitical relationship, but the combined displeasure of the Fire Nation and both Water Tribes had effectively shamed Kuei into reentering the Yu Dao talks in good faith.

In some ways, Zuko could see where Kuei was coming from. Here was a ruler who had been for decades a puppet of the Dai Li, kept ignorant of the war raging beyond the walls of Ba Sing Se. Perhaps he had felt humiliated after the truth had been unveiled, perhaps he felt a need to reassert his power and authority. But, gripped by some kind of misdirected nostalgia, he had wreaked so much harm. There, Zuko had come to realise, the line must be drawn.

So this was the result: Yu Dao re-ran its elections, and was now boasting a brand new legislature. Hoilam proudly represented a small residential district. As predicted, the pro-Ba Sing Se crowd was all but decimated, left with just a pocket of representation in the city. All the big players had turned up to the swearing-in ceremony: Aang, resplendent in his formal yellow robes; the elders of the Southern Water Tribe alongside Hakoda, who roped Zuko into conversations about his son whenever they had a spare moment; even the elusive Yee Kwan of the neighbouring Cranefish Republic had showed up in his modernist Tang suit, a certainly charismatic man whom Zuko couldn’t work out whether he liked or disliked.

In the midst of all that, Sokka had returned to Shu Jing. They had only managed to snatch a few moments together amid the political drama. Now, Zuko let Sokka tuck his nose into his neck, press a kiss against his throat. He had missed this.

“Are these the new robes I see?” said another voice.

Zuko stepped back. “Sifu!” He made the sign of the flame and bowed. “You came too.”

Piandao bowed back, fluid and elegant as ever. “Of course. I had to inspect what I paid for in person.”

“I have to thank you,” said Zuko. “They’re so beautiful.”

“Yeah, you look great,” Sokka enthused. “Give us a twirl?”

Zuko obliged. The ceremonial robes Piandao had commissioned for him all those months ago were in their full glory, in anticipation of his twenty-sixth birthday celebrations tomorrow. In shades of red and gold, the batik panels were painstakingly dyed and embroidered to depict fire lilies and volcanic islands and the symbols of all four nations along the hems. The outer garment was sleeveless and open, the shoulders creased and starched to replicate the sharp shape of his old pauldron. Then there was the pièce de résistance, the portrait of Druk splayed in full glory over the back. The light fabric swirled around his ankles as he turned to let Sokka and Piandao take in the robes from every angle.

“You look incredible,” said Sokka.

“Worth every tael,” said Piandao, eyes sparkling with humour.

Zuko flushed. He let the seamstresses usher him back before the mirrors. The remaining alterations were minor; since the last fitting, Zuko had—gratifyingly—put on some bulk. Osha took credit for that: *Look at the world of good our sparring sessions have done you!*

“Hey Azula,” said Sokka, looping behind her chair.

She had paused in her snacking, eyeing the visitors warily. “Hi.”

“Sifu,” said Zuko as Osha set about fetching extra teacups, “have you met my sister, Sigu Azula?”

“I don’t believe so. Good afternoon, Sigu.”

Azula’s eyes darted to Zuko’s. He nodded back. “Hello,” she said.

“This is Master Piandao, I’ve told you about him,” said Zuko. “Sifu, Azula helped redesign the new temple complex at Crescent Island.”

“Indeed?” said Piandao. “How fascinating. Tell me more.”

Azula’s eyes found Zuko’s again. “What do you want to know?”

“Well,” said Piandao, taking a meditative step in her direction, “I do have a passing interest in early dynastic architecture. Did you have any major inspirations?”

“Oh,” said Azula. She plucked another rambulongan from the bowl, twisting the spiny skin between her fingers. “I—I mean. I was reading through some of the annals and found this description that completely *diverged* from that old building they had…”

“With no consideration of the history of the site?” supplied Piandao

“Yes. That, exactly.”

Piandao pulled up a chair beside her. She hesitated, then nudged her bowl towards him. “Rambulongan?”

Sokka drifted over to Zuko, smiling. “Look at those two nerds go,” he said in an undertone.

Zuko let his eyes linger over him. The quivering energy of his body, his bronzed skin, the blue of his eyes that Zuko saw in the sky and seas of the hot-dry. How familiar this all was, how dear. “You’re here early,” he remarked. “I thought you’d come at night, or tomorrow.”

“I had something on,” said Sokka. In the mirror, Zuko saw the coyness cross his face. “I—it’s a bit of a surprise, actually.”

“Tell me.”

Sokka twisted his hands together, met Zuko’s eye in the reflection. “I had a job interview. Here. In Caldera.”

“An interview!” said Zuko. “You didn’t mention.”

“It’s a surprise, as I said.” Sokka cocked his hip against the frame of the mirror. “I didn’t really expect anything. I sent the models and experiments of my geothermal engine to a few places and— yeah. I heard back.”

It was a surprise. “Good luck,” said Zuko earnestly. “I hope they hire you. You’re the best. They’d be crazy not to.”

Sokka ducked his head. “It’s because of your urban planning initiatives they’re hiring, anyway. I was seeing this transport company, they were thinking of adapting the engine for a cable car, you know, like at Boiling Rock…”

“What about Piandao?” said Zuko. “What about the—*you know*?”

“He encouraged it, actually. Wrote my references. He says it’s good to have *personnel* across different sectors. So yeah.” He was smiling, a little soft flush creeping up his neck. “I’m relocating. Here.”

Zuko’s heart thudded against the lotus-flax. “You’re coming to live here. In the capital. With me.”

“That’s the plan.”

Seamstresses be damned. Zuko flung himself onto Sokka, a whirl of burgundy fabric. He pressed his face hard against Sokka’s cheek. “I really want to kiss you right now,” he murmured.

“Why don’t you?”

So Zuko did. A hand cupping his nape, scuffing against the shorn hair there, Sokka bending him backwards with reciprocal enthusiasm. “Get a *room*!” came Azula’s dismayed cry, and they broke apart laughing.

“That’s why,” said Zuko as he let the seamstresses reel him back.

“Your Majesty,” said one of them, a little put upon, “you may step briefly out of the robes while we put on the finishing touches.”

Apologetic, Zuko climbed out of the robes so they could pin them to a dress form. Sokka ushered him away from the mirror, pressed a cup of tea into his hands.

“So I take it that’s a royal endorsement of my move?” he said.

“*Yes*,” said Zuko. “I’ll stamp it, if you want. Make it official.”

Sokka grinned at him over his own teacup. “I figure I ought to save you the air fares.”

“Considerate.”

“I mean, no one’s offered me a job yet,” Sokka said. “But even if they all reject me, I’ll keep applying. I don’t—” He gripped Zuko’s hands. “I don’t want to be away from you as long as we were, if I can help it.”

“You don’t have to get a job just to come and live with me,” said Zuko, even as he squeezed back.

Sokka laughed, then a wistful expression crossed his face. “It’s not— Truth be told, I don’t know what to expect.”

“What about?”

He sighed, pulling his hands back towards himself. “It’s a leap of faith, I guess. When I started that volcanic engine project, it was just a bit of fun. Then I realised I…love this. I love creating things. I want to keep doing it, doing it *seriously*, even after all of…what happened. But I don’t know if…”

“It’s not the same place,” Zuko said at once. He put his arm around Sokka, stroking his side. “It’ll be different. They’ll *welcome* your brilliance. You’re reclaiming something you love for yourself. And if they dare bully the Fire Lord’s partner…”

Sokka huffed. “Yeah, yeah, you’ll rain down your righteous fiery vengeance upon them.”

“Exactly,” said Zuko, steely.

Sokka rolled his eyes, but his twitching mouth betrayed his fondness. “Alright,” he murmured, casting Zuko a look from the side. “Hey, and how was your meeting with that healer?”

“Oh! That.” The healer of the mind whom Dr Khoo had recommended. He had mentioned the upcoming appointment in his last letter to Sokka. “Yeah, I think it’s promising. Just the first meeting, she asked me about my family and how we interacted growing up. Seems…nice. We’ll see.”

“I’m glad you’re getting all that out.”

Zuko smiled thinly. “Yeah. Matter of time, I guess. That’s what she told me, at least.”

“You’re taking the right steps, sunshine.”

A clearing of the throat. “Your Majesty? May we interrupt?”

Zuko looked up. It was the seamstresses, flanking the robes laid out over the dress form. “It’s finished. Please, observe.”

They helped Zuko back into them, draping them onto him layer by layer.

“You’ll be wearing these for the celebrations tomorrow?” said Piandao.

“Yes,” said Zuko. “I’ll be opening the Archive to the public.”

“The Archive?”

“We kept all the confiscated materials from the war there,” said Zuko. “I dunno. Councillor Po floated the idea before. I never thought I’d let anyone in, I didn’t know whether they’d feel remorse looking at those objects, but *now*…” He smoothed a hand down over the glossy red fabric. “Now, I think we’re finally ready to confront that past.”

“Well, I think I have the perfect thing to go with that opening,” said Sokka.

He dug through his bag and offered Zuko a huge, thick scroll of paper. His hands vibrated with excitement. “Your present!”

“What’s this?”

“Osha, please,” said Sokka, beckoning. He handed her one end.

“Give it here,” said Azula, sticking out a hand for the other end.

“Not with all that juice!”

She wiped her hand on her tunic, rolling her eyes. “There, happy?”

Azula anchored, Osha unfurled. The characters stood stark against the crisp paper, mounted on a shining gold backing: 龍德年華. *The Age of the Dragon’s Virtue*.

“You wrote this?” said Zuko.

“Of course I did. My seal’s on it.” Sokka pointed. There it was indeed, to the side, the red stamp of his name with the little boomerangs around the character for water. Beside it was a small, smitten subscript: *For my beloved Zuko*. “I was thinking you could display it over a doorway. The Archive’s? It’s probably good enough for that.”

“It is,” Piandao supplied.

“There you have it. From the master himself. It’s about—well, what you’ve achieved.”

Zuko hugged his arms to his chest, as though to contain the swelling of his heart. He let his eyes trace the sweeping trails of ink. There was a nonchalance to the calligraphy, and he knew Sokka well enough to know that he would’ve ruminated over the curve of each stroke before committing it to paper. “About—me?”

“Well, I didn’t write it for Kuei.”

Zuko pressed his knuckles to his mouth. Sokka was looking at him with a face that told Zuko he knew everything he was feeling; that without a word, he could still feel the echo of Zuko’s emotions thrumming against his heartstrings. “This is— I— I don’t know what to say. It’s beautiful.”

“One final thing to complete the look,” said Sokka. “Osha, do you have his hairpiece somewhere?”

“Right here,” said Osha, reaching for the ornament where it had been resting on a stand. Sokka took it from her.

“Put it in for me,” said Zuko, and he did.

“Resplendent,” said Piandao. “Every bit the Fire Lord you are.”

“It suits you!” cried Osha.

Even Azula nodded with rueful approval.

Sokka turned Zuko back to face the mirror. Looking back at him was a young Fire Lord on the cusp of twenty-six. Some things were the same: the scar, the sheets of black hair, the pointed chin. But his robes told the story of his love for his nation, for the world, for the lifegiving fire that burned in his core. A man who had inherited a brutal legacy but by the spirits, was he trying to shed it. He would give his life for this, and he would give it happily: for the first green shoots that unfurl from the scorched earth.

“What do you think?” said Sokka, looping his arm around Zuko’s waist. “Handsome?”

Zuko grinned back at the mirror, smiling so widely their reflections became blurs of red and gold. “This is it.”

“What is?”

It was everything, it was the whole tableau. Behind him, Piandao was pointing out to Osha the flourishes of Sokka’s calligraphy while she pursed her lips in appreciation. Azula tossed Druk a rambulongan with her free hand. When he caught it in his mouth, zapped the fruit with fire, the force of her smile split her face open. And Sokka, his love, steadfast by his side. Zuko gripped his hand, threaded their fingers together. “Yeah,” he said. “This is how it’s meant to be.”

完

### End Notes

#### Work End Notes

Here we are. I can't believe it. It's so bittersweet to be here at the end, it feels like the end of an era. Thank you so, so much to everyone who's been reading this and cheering me on. Your feedback means everything. It's a dialogue.

There are two major inspirations for this fic I want to highlight here. First is Radiohead's "Weird Fishes/Arpeggi". The line, "I hit the bottom / and escape", pointed me back on the right track many a time while writing. The second is the concept of *epistemicide*, or the colonial murder of knowledge, coined by Bonaventura de Sousa Santos.

If this fic touched you in any way, please leave me a comment &#38; kudos, rec to a friend, reblog on tumblr, anything! A work like this is a labour of love. It's been part of my life for half a year and it's been a privilege for me to share it. If it moved you at all, I want to hear it ♡

Find me on tumblr, please come and say hi!
